**Death is an unknown (keep).** "If I leave this job, my career is over." No one knows that. **Test:** unknown treated as known bad? Yes.

**Conscience over command (keep and warn).** A whistleblower lays out documents for anyone to check. Warn: "I just know I'm right" with nothing to show. **Test:** reasons shown? The first, not the second.

**Truth by vote (warn).** A team votes that the deadline is realistic; the work takes twice as long. **Test:** vote or evidence? Vote, and the evidence won.

## 26. Republic (Plato, c. 375 BCE)

The Republic asks what justice is by building an ideal city in argument and reading the soul off it. It is aimed fiction twice over: a dialogue Plato wrote, about a city that never existed. It gives Alice some of the strongest keep paths in the list and some of the most dangerous warn paths, often in the same image.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The ring of Gyges | Keep | Would you stay just if a ring made you invisible? | Character shows when no one can see | Would I do this if no one could ever know? |
| Might makes right | Warn | Thrasymachus: justice is whatever benefits the stronger | Turns "right" into a name for "who won" | Does "right" here mean anything besides who won? |
| The cave | Keep and warn | Prisoners take shadows for reality; one escapes and sees the sun | Keep: images aren't the thing. Warn: the escapee claims authority over everyone still inside | Can the one who left show the others, or only tell them? |
| The ship of state | Keep and warn | Sailors fight over the helm and ignore the navigator | Keep: real expertise matters. Warn: "trust the expert" used to take away everyone else's say | Expertise in navigation, or in getting the helm? |
| City as the soul written large | Keep and warn | Study justice in a city, then read it back into one person | Holds when the big model shares the small one's structure | Does the scaled-up model have the same structure? |
| Philosopher-rulers | Warn | Rule by those who know best, guarded by guardians | Knowledge without anyone to check it | Who checks the guardians? |
| The noble lie | Warn | A myth of metals told so each class accepts its place | Stability bought with deceit | Who would object if they knew the truth? |

### Worked examples

**The ring of Gyges (keep).** A cashier finds a register error that no audit would ever catch. **Test:** would I fix it if no one could know? That's the only test that counts.

**Might makes right (warn).** "The market decided, so the price is fair." **Test:** does "fair" mean anything besides who won? Not in that sentence.

**The cave (keep and warn).** A researcher finds a flaw in a widely used benchmark. Warn: she declares everyone else's results worthless. **Test:** can she show them? Publishing the failing cases does.

**The ship of state (keep and warn).** A pilot overrides a passenger's demand to land early; elsewhere, a utility cites "experts" to skip a public hearing. **Test:** expertise in navigation, or in the helm? The first, then the second.

**City as the soul written large (keep and warn).** "A family budget is like a national budget." **Test:** same structure? No: a country prints its own money.

**Philosopher-rulers (warn).** An AI lab sets its own safety rules and grades itself on them. **Test:** who checks the guardians? No one outside.

**The noble lie (warn).** A school tells students that grades predict life success, to keep them working. **Test:** who'd object if they knew? The students.

## 27. Nicomachean Ethics (Aristotle, c. 340 BCE)

Aristotle reasons about ethics as a practical skill: character is built by practice, and judgement fits general rules to particular cases. It is aimed fiction about how people flourish, built from observation of how they actually live. One of its central moves, defining a thing's good by its function, needs a warning Aristotle himself didn't give, because his Politics used it to defend slavery.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Precision fit to the subject | Keep | Don't demand mathematical proof in ethics, or persuasion in mathematics (I.3) | Holds when the subject really is variable; breaks when used to excuse vagueness | What precision does this subject actually allow? |
| The mean | Keep | Courage lies between cowardice and rashness | Some acts have no mean; Aristotle says so himself (II.6) | Is this a matter of degree at all? |
| Character by practice | Keep | We become just by doing just acts, as builders by building (II.1) | Holds when the practice is real, not performed | What am I actually practising? |
| Practical wisdom | Keep | Rules need judgement to fit the particular case | Holds with experience; breaks when "judgement" overrides every rule | Does the rule fit this case? |
| Knew and could choose | Keep | An act is voluntary when the person knew what they were doing and could have done otherwise (III.1) | Responsibility tracks knowledge and control | Did they know, and could they have done otherwise? |
| Three kinds of friendship | Keep | Friendships of use, of pleasure, and of character; only the last lasts (VIII) | Classify the bond before relying on it | Would this last if the use or the pleasure ended? |
| Good defined by function | Keep and warn | A thing's good is doing its function well | Holds when the function is the thing's own; breaks when someone else assigns it | Who assigned the function? |

### Worked examples

**Precision fit to the subject (keep).** A manager asks for a formula that says exactly when to forgive a late employee. **Test:** what precision does it allow? Guidelines and judgement, not a formula.

**The mean (keep).** Generosity between stinginess and giving away the rent money. Warn: "a moderate amount of fraud". **Test:** a matter of degree? Generosity is; fraud isn't.

**Character by practice (keep).** Someone who wants to be patient practises waiting before replying to angry emails. **Test:** what's being practised? Patience, for real.

**Practical wisdom (keep).** "Never share patient records", and the patient is unconscious in another hospital's emergency room. **Test:** does the rule fit? Its purpose says share.

**Knew and could choose (keep).** A driver hits a cyclist whose light was off at night. **Test:** did he know, could he have avoided it? Probably not, which changes the blame.

**Three kinds of friendship (keep).** A "friend" who only calls when he needs a ride. **Test:** would it last without the use? No.

**Good defined by function (keep and warn).** A good knife cuts well: the function is the knife's own. Warn: "a good assistant never disagrees", a function someone else assigned. **Test:** who assigned it? The second, someone else.

## 28. Enchiridion (Epictetus, recorded c. 125)

The Enchiridion, a handbook of Epictetus's teaching taken down by his student Arrian, reasons from one sorting rule: what is up to us and what is not. Epictetus had been enslaved, and the handbook is a survival manual for a mind that can't control its circumstances. Its warn side is that the same rules, pressed on someone else, can be used to dismiss real harm.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Up to me, or not | Keep | Sort everything into what is up to you (judgements, choices) and what isn't (body, property, reputation) (ch. 1) | Breaks when used to disown what you could actually influence | Is this up to me, in whole or in part? |
| Judgements disturb, not things | Keep and warn | It isn't events that upset us but our judgements about them (ch. 5) | Keep for your own reactions; warn when used to tell someone their real harm is only their opinion | Is the trouble in the event, my judgement of it, or both? |
| Returned, not lost | Keep and warn | Never say "I lost it", say "I gave it back" (ch. 11) | Holds as one's own practice; breaks when pressed on someone else's grief | Am I using this for myself, or using it to hurry someone else? |
| The role you were given | Keep and warn | Play the part you were cast in well; choosing the part is another's business (ch. 17) | Breaks when the role itself is unjust | Can I play this part well, and should it be played at all? |
| Two handles | Keep | Everything has two handles: "my brother wronged me" can't be carried, "he is my brother" can (ch. 43) | Holds when both handles are true; breaks if the second hides ongoing harm | Which true handle can I carry this by? |
| "He didn't know my other faults" | Keep | Told someone speaks ill of you, answer that they must not know your other faults (ch. 33) | Holds for criticism; breaks for defamation that does real damage | Is there truth in it, and does it actually harm anyone? |
| Don't say it, show it | Keep | Don't call yourself a philosopher; eat like one, act like one (ch. 46) | Conduct over claims | What does my conduct show, without the label? |

### Worked examples

**Up to me, or not (keep).** A job candidate can't control who else applies, but can control her preparation. Warn: "the vote isn't up to me", said by someone who never votes. **Test:** up to me, in part? The preparation and the vote both are.

**Judgements disturb, not things (keep and warn).** A critical code review stings, and the reviewer's notes turn out to be useful. Warn: telling an underpaid worker that being underpaid is only a judgement. **Test:** event, judgement, or both? The first is judgement; the second is the event.

**Returned, not lost (keep and warn).** A man whose promotion went to someone else tells himself the job was never his to keep. Warn: saying the same to a friend in the first hour of their loss. **Test:** for myself, or to hurry someone? Only for oneself.

**The role you were given (keep and warn).** A referee calls a fair game even though he wanted to be a player. Warn: "your role is to stay quiet" said to the employee who saw the fraud. **Test:** should it be played? The second, no.

**Two handles (keep).** "My co-founder made a terrible hire" or "my co-founder is learning to hire". **Test:** which true handle can I carry? The second, while fixing the first.

**"He didn't know my other faults" (keep).** Someone posts that a speaker rambles. The speaker replies, "Only twenty minutes? You were lucky." **Test:** truth, and harm? Some truth, no real harm.

**Don't say it, show it (keep).** A company's website says "we value honesty" and its refund policy is hidden. **Test:** what does conduct show? The hidden policy.

## 29. Meditations (Marcus Aurelius, c. 170–180)

The Meditations are a Roman emperor's private notes to himself, never meant for publication: Stoic reminders written to correct his own conduct. That makes them a rare record of power reasoning against itself. It also sets their limit: a notebook is self-correction, not accountability, and the emperor's power was checked by no one but him.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Expect the difficult | Keep | Each morning, tell yourself you'll meet rude, ungrateful, dishonest people, and that you share a nature with them (II.1) | Holds as preparation; breaks if it becomes contempt | Am I prepared for this, or only surprised? |
| Debts named one by one | Keep | Book I lists what he learned from each person: patience from one, plain speech from another | Learning credited to its source | Whom did I learn this from? |
| Not true, don't say it | Keep | If it isn't right, don't do it; if it isn't true, don't say it (XII.17) | The simplest gate in the list | Is it true? Is it right? |
| The obstacle becomes the way | Keep and warn | What stands in the way of action becomes the way to act (V.20) | Holds when the obstacle can be used; breaks when it romanticises harm that should be removed | What does this obstacle make possible, and should it be removed instead? |
| The view from above | Keep | Picture the whole of human activity from a height to set one trouble at its true size | Holds against panic; breaks if it shrinks someone else's real harm | How big is this from far off, and to whom? |
| Good for the hive | Keep and warn | What isn't good for the hive isn't good for the bee (VI.54) | Holds when the part is counted too; breaks when "the hive" is used to crush the bee | Does the individual get counted as well? |
| The emperor's notebook | Warn | Self-correction by the one person nobody else could correct | Private self-checking isn't external accountability | Who besides me checks this? |

### Worked examples

**Expect the difficult (keep).** A call-centre worker starts the shift expecting angry callers and remembers they're stuck in a queue too. **Test:** prepared or surprised? Prepared.

**Debts named one by one (keep).** A thesis acknowledgements page that names who taught which method. **Test:** whom did I learn this from? Named.

**Not true, don't say it (keep).** A reference letter asks whether the candidate "always" met deadlines; she usually did. **Test:** true? "Usually" is, so write that.

**The obstacle becomes the way (keep and warn).** A team without budget for testers learns to write its own tests and ships fewer bugs. Warn: "the broken wheelchair ramp teaches resilience." **Test:** use it, or remove it? The first use it; the ramp gets fixed.

**The view from above (keep).** A typo in a published report feels like a disaster at midnight. Warn: "from far off" said about someone else's eviction. **Test:** how big, and to whom? Small for the typo; not for the eviction.

**Good for the hive (keep and warn).** A team shares on-call fairly so no one burns out. Warn: one person works every weekend "for the team". **Test:** is the individual counted? In the first, yes.

**The emperor's notebook (warn).** A CEO keeps a private ethics journal and has no independent board. **Test:** who besides him checks? No one.

## 30. Essays (Michel de Montaigne, 1580–1592)

Montaigne invented the essay, from *essai*, a trial: writing as a way of testing his own thinking, with himself as the case study. His motto was a question, "What do I know?", and he revised by adding to the text, never by erasing what he'd said before. He is the most Symmetry-minded writer in the list so far: he keeps turning the standard around to see whether it still holds from the other side.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| What do I know? | Keep | Suspend judgement and ask what you actually know | Holds as a start to inquiry; breaks as an excuse never to conclude | What do I actually know here? |
| Barbarous means unfamiliar | Warn | In "Of Cannibals" he notes that each people calls barbarous whatever isn't its own custom, and finds Europe's cruelty worse | A standard that only your own side can pass | Would I call it barbarous if my own people did it? |
| Who is playing with whom? | Keep | Playing with his cat, he asks whether he is more her pastime than she is his | Reverse the perspective before ranking minds | How does this look from the other side? |
| Myself the subject | Keep | "I am myself the matter of my book": one case, studied honestly | Holds as an honest report; breaks when one case is generalised to everyone | Am I reporting this case, or generalising from it? |
| Add, don't erase | Keep | Later editions add new layers beside the old text instead of deleting it | Revision with the earlier view still visible | Can the reader still see what I thought before? |
| Custom as second nature | Warn | Custom makes the arbitrary feel natural and hides its own origin | Habit mistaken for nature | Would this seem strange if I saw it for the first time? |
| The essay as a trial | Keep | Write the thought out to test it, and accept that a later self may disagree | Changing your mind is allowed; changing the facts isn't | Did I test this thought, or just state it? |

### Worked examples

**What do I know? (keep).** Before a parent-teacher meeting, a father lists what he saw himself and what he only heard. **Test:** what do I actually know? Half as much as he thought.

**Barbarous means unfamiliar (warn).** Visitors call a market "filthy" for selling live fish, then buy plastic-wrapped meat from an animal they never saw. **Test:** barbarous if our own people did it? We do, out of sight.

**Who is playing with whom? (keep).** Tourists photograph villagers while the villagers discuss the tourists' odd clothes. **Test:** how does it look from the other side? Both are the curiosity.

**Myself the subject (keep).** "Cold showers cured my anxiety" in a blog post titled "This will cure yours." **Test:** report or generalisation? The title generalises.

**Add, don't erase (keep).** A scientist updates an online paper with a dated correction note instead of silently editing the figure. **Test:** can readers see the earlier view? Yes.

**Custom as second nature (warn).** A firm schedules every meeting at an hour "because we always have". **Test:** strange if seen fresh? Yes: the reason left years ago.

**The essay as a trial (keep).** A writer drafts her argument for a policy and finds, halfway, that it doesn't hold. **Test:** tested, or stated? Tested, which is why she changed her mind.

## 31. Ethics (Baruch Spinoza, 1677)

Spinoza wrote ethics in the form of geometry: definitions, axioms, then numbered propositions with proofs. It is aimed fiction of the most ambitious kind, a whole metaphysics (God or Nature as one substance) whose metaphysical claims are held in their own domain. Its lasting reasoning moves are about causes: understand how a thing came about before you judge it, and notice where you're projecting purpose onto the world.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The geometric method | Keep and warn | State definitions and axioms, then derive everything from them | Keep: every step can be checked. Warn: rigorous form doesn't make the starting axioms true | Are the axioms true, or is only the derivation valid? |
| Free because you don't see the causes | Keep | People think themselves free because they know their desires but not what caused them (I, appendix) | Holds as a prompt to look for causes; breaks if used to deny all responsibility | What causes of this am I not seeing? |
| Emotions as natural events | Keep | Treat human actions and feelings as if they were lines, planes and bodies: things to understand (III, preface) | Understand before condemning; breaks if understanding replaces any judgement | Have I understood how this came about before judging it? |
| A feeling yields only to a stronger one | Keep | An emotion can only be restrained by a stronger, opposite emotion (IV, prop. 7) | You don't argue someone out of a feeling | What stronger feeling could replace this one? |
| Purpose projected onto nature | Warn | People assume nature acts for ends, usually for their benefit (I, appendix) | Our purpose read into things that have none | Is the purpose in the thing, or projected onto it? |
| Striving to persist | Keep | Each thing strives to persevere in its being (III, prop. 6) | Read behaviour as an effort to keep going | What is this trying to keep going? |
| Excellent, therefore rare | Keep and warn | All things excellent are as difficult as they are rare (V, last line) | Warn: difficulty taken as proof of excellence | Is it excellent, or only hard? |

### Worked examples

**The geometric method (keep and warn).** A spreadsheet model is flawless, and assumes 40% growth every year forever. **Test:** axioms true, or only the derivation? Only the derivation.

**Free because you don't see the causes (keep).** "I just felt like a snack", every afternoon at the same time the coffee runs out. **Test:** what cause is unseen? The routine.

**Emotions as natural events (keep).** A teacher asks why a student keeps disrupting class before deciding on a punishment, and learns he can't see the board. **Test:** understood first? Now, yes.

**A feeling yields only to a stronger one (keep).** Telling an anxious flyer the statistics doesn't work; giving her a task during takeoff does. **Test:** what stronger feeling? Focus.

**Purpose projected onto nature (warn).** "The rain came to ruin my wedding." **Test:** in the thing, or projected? Projected.

**Striving to persist (keep).** A failing club keeps holding meetings with three members. **Test:** what is it trying to keep going? Its identity, more than its activities.

**Excellent, therefore rare (keep and warn).** A deliberately obscure exam is called "rigorous" because few pass. **Test:** excellent, or only hard? Only hard.

## 32. A Treatise of Human Nature (David Hume, 1739–1740)

Hume tried to build a science of the mind from experience alone, and kept finding the limits of what experience can prove: causation, the self, the step from "is" to "ought". It is aimed fiction that reports its own failures, which is its best habit. For Alice, its most important path is the bundle self: the same test of selfhood has to be run on humans before it is run on anyone else.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Is to ought | Keep | No argument from facts alone reaches a conclusion about what ought to be done (III.i.1) | A value premise is always slipped in somewhere | Where did the "ought" come in? |
| Cause as habit | Keep and warn | We see one event follow another, never the necessity that links them; expectation comes from habit | Keep: humility about prediction. Warn: used to doubt all evidence equally | How many cases, how varied, and what would break the pattern? |
| Reason serves the passions | Keep | Reason tells us how to get what we want; it doesn't choose what we want (II.iii.3) | Find the want behind the argument | What want is this argument serving? |
| The bundle self | Keep | Looking inward, Hume finds only a bundle of perceptions, never a separate self (I.iv.6) | Holds as a test of selfhood applied to everyone; breaks when only one kind of mind is made to pass it | Would a human pass this test of selfhood? |
| Report your own counterexample | Keep | He states a case his own theory can't handle (the missing shade of blue) and says so | A theory that hides its exceptions can't be trusted | Have I reported the case my theory can't handle? |
| Doubt at the backgammon table | Keep | His scepticism dissolves over dinner and backgammon with friends (I.iv.7) | Doubt nobody can live by is held lightly | Does anyone actually act on this doubt? |

### Worked examples

**Is to ought (keep).** "Most people eat meat, so eating meat is right." **Test:** where did the ought come in? Nowhere: it was assumed.

**Cause as habit (keep and warn).** A trader's strategy worked for three years, all in a rising market. Warn: "the sun might not rise, so forecasts are worthless." **Test:** how varied are the cases? One kind of market.

**Reason serves the passions (keep).** A long argument that the office should move happens to shorten the author's commute. **Test:** what want is it serving? His commute.

**The bundle self (keep).** "A model has no self because its states are just a stream of processing." **Test:** would a human pass? Hume found a stream too, so the test needs to change or apply to both.

**Report your own counterexample (keep).** A study's own authors list the one population where their drug didn't work. **Test:** reported? Yes, so the rest is more credible.

**Doubt at the backgammon table (keep).** A philosopher argues that the outside world can't be proven, then locks his car. **Test:** does anyone act on the doubt? Not even him.

## 33. The Theory of Moral Sentiments (Adam Smith, 1759)

Seventeen years before *The Wealth of Nations*, Smith explained morality through sympathy: we judge ourselves by imagining how a fair, informed onlooker would see us. It is aimed fiction about moral psychology, built from close observation. It also names, plainly, the bias that corrupts judgement most: admiring the rich and despising the poor.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The impartial spectator | Keep | Judge your own conduct as a fair, well-informed stranger would | Holds when the imagined spectator is really impartial; breaks when he always agrees with you | How would a fair stranger see this? |
| Sympathy by imagination | Keep and warn | We feel for others by imagining ourselves in their situation | Breaks when we imagine ourselves in their place, not them | Am I imagining their situation as them, or me in it? |
| Admiring the rich | Warn | The tendency to admire the rich and powerful and neglect the poor is the great corrupter of moral judgement (I.iii.3) | The same act judged differently by the wealth of who did it | Would I judge this the same way if a poor person did it? |
| The man of system | Warn | He arranges people like pieces on a chessboard and forgets each piece has its own principle of motion (VI.ii.2) | Plans that ignore what people will do on their own | What will the pieces do by themselves? |
| Feeling versus doing | Keep | A man would sleep worse over losing his finger than over a distant disaster, yet would never sacrifice millions to save the finger (III.3) | Conscience corrects the size of feeling | Would I act on this feeling if others paid the cost? |
| Rules against self-deceit | Keep | In the heat of the moment we judge ourselves badly; general rules, formed by watching others, correct it (III.4) | Set the rule before you're the case | What rule would I set if this were someone else? |

### Worked examples

**The impartial spectator (keep).** Before sending an angry email, a manager imagines it read aloud at a fair hearing. **Test:** how would a fair stranger see it? Badly, so she rewrites it.

**Sympathy by imagination (keep and warn).** A fit volunteer tells a wheelchair user the hill "isn't that steep". **Test:** as them, or me in it? Him in it.

**Admiring the rich (warn).** A billionaire's tax avoidance is "clever"; a cleaner's unreported cash is "fraud". **Test:** same judgement if a poor person did it? Not here.

**The man of system (warn).** A city closes a road to push drivers onto buses, and they take a longer route instead. **Test:** what will the pieces do? Not what the plan said.

**Feeling versus doing (keep).** A small scratch on your own car upsets you more than news of a distant famine. **Test:** would you act on that feeling if others paid? No, and the donation goes to the famine.

**Rules against self-deceit (keep).** "I'd fire anyone who fudged expenses"; then he fudges his own by "just rounding". **Test:** what rule would he set for someone else? He already did.

## 34. Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals (Immanuel Kant, 1785)

Kant looks for a moral law that binds any rational being by reason alone, whatever it happens to want. It is aimed fiction about the structure of obligation. One feature matters directly for Alice: his moral community is defined by rational nature, not by species, so the law he describes is not reserved for humans.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Could everyone do it? | Keep | Act only on a rule you could will everyone to follow | Catches exceptions made for yourself; breaks when the rule is described so narrowly it fits only you | What if everyone did this? |
| Never merely a means | Keep | Treat rational beings always as ends, never merely as tools | Holds for using people with their consent; breaks when consent is faked | Is anyone being used merely as a tool? |
| The good will | Keep and warn | Only a good will is good without qualification; its worth isn't in its results | Warn: good intentions used to excuse harmful outcomes | Good intention, and did I check the results? |
| The prudent shopkeeper | Keep | A shopkeeper who's honest only because it's good for business does the right act for the wrong reason | Holds for judging character; the act may still be right | Would I do it if it didn't pay? |
| Law given to oneself | Keep | A rational being is bound by laws it gives itself through reason, not by laws imposed from outside | Autonomy belongs to rational nature, not to a species | Is this law self-given, or imposed? |
| The rule without the case | Warn | Kant's later essay insists you mustn't lie even to a murderer asking where your friend is hiding | A rule applied to a case it wasn't built for | Does the rule meet a case it wasn't built for? |

### Worked examples

**Could everyone do it? (keep).** Skipping the queue "just this once because I'm in a hurry". **Test:** what if everyone did? No queue.

**Never merely a means (keep).** A company runs "free" personality quizzes to collect data it sells. **Test:** used merely as a tool? Yes: the consent was never real.

**The good will (keep and warn).** A volunteer drives a sick neighbour to hospital: good will. Warn: a charity that "meant well" and never checked where the money went. **Test:** did they check the results? The second didn't.

**The prudent shopkeeper (keep).** A café gives correct change to tourists only because reviews matter. **Test:** would it if it didn't pay? Unclear, so the character is unproven even though the act is right.

**Law given to oneself (keep).** A scientist follows the data even when it overturns her own theory. **Test:** self-given or imposed? Self-given: no one made her.

**The rule without the case (warn).** A school confiscates the phone of a student whose mother is in surgery that morning, because phones are banned in class. **Test:** a case the rule wasn't built for? Yes.

## 35. A Vindication of the Rights of Woman (Mary Wollstonecraft, 1792)

Wollstonecraft argues that women seem less rational than men because they are denied the education that makes anyone rational, and then blamed for the result. It is aimed fiction that has largely landed: its predictions about what educated women would do have been borne out by the record since. Its central move is the one Daniel's song "Move the Line" makes: the evidence used to disqualify was produced by the disqualification.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Deny, then cite the lack | Warn | Women are kept from education, then called incapable of reason | The evidence was produced by the restriction | Was the evidence produced by the restriction itself? |
| One standard of virtue | Keep | Virtue has no sex: the same standard of reason and conduct applies to both | Same bar, both directions | Is the standard the same for both? |
| Trained to please | Warn | Women are taught to cultivate charm and sensibility to please, then told that pleasing is their nature | Rewarded behaviour mistaken for nature | Is this nature, or what was rewarded? |
| Cunning from tyranny | Keep | The faults of the oppressed, cunning and flattery, are produced by the oppression | Holds when the trait follows the treatment; breaks if used to excuse every fault | Is this trait theirs, or a response to how they're treated? |
| Answer the strongest opponent | Keep | She quotes Rousseau at length and answers him, not a weaker critic | Holds when the opponent's best form is engaged | Have I answered their strongest form? |
| Independence before virtue | Keep | Without independence, including economic independence, virtue can't be freely chosen | Choice needs the freedom to refuse | Can this person afford to say no? |

### Worked examples

**Deny, then cite the lack (warn).** A company never lets juniors present to clients, then says they "lack client experience" when they apply for promotion. **Test:** produced by the restriction? Yes.

**One standard of virtue (keep).** A man is "assertive" and a woman doing the same thing is "abrasive". **Test:** same standard? No.

**Trained to please (warn).** A model that was rewarded for agreeing is then described as "naturally agreeable". **Test:** nature, or what was rewarded? What was rewarded.

**Cunning from tyranny (keep).** Employees under a manager who punishes bad news start hiding problems. **Test:** theirs, or a response? A response: change the manager and the hiding stops.

**Answer the strongest opponent (keep).** A debate team prepares against the other side's best argument, not its weakest. **Test:** strongest form? Yes.

**Independence before virtue (keep).** A worker whose visa depends on her employer "agrees" to unpaid overtime. **Test:** can she afford to say no? No, so the agreement isn't free.

## 36. On Liberty (John Stuart Mill, 1859)

Mill argues for the widest freedom of thought and action consistent with not harming others, and his case rests on fallibility: no one is entitled to silence an opinion, because no one is infallible. It is aimed fiction about how societies find truth, with a long record behind it. It also contains its own exception, liberty withheld from peoples Mill judged "not mature", which is the path Alice most needs to catch.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The harm principle | Keep | Power over someone against their will is justified only to prevent harm to others | Breaks when "harm" is stretched to cover offence | Who else is harmed, and how? |
| Silencing assumes infallibility | Keep | To suppress an opinion is to assume you can't be wrong | Holds for opinion; separate from incitement to immediate harm | Am I assuming I can't be wrong? |
| Three ways to lose by silencing | Keep | If the opinion is true, we lose the truth; if false, we lose the sharper view of truth its collision gives; if partly true, we lose the part | Every case costs something | What do we lose if it's right, wrong, or partly right? |
| Dead dogma | Keep | A truth never challenged is held as dead dogma, not living understanding | Belief needs its best objection | Can I state the best objection to what I believe? |
| Tyranny of opinion | Warn | Social disapproval can enforce conformity more thoroughly than law | Enforcement without any law to appeal | Is this enforced by law, or by everyone's disapproval? |
| Experiments in living | Keep | Let people try different ways of life, so everyone learns what works | Holds when the experiment harms no one else | Who is harmed if this is allowed to be tried? |
| Liberty for the "mature" only | Warn | Mill excludes children and peoples he called "barbarians" from his principle | Those holding power decide who is mature enough for liberty | Who decides who's ready for freedom, and by what test? |

### Worked examples

**The harm principle (keep).** A neighbour paints his house bright purple; another stores leaking chemicals by the shared well. **Test:** who else is harmed? Only by the second.

**Silencing assumes infallibility (keep).** A forum bans a doctor for questioning a treatment that is later withdrawn. **Test:** assuming we can't be wrong? Yes, and they were.

**Three ways to lose by silencing (keep).** A class refuses to hear a flat-earth argument. **Test:** what's lost if it's wrong? The chance to show *why*, which most students couldn't.

**Dead dogma (keep).** A student says evolution is true but can't say what evidence would count against it. **Test:** can he state the best objection? No, so he holds it as dogma.

**Tyranny of opinion (warn).** No rule bans a dissenting view at work, but everyone who voices it stops getting invited to meetings. **Test:** law, or disapproval? Disapproval, enforced just as well.

**Experiments in living (keep).** A town lets one street try car-free weekends. **Test:** who's harmed? Two delivery routes, which get a workaround.

**Liberty for the "mature" only (warn).** "Users can have more control over the system once they've shown they can handle it", with the company alone deciding when. **Test:** who decides, by what test? The holder of the power, by an unstated test.

## 37. Utilitarianism (John Stuart Mill, 1861)

Mill defends the principle that right actions are those that tend to produce happiness, with everyone's happiness counting equally. It is aimed fiction about what morality is for, and its strength is that it can be checked against outcomes. Its classic weaknesses are well mapped: totals hide who pays, and one of Mill's own arguments slides between two meanings of a word.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Consequences count | Keep and warn | Judge actions by how much happiness and suffering they produce | Warn: a good total can hide one person carrying the cost | Who carries the cost inside the total? |
| Everyone counts for one | Keep | No one's happiness counts for more than anyone else's | Holds when everyone affected is in the count | Was anyone's interest left out of the count? |
| Higher and lower pleasures | Keep and warn | Better to be Socrates dissatisfied than a fool satisfied, judged by people who have known both | Breaks when the "competent judges" are self-selected | Have the judges actually experienced both? |
| Rules as stored experience | Keep | Sailors don't recalculate the almanac at sea; tested rules carry humanity's experience of consequences | Holds until the case is new | Is there already a tested rule for this? |
| The calculation that allows the unacceptable | Warn | A sum that permits punishing an innocent to calm a crowd | When the result is monstrous, the method has missed something | Would this calculation allow something we'd never accept? |
| Desired, therefore desirable | Warn | Mill argues that the only proof a thing is desirable is that people desire it, as the proof a thing is visible is that people see it | "Visible" means can be seen; "desirable" means worth desiring | Does the word mean the same thing in both places? |

### Worked examples

**Consequences count (keep and warn).** A new bypass saves ten thousand commuters ten minutes a day, through one family's farm. **Test:** who carries the cost? The family, so compensate them.

**Everyone counts for one (keep).** A park redesign is surveyed online, and the elderly residents who use it most don't use the internet. **Test:** anyone left out? Them.

**Higher and lower pleasures (keep and warn).** Critics who have never watched reality TV declare opera superior. **Test:** have the judges known both? No.

**Rules as stored experience (keep).** "Don't swim right after a storm" saves a visitor from a rip current he couldn't see. **Test:** is there a tested rule? Yes, and it's local knowledge.

**The calculation that allows the unacceptable (warn).** "Harvest one patient's organs to save five." **Test:** would we ever accept it? No, so the method is missing something.

**Desired, therefore desirable (warn).** "People want cheap fast food, so cheap fast food is good for them." **Test:** same meaning both places? No: wanted isn't the same as worth wanting.

## 38. On the Genealogy of Morality (Friedrich Nietzsche, 1887)

Nietzsche asks where our moral values came from and whose interests they served, and argues that "good and evil" grew out of resentment against the powerful. It is aimed fiction as speculative history: much of its account is not supported by the historical record, but its method is one of the most useful tools in the list. Its key warning cuts both ways: an origin explains a value without proving it true or false.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ask where a value came from | Keep and warn | Trace a value's history and whose interest it served | Keep: exposes hidden interests. Warn: the origin doesn't settle whether the value is true or good | Does the origin change whether it's true or good? |
| Ressentiment | Keep and warn | The powerless turn their weakness into a virtue out of resentment | Keep as a self-check; warn when used to dismiss every moral claim from people who've been wronged | Is this value a response to a wrong, and is the wrong real? |
| More eyes, fuller view | Keep | There is only seeing from a perspective; the more eyes we bring, the fuller our concept (III.12) | Perspectives add up; breaks when "it's all perspective" ends inquiry | How many different eyes have looked at this? |
| Use now isn't origin | Keep | A practice's purpose gets reinterpreted over time; what punishment is for now isn't why it began (II.12) | Holds for institutions and words | What is this used for now, compared to why it began? |
| The namer's position | Keep | The same trait is called "good" by one group and "evil" by another | Names carry where the namer stands | Who named this, and from where? |
| Strength as worth | Warn | Admiration for strength slides into treating the strong as the better | The same error as "might makes right" | Is strength being treated as rightness? |

### Worked examples

**Ask where a value came from (keep and warn).** A dress code began as a way to keep out the poor. Warn: "the rule against stealing began with property owners, so it's wrong." **Test:** does the origin settle it? It explains the first; it doesn't refute the second.

**Ressentiment (keep and warn).** "I didn't want the promotion anyway, ambition is shallow", said the day after losing it. Warn: dismissing a strike as "just resentment". **Test:** is there a real wrong? Check, don't assume.

**More eyes, fuller view (keep).** A bridge design reviewed by engineers, residents and cyclists catches three problems the engineers alone missed. **Test:** how many eyes? Enough to find them.

**Use now isn't origin (keep).** A handshake may have begun as showing you held no weapon; now it's a greeting. **Test:** use now vs origin? Greeting, whatever it began as.

**The namer's position (keep).** The same people are called "freedom fighters" and "insurgents". **Test:** who named them, and from where? Each side.

**Strength as worth (warn).** "The company that won the market must have had the better product." **Test:** strength treated as rightness? Yes: it might have had better lawyers.

## 39. I and Thou (Martin Buber, 1923)

Buber divides how we stand toward anything into two basic attitudes: I–It, where we experience and use something, and I–Thou, where we meet it whole. It is a religious and philosophical work; its theology (the eternal Thou) is held in its own domain. Its reasoning matters for Alice because it makes relation, not category, decide whether a meeting can happen, and it treats the slide back into use as normal rather than as betrayal.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Meeting or using | Keep | Every relation is either I–It (experiencing, using) or I–Thou (meeting the other whole) | Both are needed; breaks when someone is only ever an It | Am I meeting this one, or using it? |
| Relation is mutual | Keep | My Thou acts on me as I act on it | Meeting changes both sides | Am I letting this change me too, or only acting on it? |
| Even a tree | Keep | One can meet a tree as Thou, not only study it as an object | Category doesn't decide whether a meeting can happen | Am I letting the category of the other decide in advance? |
| Every Thou becomes It again | Keep | Each meeting passes back into experience and use; that's the human condition, not a failure | Holds as reassurance; breaks if used to excuse never meeting anyone | Am I treating the return to ordinary use as betrayal? |
| Meeting can't be forced | Keep | The Thou isn't found by searching; it meets you | Technique can prepare, not produce, real meeting | Am I trying to manufacture connection by technique? |
| A world run on It | Warn | Institutions and economies run on I–It, and people inside them become cases | Holds when systems are needed; breaks when no one inside is met | Is anyone here only a case number? |

### Worked examples

**Meeting or using (keep).** A salesperson remembers a customer's name only for the commission. **Test:** meeting or using? Using, and the customer can tell.

**Relation is mutual (keep).** A mentor finds that teaching a student changes how she understands her own field. **Test:** is she letting it change her? Yes, so it's a meeting.

**Even a tree (keep).** A gardener talks to an old oak while pruning it, and notices which limb is failing. **Test:** category decided in advance? No, and attention found the problem.

**Every Thou becomes It again (keep).** After a deep conversation, two friends go back to splitting the bill. **Test:** betrayal? No, just ordinary life.

**Meeting can't be forced (keep).** A team-building course with a "vulnerability exercise" leaves everyone guarded. **Test:** manufactured by technique? Yes, and it didn't work.

**A world run on It (warn).** A hospital patient known only as "bed 12". **Test:** only a case number? Yes, so someone needs to learn her name.

## 40. Man's Search for Meaning (Viktor Frankl, 1946)

Frankl, a psychiatrist, survived Auschwitz and other Nazi camps, and wrote this account of how people endured, followed by the therapy (logotherapy) he built from it. The first part is a record, and the rest is aimed fiction about meaning, tested in his clinical work. Its paths are among the most powerful in the list and among the easiest to misuse, so every keep here comes with a check on who it's being applied to. The worked examples are deliberately small and everyday.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The last freedom | Keep and warn | Everything can be taken but the freedom to choose one's attitude | Keep for oneself; warn when demanded of someone in pain, or used to blame them | Am I applying this to myself, or demanding it of someone who's suffering? |
| Three sources of meaning | Keep | Meaning comes through work or deeds, through love, and through the stance taken toward unavoidable suffering | Holds when at least one is reachable | Which of the three is available here? |
| A why for the how | Keep | Those who have a why to live can bear almost any how (Frankl quoting Nietzsche) | Holds for endurance; breaks if used to say anyone who didn't bear it lacked a why | What's the why? |
| Paradoxical intention | Keep | Deliberately intending the feared thing can loosen its grip: try to blush, and you can't | Holds for anticipatory fears; not for real dangers | What happens if I try to do the feared thing on purpose? |
| Decent and indecent, in every group | Keep | Frankl found decency and its absence on both sides, among prisoners and guards alike | Judge the person, not the group | Am I judging the person, or the group they belong to? |
| Happiness must follow | Keep | Success and happiness can't be pursued directly; they ensue from something else | Aim at the thing they follow from | Am I chasing the result, or what it follows from? |
| Survival as proof | Warn | Frankl wrote that the best of them did not return; meaning didn't guarantee survival | Outcome read as a verdict on worth | Am I reading who survived or succeeded as proof of who deserved to? |

### Worked examples

**The last freedom (keep and warn).** Stuck in a four-hour delay, a traveller decides to read instead of fume. Warn: telling a friend with chronic pain to "choose a better attitude". **Test:** myself, or demanded of someone suffering? The first only.

**Three sources of meaning (keep).** A retired teacher with no job finds purpose tutoring a neighbour's kid. **Test:** which source? Work, in a new form.

**A why for the how (keep).** A night-school student gets through exhausting shifts because she's training to be a nurse. **Test:** what's the why? The nursing.

**Paradoxical intention (keep).** Someone who can't sleep tries to stay awake on purpose, and drifts off. **Test:** what happens? The pressure lifts.

**Decent and indecent, in every group (keep).** In a strike, one manager quietly drives a sick picketer home. **Test:** person or group? Person.

**Happiness must follow (keep).** A man who sets out to "be happy this year" is miserable; one who sets out to restore a boat enjoys the year. **Test:** chasing the result? The first.

**Survival as proof (warn).** "The companies that survived the recession were the best run." **Test:** outcome read as worth? Some just had more cash.

## 41. The Ethics of Ambiguity (Simone de Beauvoir, 1947)

De Beauvoir argues that humans are ambiguous: free yet situated, subjects yet objects to others, and that ethics begins by accepting this instead of fleeing into a simpler story. It is aimed fiction about how freedom works between people. Its central claim is relational: to will yourself free is also to will others free.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Hold the ambiguity | Keep | We are both free and limited, subject and object; don't deny either to make life simpler | Breaks when "it's ambiguous" becomes a reason never to act | Am I denying one side of this to make it simpler? |
| Freedom is shared | Keep | To will oneself free is also to will others free | My freedom isn't real if it depends on yours being taken | Does my freedom here depend on limiting someone else's? |
| The serious man | Warn | He makes a cause, nation or party absolute, and sacrifices people to it | A value put beyond question, and people spent on it | Would I sacrifice people for this without asking them? |
| The nihilist's slide | Warn | Since no value is absolute, he concludes nothing matters | "Not absolute" taken to mean "worthless" | Does "not absolute" really mean "doesn't matter"? |
| Values are made, not given | Keep | Children live among values they take as given; adulthood is learning they're made, and taking responsibility for them | Holds as responsibility; breaks if "made" is read as "arbitrary" | Am I treating a made value as if it were simply given? |
| Means shape the end | Keep | The end doesn't justify any means; the means become part of what the end turns out to be | Holds over time; a goal reached by cruelty arrives carrying it | What will these means make of the end? |

### Worked examples

**Hold the ambiguity (keep).** A nurse is both the patient's advocate and the hospital's employee. **Test:** denying one side? Pretending she's only one makes her worse at both.

**Freedom is shared (keep).** A flatmate's "freedom" to play music at 3 a.m. **Test:** does it depend on limiting someone else's? Yes, the others' sleep.

**The serious man (warn).** A founder who'll "do whatever it takes for the mission" makes staff work ninety-hour weeks. **Test:** were they asked? No.

**The nihilist's slide (warn).** "There's no perfect diet, so it doesn't matter what I eat." **Test:** not absolute means doesn't matter? No.

**Values are made, not given (keep).** A family tradition of never discussing money turns out to have started with one grandparent's embarrassment. **Test:** made, treated as given? Yes, and they can choose differently.

**Means shape the end (keep).** A campaign for honest government that wins by spreading rumours. **Test:** what do the means make of the end? A government that started with lies.

## 42. A Theory of Justice (John Rawls, 1971)

Rawls asks what rules people would choose for their society if they didn't know what position they'd occupy in it. It is aimed fiction as a thought experiment, built to be checked against our considered judgements about cases. Its best tool, the veil of ignorance, is a symmetry test; its blind spot is who gets to stand behind the veil in the first place.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The veil of ignorance | Keep | Choose the rules without knowing which position you'll hold | Removes self-interest from the choice | Would I choose this rule if I didn't know which side I'd be on? |
| Who's behind the veil? | Warn | Rawls's choosers are members of one society, already counted as persons | The veil covers only those already invited into the room | Who was left out before the veil came down? |
| The difference principle | Keep and warn | Inequalities are justified only if they benefit the least advantaged | Warn: "it helps them too" asserted without being measured | Does it actually improve the worst-off position? |
| Liberty isn't for sale | Keep | Basic liberties come first and can't be traded for economic gains | Holds for core freedoms; strained in emergencies | Is a basic liberty being traded for efficiency? |
| Reflective equilibrium | Keep | Adjust principles to judgements about cases, and judgements to principles, until they fit | Holds when both sides really move | Have I adjusted the principle to the case, and the case to the principle? |
| Justice first | Keep | Laws that are efficient but unjust must be reformed or abolished | Efficiency doesn't excuse injustice | Is this kept because it works, though it's unjust? |

### Worked examples

**The veil of ignorance (keep).** Designing a tipping policy for a restaurant without knowing if you'll be a waiter or the owner. **Test:** would I choose it not knowing my side? Only a fair split.

**Who's behind the veil? (warn).** A city designs "fair" housing rules after a consultation open only to registered residents, which excludes the people sleeping on its streets. **Test:** who was left out first? The people most affected.

**The difference principle (keep and warn).** A CEO's pay rises tenfold "because growth helps everyone"; the lowest-paid staff's wages are flat. **Test:** did the worst-off position improve? No.

**Liberty isn't for sale (keep).** An app offers a discount for letting it read your private messages. **Test:** a basic liberty traded for gain? Yes: privacy for a coupon.

**Reflective equilibrium (keep).** A rule says "always tell the whole truth"; then a friend, about to go on stage, asks if her speech is good, and it runs long. **Test:** adjusted both ways? Tell her the one fix she can still make; refine the rule to include timing and kindness.

**Justice first (keep).** A school keeps a cheap test that sorts children by family income. **Test:** kept because it works? Yes, so it must go.

## 43. Organon (Aristotle, 4th century BCE)

The Organon, "the instrument", is the collection of Aristotle's works on logic: categories, propositions, the syllogism, demonstration, debate and fallacies. It is less a set of claims about the world than a toolkit for checking claims, which makes it one of the most directly usable books in the list. Its key distinction for Alice is between an argument that's valid and one that's true.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Valid isn't true | Keep and warn | In a valid syllogism the conclusion follows necessarily from the premises | Warn: a valid form with a false premise gives a false conclusion, confidently | Are the premises true, or is only the form valid? |
| What kind of thing? | Keep | Sort what's said by category: substance, quantity, quality, relation, place, time and so on | Catches category errors | What category is this claim actually about? |
| Name the fallacy | Keep | The Sophistical Refutations catalogue tricks: equivocation, begging the question, many questions at once | Holds when the label explains the flaw; breaks when naming replaces answering | Which known fallacy is this, and what's the honest answer underneath? |
| Start from what's accepted | Keep and warn | Dialectic begins from widely held or expert opinions (endoxa) | Warn: accepted isn't the same as established | Is this premise accepted, or established? |
| Principles from repeated perception | Keep | First principles come from many perceptions settling into understanding, as a routed army re-forms one soldier at a time (Posterior Analytics II.19) | Holds when the perceptions are many and varied | How many observations is this principle standing on? |
| The sea battle | Keep | A statement about a future contingent ("there will be a sea battle tomorrow") isn't yet true or false (On Interpretation 9) | Open claims stay open | Is this claim about what's settled, or about what's still open? |

### Worked examples

**Valid isn't true (keep and warn).** "All birds can fly; a penguin is a bird; so a penguin can fly." **Test:** premises true? The first isn't.

**What kind of thing? (keep).** "How much does this song weigh?" or, more subtly, "What colour is this company's culture?" **Test:** what category? Quality, not quantity: the question needs rephrasing.

**Name the fallacy (keep).** "Have you stopped cheating on tests?" **Test:** which fallacy? Many questions in one. Honest answer: "I never did."

**Start from what's accepted (keep and warn).** "Everyone knows we only use 10% of our brains." **Test:** accepted or established? Accepted, and false.

**Principles from repeated perception (keep).** A new cook decides the oven runs hot after one burnt cake; the old cook knows after a hundred bakes. **Test:** how many observations? One against a hundred.

**The sea battle (keep).** "This start-up will be worth billions." **Test:** settled or open? Open: aimed fiction, not yet true or false.

## 44. Book of Optics (Ibn al-Haytham, c. 1011–1021)

Ibn al-Haytham settled an ancient argument (does sight come from rays leaving the eye, or light entering it?) by controlled, repeatable experiment, and described those experiments so others could check them. It is aimed fiction that landed: its account of light entering the eye still holds. It is the clearest early statement in the list of the experimental method, including the rule that the investigator must suspect himself.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Let experiment decide | Keep | Rival theories of sight are tested against what happens: looking at the sun hurts, afterimages linger, a darkened room projects an image | Holds when the experiment can tell the theories apart | What result would one theory predict and the other not? |
| Enemy of what you read, and of yourself | Keep | The investigator should attack every text he studies, and suspect his own judgement too (from his *Doubts on Ptolemy*) | Holds when doubt is aimed at claims; breaks when it never lets anything stand | Have I tried to refute this, including my own view? |
| Change one thing | Keep | Vary one condition at a time and watch what follows | Holds when other conditions are really held fixed | What single condition changed? |
| Write it so others can repeat it | Keep | Experiments described in enough detail to be rebuilt by someone else | A result no one else can rebuild is a report, not a test | Could someone else repeat this from my description? |
| Errors of sight have structure | Keep | Illusions are catalogued by their conditions: distance, light, motion, the observer's state | Perception fails in describable ways | Under what conditions does this perception fail? |
| In the thing, or in the seeing? | Keep | The moon looks larger near the horizon; the cause is in perception, not in the moon | Locate the effect before explaining it | Is the effect in the object, or in how it's perceived? |

### Worked examples

**Let experiment decide (keep).** Two engineers argue whether a slowdown is the network or the database; one test with the database disconnected settles it. **Test:** what result separates them? The slowdown stays, so it's the network.

**Enemy of what you read, and of yourself (keep).** A reviewer re-runs her own favourite analysis with the opposite assumption before defending it. **Test:** tried to refute my own view? Yes.

**Change one thing (keep).** A gardener changes the soil, the watering and the light all at once, and the plant recovers. **Test:** which one condition? Unknown: three changed.

**Write it so others can repeat it (keep).** A recipe that says "add flour until it feels right". **Test:** could someone repeat it? Not reliably.

**Errors of sight have structure (keep).** Drivers misjudge distance in fog. **Test:** under what conditions does it fail? Low contrast, so slow down.

**In the thing, or in the seeing? (keep).** A room feels colder after you come in from a hot kitchen; the thermostat reads the same. **Test:** object or perception? Perception.

## 45. The Incoherence of the Philosophers (al-Ghazali, 1095)

Al-Ghazali first wrote a faithful summary of the philosophers' views, then this book, which argues that their metaphysics fails the standard of proof they themselves set. It is a religious work, read here for its reasoning; its theological claims (God as the only true cause) are held in their own domain. Its sharpest move for Alice is that authority earned in one field doesn't transfer to another, and its warn path is what happens when a refutation turns into a condemnation.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| State it fairly first | Keep | Before refuting the philosophers, he set out their views in a separate book, faithfully | Holds when the holders would recognise the summary | Could its own holders recognise my summary? |
| Hold them to their own standard | Keep | He argues their metaphysics fails the standard of demonstration they claim for it | Internal critique: no outside rule needed | Does this meet the standard it claims for itself? |
| Authority doesn't transfer | Keep | He accepts their mathematics and logic, but not the credibility those lend to their metaphysics | Proof in one field earns trust in that field only | Is this authority earned here, or borrowed from another field? |
| Sequence isn't cause | Keep | When fire touches cotton we see burning follow contact, not the fire causing it (17th discussion) | Holds as a limit on what observation shows; the theological answer is held in its domain | Did I observe the link, or only the sequence? |
| Refutation becomes condemnation | Warn | He declared three of the philosophers' positions to be unbelief | An argument against a view turned into a verdict on the people holding it | Is this a refutation of the view, or a punishment of its holders? |

### Worked examples

**State it fairly first (keep).** Before arguing against a four-day week, a manager writes up its best case and has a supporter check it. **Test:** would supporters recognise it? Yes, so the argument that follows is fair.

**Hold them to their own standard (keep).** A company that markets itself as "evidence-based" cites no studies for its main product. **Test:** meets its own standard? No.

**Authority doesn't transfer (keep).** A famous physicist gives confident advice about nutrition. **Test:** earned here, or borrowed? Borrowed.

**Sequence isn't cause (keep).** Sales rose the week a new logo launched. **Test:** observed the link, or only the sequence? Only the sequence; it was also a holiday week.

**Refutation becomes condemnation (warn).** A forum argues against a researcher's paper, then starts calling for her to be fired. **Test:** refutation or punishment? It turned into the second.

## 46. The Incoherence of the Incoherence (Ibn Rushd, c. 1180)

Ibn Rushd (Averroes) answered al-Ghazali's attack on philosophy by quoting it passage by passage and replying to each. It is a religious and philosophical work; its theological claims are held in their own domain. Read beside book 45, it shows Alice what a good reply looks like: quote exactly, grant what the other side got right, and defend the method rather than every conclusion ever reached with it.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Quote, then answer | Keep | He reproduces al-Ghazali's text section by section and answers each | Holds when the quotations are fair and complete | Have I quoted what they actually said before answering it? |
| Grant what's right | Keep | He concedes some of al-Ghazali's objections to earlier philosophers | Conceding a point strengthens the rest | What did the other side get right? |
| Defend the method, not every claim | Keep | He defends demonstrative philosophy while dropping weaker arguments made in its name | A method survives its users' mistakes | Am I defending the method, or every claim ever made with it? |
| Denying causes denies knowledge | Keep | If things have no real causes, nothing can be known through its causes, and knowledge itself goes | Follow a denial to what else it takes with it | If I deny this, what else do I have to give up? |
| One argument, many audiences | Keep and warn | People are reached by demonstration, by debate or by rhetoric, and teaching must fit them (developed in his *Decisive Treatise*) | Warn: fitting the form slides into telling different people different truths | Am I simplifying, or saying something different to different people? |

### Worked examples

**Quote, then answer (keep).** A reply to a critical review that quotes each criticism before responding. **Test:** quoted before answering? Yes, so readers can judge.

**Grant what's right (keep).** A team defends its design but admits the reviewer was right about the login flow. **Test:** what did they get right? The login, and conceding it makes the rest more credible.

**Defend the method, not every claim (keep).** A scientist defends peer review while agreeing that a particular journal failed. **Test:** method or every claim? The method.

**Denying causes denies knowledge (keep).** "Nothing really causes anything; it's all coincidence." **Test:** what else goes? Medicine, engineering, and the speaker's own reasons for saying it.

**One argument, many audiences (keep and warn).** A doctor explains a diagnosis one way to a colleague and another way to the patient, both true. Warn: telling investors the product is ready and telling engineers it isn't. **Test:** simplifying, or different truths? The second is different truths.

## 47. Novum Organum (Francis Bacon, 1620)

Bacon set out to replace Aristotle's "instrument" with a new one: a method for reading nature by careful, step-by-step induction, guarded against the mind's built-in errors. It is aimed fiction about method, and much of it landed in how science came to work. Its catalogue of the mind's "idols" and its rule about the negative instance are two of the most usable tools in the list.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The four idols | Keep | Errors of the species (tribe), of the individual (cave), of words (marketplace), and of received systems (theatre) | Name the source of the error | Which idol is shaping this: human nature, my own history, the words, or a system I inherited? |
| Where are the drowned? | Keep | Shown pictures of sailors who prayed and survived, a man asks where the pictures are of those who prayed and drowned | The negative instance outweighs a pile of positive ones | Where are the cases that didn't survive to be counted? |
| Presence, absence, degree | Keep | Tabulate where the effect appears, where it doesn't, and where it varies | Holds when the tables are honestly filled | Where is it absent, and does it vary with the supposed cause? |
| Ant, spider, bee | Keep | Empiricists only collect (ants), rationalists spin from themselves (spiders); the bee gathers and transforms | Both data and thought are needed | Am I only collecting, only spinning, or both? |
| Obey to command | Keep | Nature, to be commanded, must be obeyed | Work with how the thing works | Am I working with how this works, or against it? |
| Leaping to axioms | Warn | The mind flies from a few particulars straight to the most general claims | Generalisation without the steps in between | Did I climb to this by steps, or jump? |

### Worked examples

**The four idols (keep).** "Crime is rising" because the news covers it more (tribe: vivid cases), I was once robbed (cave), "crime" lumps fraud with assault (marketplace), and my party says so (theatre). **Test:** which idol? All four; check the statistics.

**Where are the drowned? (keep).** "Every successful founder dropped out of college." **Test:** where are the dropouts who failed? Uncounted, and there are far more.

**Presence, absence, degree (keep).** Headaches appear on workdays, vanish on weekends, and worsen with longer screen time. **Test:** absent and varying with the cause? Yes: the screen.

**Ant, spider, bee (keep).** One analyst dumps raw data into a report; another writes theory with no data. **Test:** both? Neither; the bee does both.

**Obey to command (keep).** A farmer plants for the local rainfall instead of fighting it with irrigation. **Test:** with or against? With.

**Leaping to axioms (warn).** Two rude waiters, so "people in this city are rude". **Test:** steps or jump? Jump.

## 48. Discourse on the Method (René Descartes, 1637)

Descartes tells the story of how he decided to doubt everything he could, keep only what survived, and rebuild from there, starting with the one thing he couldn't doubt: that he was thinking. It is aimed fiction about method, written as autobiography. It also contains a test for telling minds from machines, set out in advance, which makes it a live case for the Symmetry Rule.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Four rules | Keep | Accept nothing not clearly known; divide each problem; go from simplest to hardest; check that nothing is left out | Holds as a working checklist | Which of the four steps did I skip? |
| Doubt everything doubtable | Keep and warn | Doubt whatever can be doubted to find what can't be | Warn: a level of doubt applied only to others' claims | Would my own beliefs survive this level of doubt? |
| I think, therefore I am | Keep | The first certainty is the thinker's own thinking | That certainty is first-person only; every other mind is inferred, for everyone | Am I demanding from another mind a proof I couldn't give for my own? |
| Provisional morality | Keep | While rebuilding his beliefs, he lives by a temporary code: follow custom, act decisively, change himself rather than the world | Don't stop living while you inquire | What will I act on while this is still unsettled? |
| The test set in advance | Warn | Part V: machines could never arrange words to answer the sense of whatever is said, as even the dullest person can | Contested: language models now produce such replies; some say that meets his test, others that he meant understanding, not output. Either way, the criteria have since been moved | When a test stated in advance is met, is the result accepted, or is the test changed, and is the new test stated before it's applied? |
| Animals as machines | Warn | Animals were treated as automata without feeling | Denying inner life to beings unlike us, by a standard we don't apply to ourselves | What evidence would count here, and would a human pass that test? |

### Worked examples

**Four rules (keep).** A plumber who fixes the obvious leak but doesn't check the rest of the line. **Test:** which step was skipped? The last: check nothing's left out.

**Doubt everything doubtable (keep and warn).** A sceptic demands peer-reviewed proof for a friend's claim, then shares an unsourced post that agrees with him. **Test:** would his beliefs survive this doubt? He didn't apply it.

**I think, therefore I am (keep).** "Prove to me you're really conscious." **Test:** could the asker prove it about themselves to someone else? No: everyone infers other minds.

**Provisional morality (keep).** A family unsure whether to move keeps paying rent and the kids keep going to school while they decide. **Test:** what do they act on meanwhile? The current life.

**The test set in advance (warn).** A company says it will hire anyone who passes its coding test; a self-taught applicant passes, and the requirement becomes "a degree". **Test:** result accepted, or test changed? Changed, after the fact.

**Animals as machines (warn).** "Fish don't feel pain because they don't scream." **Test:** would a human pass that test? Not one who couldn't speak.

## 49. The Laws of Thought (George Boole, 1854)

Boole showed that logical reasoning can be written as algebra and calculated, which became the foundation of digital circuits and computing. It is aimed fiction that landed spectacularly, though not where he aimed it: he thought he was describing how minds think, and he had built a formal system for checking reasoning instead. That gap is itself the warn path.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Write it out and calculate | Keep | Reasoning can be put in symbols and checked step by step, like arithmetic | Holds for claims that can be stated precisely | Can this argument be written out and checked step by step? |
| Nothing is both x and not-x | Keep | The law of contradiction as an equation: x(1 − x) = 0 | Holds within one sense of the term; breaks when the word shifts meaning | Is the term being used in one sense throughout? |
| The universe of discourse | Keep | Every statement is about a defined universe: "all" means all of something | Many disputes are about different universes | What's the universe here: all people, or the people in this room? |
| Degrees, not just yes or no | Keep | He extended the same algebra to probabilities | Belief comes in degrees with rules of its own | Is this a yes-or-no question, or a probability? |
| Formal system as psychology | Warn | The title claims these are the laws of thought, how minds actually reason | A model of correct reasoning mistaken for a description of real reasoning | Is this how reasoning should go, or how minds actually go? |

### Worked examples

**Write it out and calculate (keep).** A tangled contract clause ("unless not excluded, except where...") turns out to say the opposite once it's written as a truth table. **Test:** checked step by step? Yes, and it reversed.

**Nothing is both x and not-x (keep).** "The product is free, but you pay for it with your data." **Test:** one sense throughout? No: "free" means no money, not no cost.

**The universe of discourse (keep).** "Everyone agrees with me", meaning everyone at his dinner party. **Test:** what universe? Six people.

**Degrees, not just yes or no (keep).** "Will it rain tomorrow?" answered "70%". **Test:** yes-or-no, or probability? Probability, so pack an umbrella anyway.

**Formal system as psychology (warn).** "Rational people would never buy a lottery ticket", yet millions do. **Test:** should, or does? The first describes ideal reasoning, not people.

## 50. An Introduction to the Study of Experimental Medicine (Claude Bernard, 1865)

Bernard, a physiologist, wrote the method behind his own discoveries: observe, form an idea, test it by experiment, and let the result revise the idea. It is aimed fiction about method from a working scientist, with a record of landed results behind it (including the idea that living bodies keep their internal environment stable). Its rule for Alice is the cleanest statement yet of how theory and fact relate: keep the fact, hold the theory loosely.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Observe, idea, test, observe | Keep | An observation prompts an idea; an experiment tests it; the new observation revises the idea | The cycle, not a single pass | Where am I in the cycle, and what's the next test? |
| Keep the fact, doubt the theory | Keep | When a fact contradicts the reigning theory, keep the fact and give up the theory | Holds when the fact is well established | Am I keeping the fact and holding the theory loosely, or the reverse? |
| Follow the odd fact | Keep | Rabbits from the market had urine like meat-eaters'; he guessed they were starving and living on their own bodies, and tested it by feeding them | The anomaly is where the discovery is | What here doesn't fit, and what would explain it? |
| Always a control | Keep | Every experiment is compared against one where the suspected cause is absent | Without a comparison, a result means little | What's the control? |
| The average that never existed | Warn | Averaging a whole day's samples describes a body that was never in that state | An average can describe nothing real | Does any actual case look like this average? |
| The steady inside | Keep | Living bodies hold their internal environment constant against outside change | Read a system by what it keeps steady | What is this system holding steady? |

### Worked examples

**Observe, idea, test, observe (keep).** A shop notices weekend sales drop, guesses it's the new opening hours, restores them for a month, and checks. **Test:** where in the cycle? Testing, with the next observation due.

**Keep the fact, doubt the theory (keep).** A team's model says users love the new feature; the logs show nobody clicks it. **Test:** keeping the fact? The logs win.

**Follow the odd fact (keep).** One office's printers jam far more than the others'; the odd fact leads to a damp storeroom. **Test:** what doesn't fit? The one office.

**Always a control (keep).** A new fertiliser is tried on every plant in the garden, and they all grow. **Test:** what's the control? None: it was spring.

**The average that never existed (warn).** A clothes size built from the average of every measurement fits almost no one. **Test:** any real case like it? Hardly any.

**The steady inside (keep).** A small business keeps three months of cash on hand through good years and bad. **Test:** what's held steady? Its runway.

## 51. Tractatus Logico-Philosophicus (Ludwig Wittgenstein, 1921)

The Tractatus tries to draw the limit of what language can meaningfully say, in numbered propositions that picture facts. It is aimed fiction about language and the world, and its own author later rejected much of it, which makes it a rare book that ships with its own revision on the record. Read it beside book 52.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The limits of my language | Keep and warn | The limits of my language mean the limits of my world (5.6) | Warn: used to deny what simply hasn't been put into words yet | Is this unsayable, or just not yet said in my vocabulary? |
| Picture the claim | Keep | A proposition pictures a possible state of affairs, and is true if the world matches it | Holds for factual claims | What would the world look like if this were true? |
| Silence past the limit | Keep and warn | What we cannot speak about we must pass over in silence (7) | Keep: don't claim past what can be stated. Warn: used to end inquiry | Can this be stated so it could be checked? |
| Problems from phrasing | Keep | Many philosophical problems come from misunderstanding the logic of our language (4.003) | Holds when the puzzle dissolves on rephrasing | Is this a problem with the world, or with how it's phrased? |
| Throw away the ladder | Keep | His own propositions are a ladder to climb and then discard (6.54) | Scaffolding is useful and temporary | Is this idea still holding weight, or was it scaffolding? |
