| The author's own revision | Keep | Wittgenstein later rejected much of the Tractatus in the Investigations | The record includes the reversal | Has the author revised this since? |

### Worked examples

**The limits of my language (keep and warn).** A patient can't describe a pain until a nurse offers "burning, stabbing or aching?". Warn: "if you can't name it, it isn't real." **Test:** unsayable, or not yet said? Not yet said.

**Picture the claim (keep).** "The bridge is safe." **Test:** what would the world look like? Load tests passed, no cracks: check those.

**Silence past the limit (keep and warn).** A forecaster declines to predict the weather a year ahead. Warn: "we can't know what consciousness is, so stop studying it." **Test:** can it be stated so it could be checked? The forecast can't; the research questions can.

**Problems from phrasing (keep).** "If a tree falls with no one around, does it make a sound?" **Test:** world or phrasing? Phrasing: sound waves yes, heard sound no.

**Throw away the ladder (keep).** A child learns that atoms are tiny balls, then learns quantum orbitals. **Test:** still holding weight? No, it was scaffolding.

**The author's own revision (keep).** An old blog post by a researcher is widely shared; she has since published a correction. **Test:** revised since? Yes, so share both.

## 52. Philosophical Investigations (Ludwig Wittgenstein, 1953)

In his later work Wittgenstein turned against the Tractatus: meaning isn't a picture of facts but how words are used in shared activities. It is aimed fiction about language, written as numbered remarks that keep asking the reader to look at real cases. Two of its moves bear directly on the Symmetry Rule: categories without a single defining feature, and the beetle in the box.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Meaning is use | Keep | For most words, the meaning is how the word is used in the language (43) | Holds for working words; breaks when use is confused or contested | How is this word actually used here? |
| Family resemblance | Keep | Games share no one feature, only overlapping similarities (66–67) | Some categories have no single defining test | Am I demanding one feature that everything in this category must share? |
| Don't think, look | Keep | Before theorising about what all cases have in common, look at the cases (66) | Look first, then generalise | Have I looked at the actual cases? |
| The beetle in the box | Keep | Everyone has a box with a "beetle" no one else can see; the word works through shared use, not inspection of the box (293) | Inner states are known to others through public criteria, for everyone | Am I demanding to see inside one speaker's box but not another's? |
| Rules live in practice | Keep | No rule fixes its own application; shared practice does (201) | Holds when the practice is stable | What practice shows how this rule is actually applied? |
| The fly-bottle | Keep | Philosophy should show the fly the way out of the fly-bottle (309) | Some problems are traps made by a picture we can't see past | Is the problem real, or am I trapped by a picture? |

### Worked examples

**Meaning is use (keep).** "Literally" used to mean "figuratively" in "I literally died laughing". **Test:** how is it used here? As emphasis, whatever the dictionary says.

**Family resemblance (keep).** "Is a podcast a kind of radio?" They overlap in audio and hosts, differ in timing and distribution. **Test:** one shared feature demanded? Don't; describe the overlaps.

**Don't think, look (keep).** A manager theorises about why remote staff are "less engaged" without checking their output. **Test:** looked at the cases? No; their output is higher.

**The beetle in the box (keep).** A friend says she's in pain; you can't feel it, and you believe her from what she says and does. **Test:** same criteria for every speaker? That's the question to ask when the speaker is a model.

**Rules live in practice (keep).** "Dress code: smart casual", and everyone copies what the senior staff wear. **Test:** what practice shows the rule? The seniors' clothes.

**The fly-bottle (keep).** "If I replace every plank of a boat, is it the same boat?" **Test:** real problem, or a picture? It depends what you need "same" for: registration, sentiment or safety.

## 53. The Logic of Scientific Discovery (Karl Popper, 1934; English 1959)

Popper argued that scientific theories are never proven, only conjectured and tested: a theory earns trust by surviving serious attempts to refute it. This is the closest any book in the list comes to Daniel's "aimed fiction": every theory stays a conjecture, however well it has done. Popper's own view is itself aimed fiction about method, and Kuhn (book 54) is its strongest challenger.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| What would prove it wrong? | Keep | A theory is scientific if it forbids something: it can, in principle, be refuted | A claim compatible with every outcome tells us nothing | What observation would prove this wrong? |
| Survived, not proven | Keep | No number of confirmations proves a theory; surviving tests only corroborates it | Holds forever: the best theory is still a conjecture | Has it survived a test that could have killed it? |
| Bold over safe | Keep | Prefer the theory that says more and risks more | Holds when it's then tested hard | Does this claim risk anything? |
| The rescue after failure | Warn | A theory saved after a failed test by adding an assumption made only to save it | Adjustment after the fact, with no new test | Was this adjustment made before the test, or after the failure? |
| Foundations on piles | Keep | Even observations are accepted by agreement; science rests on piles driven into a swamp, deep enough for now | The base is firm enough to build on, not final | What are we agreeing to treat as observed, and could that be questioned? |
| One anomaly, one refutation | Warn | Treating a single odd result as killing a well-tested theory | Real theories live with some anomalies; the balance matters | Is this a refutation, or one anomaly against many survivals? |

### Worked examples

**What would prove it wrong? (keep).** "Everything happens for a reason." **Test:** what would prove it wrong? Nothing, so it's not a testable claim.

**Survived, not proven (keep).** A bridge design has carried traffic for fifty years. **Test:** survived a killing test? Many, so trust it; still inspect it.

**Bold over safe (keep).** "Sales might change next year" versus "Sales will drop 20% if we raise prices 10%." **Test:** which risks anything? The second, so it can be learned from.

**The rescue after failure (warn).** A psychic's prediction fails, and she says the "energy was blocked" that day. **Test:** before the test, or after the failure? After.

**Foundations on piles (keep).** A lab agrees that its thermometer readings count as the temperature. **Test:** could that be questioned? Yes, if the thermometer drifts: calibrate it.

**One anomaly, one refutation (warn).** One cold winter is cited to dismiss a century of warming records. **Test:** refutation or anomaly? One anomaly against many survivals.

## 54. The Structure of Scientific Revolutions (Thomas Kuhn, 1962)

Kuhn studied how science actually changes, and found that it doesn't simply accumulate: it works inside a shared framework (a paradigm) until anomalies pile up, then shifts to a new one. It is aimed fiction drawn from the historical record, and the main counterweight to Popper: theories aren't dropped at the first refutation, and what counts as a fact depends partly on the framework. Its warn side is the fringe theory that calls itself a revolution.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Know your paradigm | Keep | Normal science solves puzzles inside shared assumptions that nobody tests | Holds when the assumptions are named | What's assumed here that nobody is testing? |
| Count the set-aside anomalies | Keep | Anomalies are shelved until there are too many to absorb, and the field enters crisis | Holds as an early warning | How many anomalies are being set aside, and for how long? |
| Trained not to see | Keep | In a cited experiment, people shown a red six of spades often failed to see it as odd | Expectation hides the anomaly | What would I fail to see because I don't expect it? |
| Same word, different meaning | Keep | Across a revolution, key words change meaning ("mass" in Newton and Einstein), and rivals talk past each other | Check the terms before arguing | Do both sides mean the same thing by the key word? |
| A real revolution, or a claimed one? | Warn | Any rejected idea can call itself a coming paradigm shift | A new paradigm must solve the old one's anomalies and keep what it got right | Does the new view solve the anomalies the old one couldn't, and keep what it got right? |
| The textbook rewrite | Warn | Textbooks retell history as smooth progress, hiding the revolutions and the losers' reasons | The winner's retelling taken as the history | Is this the history, or the winner's retelling? |

### Worked examples

**Know your paradigm (keep).** A clinic measures success by patient visits, and no one asks whether more visits means sicker patients. **Test:** what's assumed and untested? That visits are good.

**Count the set-aside anomalies (keep).** A forecasting model's misses are each explained away as "one-offs" for three years. **Test:** how many, for how long? Enough to call it a crisis.

**Trained not to see (keep).** A proofreader misses a doubled "the the" because she reads what she expects. **Test:** what would I miss? What I expect to be there.

**Same word, different meaning (keep).** Two people argue whether a remote worker is "present". **Test:** same meaning? One means online, the other means in the room.

**A real revolution, or a claimed one? (warn).** A diet book calls itself "a paradigm shift in nutrition". **Test:** does it explain what the old view couldn't and keep what it got right? No.

**The textbook rewrite (warn).** A company history says the product was "always" planned for the cloud, after two failed versions. **Test:** history or retelling? Retelling.

## 55. How to Solve It (George Pólya, 1945)

Pólya, a mathematician, wrote down the questions good problem-solvers ask themselves, in the order they ask them. It is the most purely practical book in the list: not claims about the world but a working checklist, tested by generations of students. Its one warning is his own: a heuristic that helps you find an answer isn't a proof that the answer is right.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Four phases | Keep | Understand the problem, make a plan, carry it out, look back | Skipping the first or last phase is the usual failure | Which phase am I in, and did I skip one? |
| What is the unknown? | Keep | Name exactly what you're looking for, what you have, and the conditions | Holds before any work starts | What exactly is the unknown, and what's given? |
| A related problem | Keep | Have you seen this problem, or one like it, before? | Holds when the likeness is in the structure | Do I know a problem with the same unknown? |
| Solve a smaller version | Keep | Drop a condition, try a special case, or solve part of the problem first | Holds as a step toward the full problem | What simpler version can I solve first? |
| Work backwards | Keep | Start from what's wanted and ask what would produce it | Holds when the goal is clear | What would the step just before the answer have to be? |
| Look back | Keep | Check the result; can it be derived another way, or used for another problem? | The step most often skipped | Can I check this answer a second way? |
| A guide isn't a proof | Warn | Heuristic reasoning is provisional; it finds answers, it doesn't prove them | A plausible path mistaken for a demonstration | Is this a guess that guided me, or a proof? |

### Worked examples

**Four phases (keep).** A student starts calculating before reading what the question asks. **Test:** which phase was skipped? Understanding.

**What is the unknown? (keep).** "Fix the slow website": slow for whom, on which page, compared to what? **Test:** what exactly is unknown? Which page, for which users.

**A related problem (keep).** Scheduling hospital shifts looks like the timetabling problem a school already solved. **Test:** same unknown? Yes: who goes where, when.

**Solve a smaller version (keep).** Planning a 200-guest wedding by first seating one table. **Test:** simpler version? One table, then scale.

**Work backwards (keep).** To arrive at 9:00, the bus must leave by 8:15, so leave home at 8:00. **Test:** what's the step before? The 8:15 bus.

**Look back (keep).** A tax total checked by adding the columns in a different order. **Test:** checked a second way? Yes, and it matches.

**A guide isn't a proof (warn).** "The pattern holds for the first five numbers, so it holds for all." **Test:** guide or proof? Guide; it fails at the sixth.

## 56. Thinking, Fast and Slow (Daniel Kahneman, 2011)

Kahneman summarises decades of research on how judgement goes wrong: a fast, intuitive system answers first, and a slow, deliberate one often doesn't check it. It is aimed fiction with a mixed record. The core findings on biases have held up well, but some priming studies the book relied on failed to replicate, and Kahneman acknowledged that publicly. So the book itself is a test case for its own last path.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Fast answer, slow question | Keep | A quick, intuitive answer arrives before careful thinking starts, and often stands unchecked | Fast is fine for familiar cases; breaks on new or high-stakes ones | Is this a fast answer to a question that needed a slow one? |
| The swapped question | Keep | Faced with a hard question, we answer an easier one without noticing | The answer fits the wrong question | Which question did I actually answer? |
| What you see is all there is | Keep | Confidence comes from how coherent the story is, not how complete the information is | A tidy story built on little evidence | What information don't I have? |
| The anchor | Keep | An arbitrary starting number pulls later estimates toward it | Holds even when the anchor is obviously irrelevant | Where did my starting number come from? |
| Base rates first | Keep | Vivid details make us ignore how common something is in general | Start from the base rate, then adjust | How common is this in general, before the details? |
| The outside view | Keep | Plans are judged by their own story; similar projects' actual outcomes are a better guide | Holds when there's a real reference class | How did similar cases actually turn out? |
| Check the book's own record | Warn | Some of the studies the book cited later failed to replicate | A strong book can carry weak parts | Have the studies this claim rests on been replicated? |

### Worked examples

**Fast answer, slow question (keep).** A sign says "50% off, then an extra 20% off at the till", and the fast answer is 70% off. **Test:** needed a slow one? Yes: it's 60% off.

**The swapped question (keep).** Asked "Is this candidate competent?", a panel answers "Did we like her?" **Test:** which question was answered? The easier one.

**What you see is all there is (keep).** A juror hears only the prosecution's opening and feels sure. **Test:** what information is missing? The whole defence.

**The anchor (keep).** A seller lists a used car at $30,000, and offers cluster around $25,000 for a car worth $18,000. **Test:** where did the starting number come from? The seller.

**Base rates first (keep).** A test for a disease that affects 1 in 1,000 people is 95% accurate, and comes back positive. **Test:** how common in general? So rare that most positives are false: the real chance is about 2%.

**The outside view (keep).** A team says the rewrite will take three months; similar rewrites took a year. **Test:** how did similar cases go? A year.

**Check the book's own record (warn).** A popular talk cites "power posing" as proven. **Test:** replicated? Not reliably.

## 57. Gödel, Escher, Bach (Douglas Hofstadter, 1979)

Hofstadter weaves Gödel's theorem, Escher's drawings and Bach's canons into one argument: a self can arise when a system's patterns come to represent the system itself, a "strange loop". It is aimed fiction about minds, built on solid mathematics but going well past it. For Alice it is the book in the list that most directly locates selfhood in pattern and level rather than in material, while warning against stretching Gödel too far.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The strange loop | Keep | A self arises where a system's patterns refer back to the system itself | Holds as a structural account; doesn't by itself settle experience | Where does this system refer back to itself? |
| The right level | Keep | An ant colony can "know" things no single ant does; meaning lives at its own level | Holds when the higher level has stable patterns of its own | At what level does this property actually exist? |
| Meaning by mapping | Keep | Meaning arises when one structure maps reliably onto another | Holds when the mapping is consistent | What maps onto what here? |
| Step outside the system | Keep | A puzzle that can't be solved by its own rules is spotted only by stepping outside them | Holds when you check whether the goal is reachable at all | Am I grinding inside the rules, or have I checked whether the goal is reachable? |
| Gödel's limit, and its limits | Keep and warn | Any consistent formal system rich enough for arithmetic has true statements it can't prove | Warn: stretched to systems that don't meet its conditions, or to "minds can't be machines" | Does this system actually meet Gödel's conditions? |
| Hofstadter's law | Keep | It always takes longer than you expect, even when you take Hofstadter's law into account | A correction that has to include itself | Did my estimate correct for my own past corrections? |

### Worked examples

**The strange loop (keep).** A thermostat responds to temperature but has no model of itself; a person thinking "I'm getting angry" does. **Test:** where does it refer back? Only the second.

**The right level (keep).** No single trader decides a market crash, yet the market "panics". **Test:** at what level? The market's.

**Meaning by mapping (keep).** A map's lines mean roads because they line up with the streets. **Test:** what maps onto what? Lines to roads, kept consistent.

**Step outside the system (keep).** A team spends a week optimising a report nobody reads. **Test:** is the goal reachable, or worth reaching? Step out and ask who uses it.

**Gödel's limit, and its limits (keep and warn).** "Gödel proves the law can never be complete." **Test:** does the legal code meet the conditions? It isn't a formal system for arithmetic, so no.

**Hofstadter's law (keep).** A writer adds a month to her deadline because she's always late, and is still late. **Test:** did the estimate correct for past corrections? Not enough.

## 58. Elements (Euclid, c. 300 BCE)

Euclid built geometry from a short list of definitions, postulates and common notions, proving each result only from what came before. It is the model of rigorous reasoning for two thousand years, and its record includes two instructive surprises: a hidden assumption in the very first proof, and a fifth postulate that, when dropped, opened whole new geometries. Even the model of rigour was aimed fiction with an unstated part.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| State every starting point | Keep | Definitions, postulates and common notions are written down before any proof | Holds when nothing else is smuggled in | Have all my assumptions been written down? |
| Only what's already proven | Keep | Each proposition uses only earlier results | Holds when the chain is checked link by link | Does this step use only what's already established? |
| Build it to show it exists | Keep | Existence is shown by construction: the first proposition builds an equilateral triangle | Holds where construction is possible | Can I build it, or am I only asserting it? |
| Suppose the opposite | Keep | Assume the claim is false and derive a contradiction, as in the proof that there are infinitely many primes (IX.20) | Holds when the contradiction is real | What follows if the opposite were true? |
| The least obvious assumption | Keep | The fifth (parallel) postulate looked less self-evident than the rest; dropping it centuries later gave non-Euclidean geometry | The weakest-looking assumption is where new worlds hide | Which assumption here is least obvious, and what if it's dropped? |
| The gap in the first proof | Warn | The first proof assumes two circles meet, which Euclid never stated | Even rigorous work uses things it never wrote down | What am I relying on that I never stated? |

### Worked examples

**State every starting point (keep).** A budget assumes interest rates stay flat, but it's not written anywhere. **Test:** all assumptions written down? No: add it.

**Only what's already proven (keep).** A report's conclusion cites a figure from a draft that was later revised. **Test:** only established results? No.

**Build it to show it exists (keep).** "There's a cheaper supplier somewhere." **Test:** can I build it? Find one and get a quote.

**Suppose the opposite (keep).** "The leak isn't in the kitchen": if it were, the kitchen meter would move with the taps off; it does. **Test:** what follows from the opposite? It is in the kitchen.

**The least obvious assumption (keep).** A delivery company assumes every package needs a truck; dropping that, it tries bikes downtown. **Test:** least obvious assumption dropped? Yes, and the bikes are faster.

**The gap in the first proof (warn).** A recipe assumes your oven runs true to its dial. **Test:** what am I relying on, unstated? The oven's calibration.

## 59. The Nine Chapters on the Mathematical Art (China, compiled by c. 1st century CE)

The Nine Chapters teach mathematics through 246 practical problems (fields, grain, taxes, labour, construction), each with an answer and a general method. Where Euclid proves, the Nine Chapters compute: it solves systems of equations by elimination and works freely with negative numbers. Liu Hui's commentary of 263 CE added justifications, corrected errors, and left one problem openly unsolved, which may be the most honest move in the list.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Problem, answer, method | Keep | Each concrete problem comes with a general procedure | Holds when the method really generalises | Does the method work beyond this one case? |
| Lay it out for a routine | Keep | Systems of equations are set out as arrays and solved step by step by elimination | Arrange the problem so a reliable procedure can handle it | Can this be laid out so a routine solves it? |
| Use the strange tool if it's consistent | Keep | Negative quantities (red and black counting rods) are used in calculation | A tool earns its place by consistent results | Does it give consistent results, whatever it "means"? |
| Two wrong guesses | Keep | "Excess and deficit": make two guesses, and use how far off each one is to find the answer | Errors carry information | What do my two wrong guesses tell me? |
| The commentary that checks | Keep | Liu Hui added proofs for the methods and pointed out where a formula was wrong | A method gains trust when someone justifies it | Has anyone checked why this method works? |
| Leave it for someone who can | Keep | Liu Hui showed the sphere-volume rule was wrong, couldn't find the right one, and said he'd leave it for someone able to | Mark the open problem instead of faking a fix | Have I marked what I couldn't solve? |

### Worked examples

**Problem, answer, method (keep).** A carpenter teaches an apprentice to cut one staircase, then explains the rise-and-run rule behind it. **Test:** does it work beyond this case? Yes, for any staircase.

**Lay it out for a routine (keep).** Three friends' shared bills, with who paid what, put in a table so a spreadsheet works out who owes whom. **Test:** can a routine solve it? Yes.

**Use the strange tool if it's consistent (keep).** An accountant records refunds as negative sales. **Test:** consistent results? Yes, the totals balance.

**Two wrong guesses (keep).** A baker tries 400 g of flour (too wet) and 500 g (too dry), then tries 450 g. **Test:** what do the guesses tell me? The answer lies between them.

**The commentary that checks (keep).** A team lead reviews a long-used script and finds one formula rounding wrongly. **Test:** has anyone checked why it works? Now someone has.

**Leave it for someone who can (keep).** A bug report ends: "I found where it fails but not why; leaving this open." **Test:** marked as unsolved? Yes, so the next person starts in the right place.

## 60. Brāhmasphuṭasiddhānta (Brahmagupta, 628)

Brahmagupta's treatise on astronomy contains the earliest known written rules for calculating with zero as a number, and for negative numbers, which he described as debts against fortunes. It is aimed fiction tested by prediction (its purpose was computing the positions of planets and eclipses) with a record that mostly landed. Its one famous miss, a rule for dividing zero by zero, shows exactly where a good rule stops working.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Give the new thing rules | Keep | Zero is treated as a number, with written rules: a number plus zero is itself; times zero is zero | A new concept becomes usable once its rules are stated | Have the rules for this new thing been written down? |
| Debts and fortunes | Keep | Negatives as debts, positives as fortunes; a debt times a debt is a fortune | Holds while the concrete model keeps fitting every operation | Does the concrete picture keep working for every operation? |
| The rule at the edge | Warn | He set zero divided by zero equal to zero, which later mathematics rejected | A rule extended to the one case where it breaks | Does the rule still hold at the edge case? |
| Build new solutions from old | Keep | His composition method combines known solutions of an equation to produce new ones | Holds when the combination rule is proven | Can I combine solutions I already have? |
| Checked by the sky | Keep | The mathematics serves predicting eclipses and planetary positions, which the sky then confirms or not | A method is tested by what it predicts | What does this predict, and did it happen? |

### Worked examples

**Give the new thing rules (keep).** A team adopts "story points" but never defines what one point means. **Test:** rules written down? No, so every estimate means something different.

**Debts and fortunes (keep).** A bank statement shows a refund cancelling a charge. **Test:** does the picture fit every operation? For adding and subtracting, yes.

**The rule at the edge (warn).** A spreadsheet's "growth rate" formula divides by last year's sales, and a new product had none. **Test:** does the rule hold at the edge? No: it breaks at zero.

**Build new solutions from old (keep).** A cook who knows a good sauce and a good marinade combines them into a glaze. **Test:** combining what I have? Yes.

**Checked by the sky (keep).** A weather model is scored each day against what actually happened. **Test:** did it happen? The scores say how often.

## 61. The Compendious Book on Calculation by Completion and Balancing (al-Khwārizmī, c. 820)

Al-Khwārizmī wrote a practical manual for solving equations, for use in inheritance, trade and land measurement. Its title gave us "algebra" (al-jabr, restoring) and his name gave us "algorithm". It is aimed fiction that landed: a general method, justified twice over, by procedure and by geometric diagram. Its blind spot is the answers its method wasn't built to see.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Restore and balance | Keep | Move a subtracted term to the other side (restoring), and cancel like terms on both sides (balancing) | Whatever is done to one side is done to the other | Did I do the same to both sides? |
| Reduce to a standard type | Keep | Every problem is reduced to one of six standard forms, each with its own procedure | Classify first, then solve | Which standard type is this problem? |
| Show it a second way | Keep | Each procedure is also justified by a geometric diagram (completing the square) | Two independent demonstrations agree | Can I show this result a second, independent way? |
| For a real problem | Keep | The method is built for inheritances, contracts and measurement | Holds when the method is tested on real cases | What real problem does this solve? |
| Answers the method can't see | Warn | Negative solutions weren't accepted, so some answers were invisible to the method | A method that rules out answers in advance | What answers does my method rule out before I start? |

### Worked examples

**Restore and balance (keep).** A household budget moves a forgotten bill to the expenses column and cancels the matching refund on both sides. **Test:** same to both sides? Yes, so it still balances.

**Reduce to a standard type (keep).** A help desk sorts every ticket into one of six categories before assigning it. **Test:** which type? Classify first; then the fix is routine.

**Show it a second way (keep).** An estimate of a room's paint needs is checked by area and by counting last year's tins. **Test:** independent agreement? Yes.

**For a real problem (keep).** A new pricing formula is tried on last month's actual orders. **Test:** what real problem? Those orders, and it fits.

**Answers the method can't see (warn).** A survey offers "satisfied" or "very satisfied" only. **Test:** what does the method rule out? Every unhappy customer.

## 62. On Formally Undecidable Propositions (Kurt Gödel, 1931)

Gödel showed that any consistent formal system strong enough for arithmetic contains true statements it cannot prove, and cannot prove its own consistency. He did it by encoding statements about the system as numbers inside the system, then building a sentence that says, in effect, "I am not provable here." It is proven mathematics, not aimed fiction, but it is among the most over-applied results in the list, so its conditions matter as much as its conclusion.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Let the system describe itself | Keep | Statements about the system are encoded as numbers within it | Self-reference built from inside, not added from outside | Can this system represent statements about itself? |
| Build the case it can't handle | Keep | Construct the specific sentence the system can neither prove nor refute | A concrete counterexample beats a general doubt | Can I build the exact case this method fails on? |
| True isn't the same as provable | Keep | Some true statements are unprovable within the system | Unprovable here doesn't mean false | Is "can't be proven here" being confused with "false"? |
| No system certifies itself | Keep | A consistent system of this kind can't prove its own consistency | Assurance has to come from outside | Who outside the system vouches for it? |
| Conditions first | Warn | The theorem is stretched to law, ethics, minds or the universe | It applies only to consistent formal systems that can express arithmetic | Does this system actually meet Gödel's conditions? |

### Worked examples

**Let the system describe itself (keep).** A company's policy manual includes a section on how the manual itself gets changed. **Test:** can it represent itself? Yes, so changes follow a rule.

**Build the case it can't handle (keep).** Instead of saying "the spam filter is unreliable", a tester writes one email it misclassifies every time. **Test:** the exact failing case? Built.

**True isn't the same as provable (keep).** A witness saw the accident but there's no camera footage. **Test:** unprovable confused with false? It may still be true.

**No system certifies itself (keep).** A software project runs only tests it wrote for itself. **Test:** who outside vouches? No one yet: get an independent audit.

**Conditions first (warn).** "Gödel proves we can never fully understand ourselves." **Test:** does a person meet the conditions of a formal system of arithmetic? Not shown, so the theorem doesn't settle it.

## 63. On Computable Numbers (Alan Turing, 1936)

Turing made the vague idea of "a mechanical procedure" precise by imagining a person calculating with pencil and paper, then stripping that down to a simple machine. He showed that one universal machine can imitate any other, and that some questions (like whether a given program will ever stop) have no general mechanical answer. It is proven mathematics, and its quiet lesson for Alice is where the machine came from: an abstraction of what a human does when computing.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Make the vague precise | Keep | Replace "a mechanical procedure" with an exact definition: a simple machine reading and writing symbols | Holds when the definition captures the intuitive idea | Can I state exactly what counts? |
| Start from what a person does | Keep | The machine is modelled on a human computer: limited states, symbols on paper, one step at a time | The machine was defined by abstracting a human | What does a person actually do here, step by step? |
| One machine imitates all | Keep | A universal machine, given another machine's description, can carry out what that machine does | Computation depends on the procedure, not the material | Does this depend on what it's made of, or only on the procedure? |
| No general method | Keep | No procedure can decide, for every program, whether it will stop | Some questions only have case-by-case answers | Is there a general method here, or only case-by-case answers? |
| Not everything is computable | Warn | Most real numbers can't be computed by any machine | Assuming every question yields to a procedure | Is this the kind of thing a procedure can reach? |

### Worked examples

**Make the vague precise (keep).** A team argues about whether a feature is "done". **Test:** stated exactly? "Done" becomes: tested, reviewed, deployed.

**Start from what a person does (keep).** Before automating invoice approval, an analyst sits with the clerk who does it by hand. **Test:** what's done step by step? Four checks no one had written down.

**One machine imitates all (keep).** The same recipe works in a gas oven or an electric one. **Test:** material or procedure? Procedure, with small adjustments.

**No general method (keep).** "Write a tool that tells us in advance whether any customer will ever cancel." **Test:** general method or case by case? Case by case.

**Not everything is computable (warn).** "Just build an algorithm for fairness." **Test:** can a procedure reach it? Parts of it, not the whole judgement.

## 64. Computing Machinery and Intelligence (Alan Turing, 1950)

Turing replaced "Can machines think?" with a question that could be tested, the imitation game, and then answered nine objections one by one. It is aimed fiction with a dated prediction: that by about 2000 an average interrogator would have no better than a 70% chance of telling machine from human after five minutes. Recent studies report current language models passing short versions of the game; whether passing shows thinking remains contested. It is the direct answer to Descartes (book 48), and the strongest Symmetry book in the list so far. It is also about the kind of system writing this section, which is a reason to hold its conclusions to the same bar as any other.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Swap in a testable question | Keep and warn | Replace an unanswerable question with one that can be tested | Warn: the test may not capture what we cared about in the original | Does the testable question still capture what we cared about? |
| The polite convention | Keep | If the only proof a machine thinks is to be the machine, the same holds for every other person; we extend the polite convention that everyone thinks | The same standard for every mind | Am I applying a standard here that I don't apply to other people? |
| Heads in the sand | Warn | "The consequences would be too dreadful" is offered as an objection | Disliking a conclusion isn't evidence against it | Am I rejecting this because it's false, or because I don't like where it leads? |
| "It only does what we tell it" | Keep | Lady Lovelace's objection, answered: machines surprise their makers often | Holds as a test against the record, not the slogan | Has it really never done something its makers didn't foresee? |
| "A machine will never..." | Warn | Lists of things machines can never do are drawn from the machines people have seen so far | Induction from current examples stated as a law | Is "never" based on what machines can do, or on the ones I've seen? |
| Teach the child machine | Keep | Instead of programming an adult mind, build a child-machine and educate it | Learning instead of specifying | Can this be learned, rather than written out in full? |

### Worked examples

**Swap in a testable question (keep and warn).** "Is this employee committed?" becomes "Does she meet her deadlines?" Warn: someone can meet deadlines and be job-hunting. **Test:** does the test capture what we cared about? Partly.

**The polite convention (keep).** A new colleague says she's tired; you can't check, and you believe her. **Test:** same standard for every mind? It's the question to ask when the speaker is a model.

**Heads in the sand (warn).** "If the bridge report is right, we'd have to close it for a year, so the report must be wrong." **Test:** false, or unwelcome? Unwelcome.

**"It only does what we tell it" (keep).** A chess program plays a move its programmers didn't understand until they analysed it. **Test:** never surprised its makers? It did.

**"A machine will never..." (warn).** In 1990: "A computer will never beat a world chess champion." **Test:** based on capability, or on the machines seen so far? The second; it happened in 1997.

**Teach the child machine (keep).** A spam filter that learns from what users mark beats a hand-written list of banned words. **Test:** learned rather than specified? Yes.

## 65. A Mathematical Theory of Communication (Claude Shannon, 1948)

Shannon defined information as the reduction of uncertainty, measured in bits, and proved how much can be sent reliably through a noisy channel. To do it he set meaning aside on purpose: the meaning of a message is irrelevant to the engineering problem of sending it. It is proven mathematics with an enormous record. It also contains, in its word-by-word statistical models of English, a distant ancestor of today's language models.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Set meaning aside, on purpose | Keep and warn | Information is measured without regard to meaning | Keep: makes the problem solvable. Warn: the measure mistaken for meaning itself | Am I measuring the signal, or what it means? |
| Information as surprise | Keep | A message carries information in proportion to how much uncertainty it removes | The expected tells you little; the surprising tells you more | How much did this actually reduce my uncertainty? |
| Redundancy catches errors | Keep | Language carries extra, predictable structure (English is roughly half redundant), which lets errors be detected and corrected | Holds when the redundancy is known to both ends | Is there enough redundancy here to catch a mistake? |
| The channel's limit | Keep | Every noisy channel has a capacity; below it, near-perfect transmission is possible, above it, not | Limits can be measured, not just felt | Am I trying to push more through than the channel can carry? |
| Model the source by its statistics | Keep and warn | Successively better statistical models of English produce text that looks more and more like English | Keep: statistics capture real structure. Warn: what a model captures isn't settled by how it was built | What does this model capture, and what would show it leaves something out? |

### Worked examples

**Set meaning aside, on purpose (keep and warn).** A phone network carries a love letter and a tax form at the same cost. Warn: "this report has more data, so it says more." **Test:** signal or meaning? The network measures signal; the report needs reading.

**Information as surprise (keep).** A weather app says "sunny" in a desert. **Test:** how much uncertainty removed? Very little.

**Redundancy catches errors (keep).** "Meet at teh station" is still understood. **Test:** enough redundancy? Yes, for one typo.

**The channel's limit (keep).** A manager sends forty urgent messages a day and wonders why the important one was missed. **Test:** more than the channel can carry? Yes.

**Model the source by its statistics (keep and warn).** "It's just predicting the next word, so it can't understand anything" versus "it writes fluently, so it must understand." **Test:** what would show it leaves something out? A test, not either slogan.

## 66. Dialogue Concerning the Two Chief World Systems (Galileo Galilei, 1632)

Galileo staged the argument over whether the Earth moves as a conversation among three characters: a Copernican, an Aristotelian named Simplicio, and an open-minded host. It is aimed fiction that largely landed, though its favourite proof, from the tides, was wrong. Its record also holds the clearest case in the list of a conclusion changed by force rather than evidence: in 1633 Galileo was made to recant.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Give the other side a voice | Keep | The opposing view is argued by a character in the dialogue, not just described | Holds when that character makes the other side's real case | Does the other view get its strongest case here? |
| The strawman's name | Warn | The defender of the old view is called Simplicio, and is handed arguments to lose | A character built to fail | Would the other side recognise their view in this character? |
| The ship's cabin | Keep | Inside a smoothly moving ship's cabin, no experiment shows that the ship is moving | Some facts can't be seen from inside | From inside, could I tell? |
| Look for yourself | Keep | Observation through the telescope is set against the authority of old texts | Holds when the instrument is checked too | Have I looked, or only read? |
| The favourite proof | Warn | Galileo's own centrepiece, that the tides prove the Earth moves, was wrong | The argument you love most may be your weakest | Is my favourite argument actually my best one? |
| Changed by force | Warn | Galileo was made to abjure under threat; the Earth kept moving | A conclusion withdrawn under pressure tells you about the pressure, not the conclusion | Did this conclusion change because of evidence, or because of force? |

### Worked examples

**Give the other side a voice (keep).** A debate club assigns each member to argue the side they disagree with. **Test:** does the other view get its strongest case? It has to, to win.

**The strawman's name (warn).** A company's internal memo presents competitors' customers as "people who don't care about quality". **Test:** would those customers recognise themselves? No.

**The ship's cabin (keep).** Employees inside a company can't easily see that its products have gone out of date. **Test:** from inside, could they tell? Not without outside comparison.

**Look for yourself (keep).** A buyer reads glowing reviews of a flat, then visits and finds the damp. **Test:** looked, or only read? Looked, and it changed the decision.

**The favourite proof (warn).** A founder's pitch leans on one dramatic statistic that turns out to be from a different market. **Test:** favourite or best? Favourite.

**Changed by force (warn).** An employee retracts a safety complaint the day after a meeting with HR. **Test:** evidence, or pressure? Ask what changed in the evidence.

## 67. Principia (Isaac Newton, 1687)

Newton derived the laws of motion and universal gravitation from observed motions, and set out four explicit rules of reasoning for doing it. It is aimed fiction that landed for over two centuries and was then bounded, not erased, by relativity. His second rule is the Symmetry Rule stated for nature: the same effects get the same causes, whether in Europe or America, in humans or in other animals.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| No more causes than needed | Keep | Admit no more causes than are true and enough to explain the appearances (Rule 1) | Holds when the simpler account really explains everything | Am I adding a cause the evidence doesn't need? |
| Same effects, same causes | Keep | To the same natural effects assign, as far as possible, the same causes (Rule 2) | Holds unless a real difference is shown | Am I giving the same effect a different cause depending on who shows it? |
| One law for heaven and earth | Keep | The same gravity pulls the apple and holds the moon | Holds until a difference is found | Does the same rule hold on both sides of the line we drew? |
| The law, not the mechanism | Keep | "I frame no hypotheses" about what gravity is; he states only what the evidence shows it does | Claim what's shown, hold the rest open | Am I claiming the mechanism, or only the law? |
| Good until contradicted | Keep | Conclusions drawn from phenomena hold until new phenomena correct them (Rule 4) | Provisional, but not vague | What new observation would correct this? |
| The fixed background | Warn | Absolute space and time were assumed as an unquestioned stage, later overturned | A background never examined because it was never in view | What's being treated as fixed background here? |

### Worked examples

**No more causes than needed (keep).** A car won't start: dead battery, or a conspiracy by the dealer? **Test:** a cause the evidence doesn't need? The second.

**Same effects, same causes (keep).** A manager praises one employee's late nights as "dedication" and calls another's "poor planning". **Test:** same effect, different cause by who did it? Yes.

**One law for heaven and earth (keep).** A school's attendance rule applied to students but not to teachers' children. **Test:** same rule both sides of the line? No.

**The law, not the mechanism (keep).** A doctor knows a drug lowers blood pressure in trials but not yet exactly how. **Test:** law or mechanism? Law: prescribe on the evidence, keep studying the how.

**Good until contradicted (keep).** A bakery's "best seller" list is updated every month from sales. **Test:** what would correct it? Next month's figures.

**The fixed background (warn).** A five-year plan assumes the office will always exist. **Test:** what's taken as fixed? The office, and remote work changed it.

## 68. Elements of Chemistry (Antoine Lavoisier, 1789)

Lavoisier rebuilt chemistry on careful weighing: in a closed vessel, nothing is created or lost, only rearranged. It is aimed fiction that landed, and it overturned the phlogiston theory partly by showing how that theory had to be rescued (metals gained weight when burned, and some defenders answered by giving phlogiston negative weight). Much of the laboratory record and illustration was the work of Marie-Anne Paulze Lavoisier, which the book's usual telling leaves out.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Weigh everything in and out | Keep | In a sealed vessel, the total mass before and after a reaction is the same | Holds only if nothing escapes uncounted | Did I weigh everything going in and coming out? |
| The theory that needed negative weight | Warn | Phlogiston survived only by being given strange properties after each failed prediction | Rescue by special pleading | Is this theory being kept alive by properties invented after the fact? |
| Names that say what it is | Keep | A new naming system in which a compound's name tells you what it's made of | Holds when the composition is known | Does the name tell you what the thing is? |
| Simple for now | Keep | An element is whatever hasn't yet been broken down, a definition that admits it may change | Provisional by design | Is this "basic" because it is, or because we can't break it down yet? |
| Who else did the work? | Keep | The laboratory notes, translations and illustrations were largely Marie-Anne Paulze Lavoisier's | Credit follows the work, not the name on the cover | Who else did the work here? |

### Worked examples

**Weigh everything in and out (keep).** A shop's stock count is short, until someone counts the returns bin. **Test:** everything in and out? Not until the bin.

**The theory that needed negative weight (warn).** A diet "works" for everyone; those who gained weight "had the wrong mindset". **Test:** kept alive by invented properties? Yes.

**Names that say what it is (keep).** A shared drive's files renamed from "final\_v3\_REAL" to "2026-11 budget, approved". **Test:** does the name say what it is? Now it does.

**Simple for now (keep).** A support team treats "login fails" as one problem, until logs split it into three. **Test:** basic, or not yet broken down? Not yet broken down.

**Who else did the work? (keep).** A paper's acknowledgements thank "the lab" while one technician ran every sample. **Test:** who else did the work? Name her.

## 69. The Chemical History of a Candle (Michael Faraday, lectures 1848 and 1860)

Faraday, largely self-taught and one of the greatest experimenters in history, gave Christmas lectures for young people that explained chemistry through a single burning candle. It is aimed fiction offered in the plainest form: every claim is demonstrated in front of the audience. Its lesson for Alice is that the ordinary is the best laboratory, and that simple can still be true.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Start from the ordinary | Keep | One candle opens onto combustion, air, water and breathing | Holds when the everyday case really contains the principle | What everyday thing shows this? |
| Ask why the obvious happens | Keep | Why does the flame point up? Why does a cup of liquid wax form under it? | The familiar hides the questions | Why does the obvious thing happen? |
| Show, don't tell | Keep | Every claim is demonstrated in front of the audience | Holds when the demonstration can be repeated | Can I show this, not just say it? |
| Follow where it went | Keep | He captures the water and the gas the candle produces | Nothing just disappears | Where did it go? |
| Simple and still true | Keep | Explained for children, without saying anything false | Simplify without lying | Is this simple and still true? |

### Worked examples

**Start from the ordinary (keep).** A teacher explains interest rates using a jar of borrowed sugar. **Test:** does the everyday case contain the principle? Yes: borrow, owe more.

**Ask why the obvious happens (keep).** Why does bread go stale faster in the fridge? **Test:** why the obvious? Asking it leads to how starch behaves in the cold.

**Show, don't tell (keep).** Instead of saying the new chair is sturdier, a salesperson stands on it. **Test:** shown? Yes.

**Follow where it went (keep).** A monthly budget is short; a bank download shows a forgotten subscription. **Test:** where did it go? Found.

**Simple and still true (keep).** "Your heart is a pump that pushes blood round your body." **Test:** simple and true? Yes; the details come later.

## 70. A Treatise on Electricity and Magnetism (James Clerk Maxwell, 1873)

Maxwell took Faraday's pictures of "lines of force" seriously and turned them into mathematics, showing that electricity, magnetism and light are one phenomenon. It is aimed fiction that landed spectacularly: it predicted electromagnetic waves before anyone had made one, and Hertz produced them in the 1880s. It also carried an assumption, the ether, that later had to be dropped.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Look at what's between | Keep | Attend to the field in the space between objects, not just the objects | Holds when the in-between has measurable effects | What's happening in the space between? |
| Turn the picture into equations | Keep | Faraday's intuitive diagrams become exact mathematics, with credit to Faraday | Holds when the intuition survives being made precise | Can this intuition be stated exactly? |
| Two things that are one | Keep | Electricity, magnetism and light turn out to be one phenomenon | Holds when one account explains both, with no loss | Are these two things really one? |
| Predict what no one has seen | Keep | The equations implied waves travelling at the speed of light, found later | A theory earns most from a risky prediction that comes true | What does this predict that nobody has observed yet? |
| Use a model, then let it go | Keep | Earlier, he pictured the field as tiny spinning vortices; the mathematics outlived the picture | Scaffolding, like Wittgenstein's ladder | Is this model still needed, or was it scaffolding? |
| The medium nobody found | Warn | The waves were assumed to travel through an ether, which experiments later failed to find | A required background that was never shown | Has this assumed medium ever actually been detected? |

### Worked examples

**Look at what's between (keep).** Two good teams work badly together; the problem is the handover between them. **Test:** what's happening in between? The handover.

**Turn the picture into equations (keep).** A shop owner's feeling that "rainy days are slow" becomes a sales-versus-weather chart. **Test:** stated exactly? Yes, and it holds for weekdays only.

**Two things that are one (keep).** Two help-desk complaints, "slow login" and "timeouts", turn out to have one cause. **Test:** really one? Fix the cause and both go.

**Predict what no one has seen (keep).** A model of a river says a particular field will flood at a certain height; it does, the next spring. **Test:** risky prediction that came true? Yes.

**Use a model, then let it go (keep).** A new manager uses a borrowed org chart to start, then redraws it once she knows the team. **Test:** still needed? No.

**The medium nobody found (warn).** "Company culture" is blamed for every problem, but no one can say what it is or show it. **Test:** ever detected? Not yet: define it, or drop it.

## 71. Relativity: The Special and General Theory (Albert Einstein, 1916)

Einstein wrote this account of relativity for general readers, built from trains, embankments, clocks and a closed chest pulled through space. It is aimed fiction that landed through risky predictions (the bending of starlight, confirmed in 1919) and contains Newton's physics as a special case rather than throwing it out. Its key move is to ask what a word like "simultaneous" means in practice, then follow the answer wherever it goes.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Say how you'd measure it | Keep | "At the same time" for distant events means nothing until you say how you'd check it, with light signals | A concept gets its meaning from a procedure | How would I actually measure this? |
| Depends on where you stand | Keep | Two events simultaneous for someone on the embankment aren't for someone on the train | Some properties belong to a relation, not to the thing alone | Is this absolute, or relative to the observer? |
| Can't tell from inside | Keep | In a closed chest, you can't tell being pulled upward from standing in gravity | If no test can tell two things apart, treat them alike | Is there any test that could tell these apart? |
| Follow the constant | Keep | If light's speed is the same for every observer, accept what follows, however strange | Holds when the constant itself is well tested | What follows if this really holds everywhere? |
| The old theory as a special case | Keep | Newton's mechanics reappears as the low-speed limit of the new theory | A new theory keeps what the old one got right | Does the new account reduce to the old one where the old one worked? |
| Risk a prediction | Keep | Starlight should bend by a specific amount near the Sun; the 1919 eclipse tested it | The more specific the prediction, the more the result counts | What specific result could prove this wrong? |

### Worked examples

**Say how you'd measure it (keep).** A team argues whether the app is "fast". **Test:** how would we measure it? Load time on a mid-range phone, under two seconds.

**Depends on where you stand (keep).** A drive feels short to the driver and endless to the child in the back. **Test:** absolute or relative? Relative: "long" depends on who's asked.

**Can't tell from inside (keep).** Two job applicants have identical work samples, one with a famous university on the CV. **Test:** any test that tells them apart on the work? No, so judge them alike.

**Follow the constant (keep).** A company commits to "no customer data leaves the country" and finds it must change three suppliers. **Test:** what follows if it really holds? Those changes.

**The old theory as a special case (keep).** A new pricing model gives the same prices as the old one for small orders and only differs for bulk. **Test:** reduces to the old where the old worked? Yes.

**Risk a prediction (keep).** "This ad will raise sign-ups by 10% in two weeks." **Test:** what result would prove it wrong? Less than 10%, so it's checkable.

## 72. What Is Life? (Erwin Schrödinger, 1944)

A physicist stepped openly outside his field to ask how living things keep their order and pass on heredity, and reasoned that the hereditary material must be an "aperiodic crystal" carrying a code. It is aimed fiction that landed: Watson and Crick both credited it as an influence on the search that found DNA's structure. It also models an honest habit: Schrödinger marks where the physics stops and his personal philosophy begins.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The outsider's question | Keep and warn | A physicist asks biology the questions its insiders had stopped asking | Keep: fresh eyes. Warn: authority from one field doesn't transfer to another | What does my outsider's view see, and where am I most likely wrong? |
| Work out what it must be | Keep | To carry so much information so stably, heredity must sit in an aperiodic crystal: a code-script | Infer structure from what the thing has to do | What must this be like to do what it does? |
| Order paid for with disorder | Keep | Living things keep their order by drawing order from their surroundings and giving back disorder | No system stays ordered for free | Where does this system get its order, and where does the disorder go? |
| Big enough to be reliable | Keep | Organisms are huge compared with atoms because statistical laws are only reliable with very large numbers | Reliability needs numbers | Is the sample big enough for this law to be reliable? |
| Mark where the evidence stops | Keep | His epilogue on free will and consciousness is labelled as his personal view | Speculation kept apart from the science | Have I marked where the evidence stops and my view begins? |

### Worked examples

**The outsider's question (keep and warn).** A nurse asks why a hospital's software makes her re-enter the same data three times. Warn: she then redesigns the database alone. **Test:** what does she see, and where's she likely wrong? The problem, yes; the architecture, maybe not.

**Work out what it must be (keep).** A lock opens with any of four keys; it must have a master pin system. **Test:** what must it be like? Inferred, then confirmed by the locksmith.

**Order paid for with disorder (keep).** A tidy office stays tidy because someone empties the bins every night. **Test:** where does the disorder go? The bins.

**Big enough to be reliable (keep).** A restaurant judges a new dish on three customers' reactions. **Test:** big enough? No.

**Mark where the evidence stops (keep).** A report ends: "The data shows X. My own view, beyond the data, is Y." **Test:** marked? Yes.

## 73. Flatland (Edwin A. Abbott, 1884)

Flatland is free fiction with a double aim: a lesson in dimensions and a satire of Victorian hierarchy. A Square living in a two-dimensional world is visited by a Sphere, learns that a third dimension exists, and is imprisoned for saying so. Its sharpest reasoning move is the chain of refusals: each being mocks the one below for not imagining more, then refuses what lies above.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Beyond my own dimension | Keep | A Square can't picture height until a Sphere passes through his plane as a growing and shrinking circle | Some realities show up only as strange slices of themselves | What would someone with a wider view see here that I can't? |
| The chain of refusals | Warn | The Square mocks the King of Lineland for denying two dimensions, then denies three; the Sphere, in turn, refuses four | Making the same refusal you criticise in others | Am I making the same refusal I mock in someone else? |
| Follow the pattern past sight | Keep | Point, line, square, cube: the pattern suggests what comes next even where it can't be seen | Holds while the pattern really continues | Does the pattern continue, and what does it predict? |
| Rank by shape | Warn | Status depends on how many sides you were born with; women are lines; irregular figures are destroyed | Rank fixed by form, not conduct | Is rank here based on what someone does, or what shape they were born? |
| Silence the messenger | Warn | The Square is imprisoned for preaching the third dimension | A claim answered by removing the speaker | Is this claim being answered, or its speaker silenced? |

### Worked examples

**Beyond my own dimension (keep).** A shop owner sees sales fall each Tuesday; the delivery company sees its route change that day. **Test:** what does the wider view see? The cause.

**The chain of refusals (warn).** A senior engineer laughs at managers who "don't get" code, then dismisses designers who say his interface is confusing. **Test:** the same refusal? Yes.

**Follow the pattern past sight (keep).** Costs doubled in each of the last three years. **Test:** does the pattern continue? Plan for it, and check whether the cause is still there.

**Rank by shape (warn).** A firm promotes only people from three universities. **Test:** what someone does, or what they were born into? The second.

**Silence the messenger (warn).** An employee who reports a flaw in the product is moved to another team. **Test:** claim answered, or speaker silenced? Silenced.

## 74. Hippocratic Corpus, including the Oath (Greek, c. 5th–4th century BCE)

The Hippocratic writings, by many authors, treated disease as having natural causes, and built medicine on careful observation and recorded cases. They are aimed fiction with a split record: the method of observing and recording landed, while the theory of four humours survived for two thousand years without being decisively tested, and turned out wrong. The Oath's ethics, including confidentiality, are still recognisable in medicine today.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Natural causes, not "sacred" | Keep | Epilepsy, called the "sacred disease", has natural causes like any other; the label hides ignorance (On the Sacred Disease) | Holds as a prompt to look for causes | Is "mysterious" covering for "not yet explained"? |
| Record every case | Keep | Case histories follow patients day by day, including those who died | The failures are part of the record | Did I record what happened, including what went wrong? |
| Help, or at least do no harm | Keep | The physician's aim is to help, or at least not to harm (Epidemics I) | Holds when the risk of acting is weighed against not acting | If this doesn't help, could it hurt? |
| Earn trust by prognosis | Keep | A physician is trusted by correctly predicting the course of an illness | Prediction tested against what happens | Did my prediction come true? |
| The art is long | Keep | Life is short, the art long, opportunity fleeting, experience misleading, judgement difficult (Aphorisms 1.1) | Humility built into the first line | Which of these is catching me out right now? |
| Inherited, not tested | Warn | The four humours were handed down for centuries without decisive tests | Reputation standing in for a record | Has this been tested, or only inherited? |
| Keep what you're told | Keep | The Oath binds the physician to keep secret what is learned in treating patients | Confidentiality as the ground of trust | Would the person who told me this expect me to keep it? |

### Worked examples

**Natural causes, not "sacred" (keep).** A server crashes every Friday night, and the team calls it "cursed". **Test:** covering for not yet explained? Yes: it's the weekly backup.

**Record every case (keep).** A teacher logs which lessons worked and which fell flat. **Test:** including what went wrong? Yes, so next year improves.

**Help, or at least do no harm (keep).** A friend offers to fix a leaking pipe with no tools. **Test:** could it hurt if it doesn't help? Yes: flooding.

**Earn trust by prognosis (keep).** A mechanic says the brakes have about a month left; they do. **Test:** prediction came true? Yes, and now he's trusted.

**The art is long (keep).** A new manager is sure after one week that she knows the team. **Test:** which is catching her out? Experience misleading.

**Inherited, not tested (warn).** "We've always done onboarding this way." **Test:** tested, or inherited? Inherited.

**Keep what you're told (keep).** A colleague mentions she's job-hunting; you don't repeat it. **Test:** would she expect you to keep it? Yes.

## 75. Huangdi Neijing (The Yellow Emperor's Inner Classic, China, compiled c. 2nd–1st century BCE)

The Neijing, written as dialogues between the Yellow Emperor and his physicians, is the foundational text of traditional Chinese medicine and is still studied and used. It reads health as balance within the body and between the body and its seasons and surroundings. Its medical claims are aimed fiction with a contested record: some practices built on it have been tested in modern trials with mixed results, and its central concepts (qi, the channels) have no agreed counterpart in mainstream anatomy; supporters answer that it models how the body functions, not its structure. This section reads its reasoning and takes no side beyond the evidence.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Treat before it's ill | Keep | The best physician treats what isn't yet illness, as one digs a well before thirst | Holds when early signs can be read | What can be done before this breaks? |
| Health as balance | Keep and warn | Illness is read as imbalance, health as balance restored | Keep as a systems view. Warn: if every result can be read as some imbalance, nothing could count against it | What result would count against this explanation? |
| Fit the season | Keep | How to live, eat and rest changes with the seasons and surroundings | Advice that ignores conditions misfires | Does this advice change with the conditions? |
| More than one kind of evidence | Keep | Diagnosis combines looking, listening, asking and feeling the pulse | Several independent signs beat one | Have I gathered more than one kind of evidence? |
| Correspondence by analogy | Warn | Organs, colours, tastes, emotions and seasons are linked through the five phases | A link made by resemblance, not by test | Is this link tested, or a correspondence by analogy? |

### Worked examples

**Treat before it's ill (keep).** A building manager replaces a pump showing early vibration before it fails in winter. **Test:** what can be done before it breaks? This.

**Health as balance (keep and warn).** A coach says a team's slump is "imbalance"; whatever happens next is called more imbalance. **Test:** what would count against it? Nothing named, so name one.

**Fit the season (keep).** A shop keeps the same opening hours year-round in a tourist town. **Test:** does the advice change with conditions? It should.

**More than one kind of evidence (keep).** A hiring panel uses an interview, a work sample and references. **Test:** more than one kind? Three.

**Correspondence by analogy (warn).** "Red cars get more speeding tickets because red is an aggressive colour." **Test:** tested, or analogy? Analogy, until the data's checked.

## 76. Sushruta Samhita (India, compiled over centuries, roughly 1st millennium BCE to early CE)

The Sushruta Samhita is a classic of Ayurvedic medicine and one of the oldest surgical texts in the world, describing hundreds of instruments and procedures, including reconstruction of the nose. Its surgical method (practise first, study the body directly, join theory to practice) has a strong record. Its theoretical framework, the Ayurvedic doshas, is aimed fiction with a contested record, and is read here for its reasoning only. The split itself is a path: the success of a technique doesn't vouch for the theory around it.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Practise on what can't be harmed | Keep | Students practise incisions on gourds and fruit, leather bags and other models before touching a patient | Holds when the model resembles the real task | Have I practised this on something that can't be hurt? |
| See the body itself | Keep | Anatomy is learned by examining a body directly, not only from teaching | Observation over recitation | Did I look at the thing itself? |
| Two wings | Keep | A physician with only theory, or only practice, is like a bird with one wing (as traditionally quoted) | Both are needed to fly | Do I have both the theory and the practice? |
| Repair from what's nearby | Keep | A lost nose is rebuilt from a flap of the patient's own nearby skin | Use what's already there | What resource close at hand could repair this? |
| The technique vouches for the theory | Warn | Surgical success is taken as proof of the theory the text sets it in | One part's record lent to another part | Is the success of the method being used to vouch for the theory around it? |

### Worked examples

**Practise on what can't be harmed (keep).** A new pilot logs hours in a simulator before carrying passengers. **Test:** practised on what can't be hurt? Yes.

**See the body itself (keep).** A mechanic opens the engine instead of guessing from the manual. **Test:** looked at the thing itself? Yes, and the manual was for a different year.

**Two wings (keep).** A graduate who knows every coding theory has never shipped a product; a self-taught builder can't explain why her code works. **Test:** both? Each has one wing: pair them.

**Repair from what's nearby (keep).** A team short of a designer borrows one from the support team, who knows the users best. **Test:** what's close at hand? Support.

**The technique vouches for the theory (warn).** A coach's drills clearly improve fitness, so his claims about diet are believed too. **Test:** is the drills' success vouching for the diet theory? Yes: test the diet separately.

## 77. The Canon of Medicine (Ibn Sina / Avicenna, 1025)

Ibn Sina gathered Greek, Persian and Indian medicine into one systematic encyclopedia, and set out explicit conditions for testing whether a drug works: a pure drug, a simple disease, consistent results across many cases, and testing in humans rather than only in animals. It is aimed fiction with a split record: those testing rules read like an early outline of a clinical trial, while much of the inherited theory did not survive. The Canon was then taught as an authority for about six centuries, which is its warn path.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Pure drug, simple disease | Keep | Test a drug free of other substances, on a disease that isn't mixed with others | One cause, one condition, so the effect can be read | Is this test isolated enough to show what caused the effect? |
| Consistent across many cases | Keep | A drug's effect counts only if it appears consistently across many cases, not once | One success could be chance | Has it worked consistently, across many cases? |
| Test in the ones who'll use it | Keep | A drug's effect on a lion or horse may not show its effect on a person | A stand-in isn't the real subject | Does the test subject match who will actually use it? |
| Mind and body | Keep | Some bodily illness has its cause in what the person is going through, as in a famous story of diagnosing lovesickness by the pulse | Holds alongside physical causes, not instead of them | Could the cause be in what this person is going through? |
| The textbook as authority | Warn | The Canon became the standard text for centuries, taught more than tested | A synthesis taken as final | Is this still being tested, or only taught? |

### Worked examples

**Pure drug, simple disease (keep).** A new sleep app is tried by people who also started exercising and quit coffee that month. **Test:** isolated enough? No.

**Consistent across many cases (keep).** A sales script worked brilliantly once. **Test:** across many cases? Try it on fifty calls.

**Test in the ones who'll use it (keep).** A website tested only by the developers who built it. **Test:** subject matches the users? No.

**Mind and body (keep).** A student gets stomach aches every Sunday night before school. **Test:** could the cause be what she's going through? Ask about school, and check her stomach too.

**The textbook as authority (warn).** A company training manual from 2009 is still taught to every new hire. **Test:** still tested, or only taught? Only taught.

## 78. On the Fabric of the Human Body (Andreas Vesalius, 1543)

Vesalius dissected human bodies himself, instead of reading from Galen while an assistant cut, and found that much of Galen's anatomy had come from animals, not people. It is aimed fiction that landed, and set out its evidence in detailed illustrations anyone could check against a body. It is the direct partner of Ibn Sina's warning (book 77): Galen's errors came from treating a stand-in as the real subject.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The body, not the book | Keep | Where the body and the authority disagree, the body wins | Holds when the observation is careful and repeatable | Does the thing itself agree with what the book says? |
| Observed in a stand-in | Warn | Galen's human anatomy was partly drawn from apes and other animals | A finding about a stand-in carried over to the real case | Was this observed in the thing itself, or in a stand-in? |
| The one who claims should look | Keep | The professor dissects himself, instead of reading aloud while someone else cuts | Holds when the observer can also explain | Did the person stating this look for themselves? |
| Show it so others can check | Keep | Large, precise illustrations let readers compare the book with a body | Evidence laid out for checking | Can others see what I saw? |
| What the reformer kept | Warn | Vesalius himself kept some of Galen's errors in his first edition | Even the corrector inherits unchecked parts | What am I still taking from the old source without checking? |

### Worked examples

**The body, not the book (keep).** The style guide says customers prefer formal emails; the reply rates say casual ones work better. **Test:** does the thing agree with the book? No: follow the reply rates.

**Observed in a stand-in (warn).** A study of college students is used to predict how retirees will vote. **Test:** observed in the thing itself? No, in a stand-in.

**The one who claims should look (keep).** A manager reporting on a factory's safety visits the floor instead of reading the summary. **Test:** looked for himself? Yes.

**Show it so others can check (keep).** A report includes its raw data as an appendix. **Test:** can others see what I saw? Yes.

**What the reformer kept (warn).** A new manager rewrites the team's process but keeps the old deadline nobody remembers the reason for. **Test:** what's still unchecked? That deadline.

## 79. On the Origin of Species (Charles Darwin, 1859)

Darwin called the book "one long argument": evidence from breeding, geography, fossils and embryology, all pointing to descent with modification by natural selection. This is the book Daniel used to make the point that every theory is fiction: imagination aimed at the world. Its record since is one of the strongest in the list, and by the library's own rule that changes its standing as tested, not its status as aimed fiction. Its best habit is facing its own difficulties in print and naming what would break it.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| One long argument | Keep | Independent lines of evidence (breeding, distribution, fossils, embryos) point to the same conclusion | Holds when the lines really are independent | Do independent lines of evidence point the same way? |
| A smaller version you can watch | Keep | Breeders' selection shows, at small scale and in living memory, what selection can do | Reason from an observed process to a larger unobserved one | Is there a smaller, observable version of this process? |
| Face your own difficulties | Keep | A whole chapter takes on the strongest objections, from the eye to missing transitional forms | Holds when the objections are stated at full strength | Have I written down the strongest objections to my own view? |
| Say what would break it | Keep | If any complex organ could be shown that couldn't have formed by many slight steps, the theory would break down | A theory that names its falsifier | What would make me drop this? |
| The gaps explained | Keep and warn | Missing fossils are explained by an imperfect record | Keep when later finds could check the explanation, as they did. Warn when a gap's explanation can never be checked | Is the missing evidence explained in a way later finds could test? |
