| The parallel discoverer | Keep | Wallace reached the same idea independently; their papers were presented together in 1858 | Credit the one who got there too | Who else got there? |

### Worked examples

**One long argument (keep).** A product's churn data, support tickets and exit surveys all point to slow onboarding. **Test:** independent lines agree? Yes.

**A smaller version you can watch (keep).** Before a citywide recycling scheme, one street tries it for three months. **Test:** a smaller, observable version? That street.

**Face your own difficulties (keep).** A proposal ends with "Three reasons this might fail" and answers each. **Test:** strongest objections written down? Yes.

**Say what would break it (keep).** "If sales don't rise in two quarters, we drop the new line." **Test:** what would make us drop it? Stated in advance.

**The gaps explained (keep and warn).** "We've no complaints from the night shift because they're too busy to file them": checkable by asking. Warn: "no evidence of the problem because it's hidden too well." **Test:** could later finds test it? The first, not the second.

**The parallel discoverer (keep).** Two teams independently find the same bug; the fix notes credit both. **Test:** who else got there? Named.

## 80. Experiments on Plant Hybridization (Gregor Mendel, 1866)

Mendel, a friar, crossed thousands of pea plants, counted how their traits appeared in each generation, and found the ratios that became the basis of genetics. It is aimed fiction that landed, but only after being largely ignored for 34 years, until three researchers rediscovered it in 1900. Its record also carries two later questions worth teaching: whether his numbers fit his predictions too well (raised by the statistician R. A. Fisher in 1936 and still debated), and why his second plant, hawkweed, didn't follow the pattern.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A case simple enough to see | Keep | Peas with clear either-or traits, easy to cross and control | Holds when the simple case shares the structure of the general one | Have I chosen a case simple enough to see the pattern? |
| Count, don't just look | Keep | Thousands of plants counted, giving ratios like 3 to 1 | Patterns appear in numbers that impressions miss | Did I count, or only look? |
| Predict, then cross again | Keep | The ratios predicted the results of further crosses, which were then made | A pattern becomes a law when it predicts | What does this pattern predict for the next test? |
| Ignored for where it came from | Warn | Published in a local society's journal by a friar, the work went largely unread for decades | Judged by its source, not its content | Is this being ignored because it's wrong, or because of where it came from? |
| Too good to be true? | Warn | Fisher argued Mendel's results were closer to prediction than chance allows; others have since disputed that | Contested: name both sides | Are these results closer to prediction than chance would allow? |
| The case that didn't fit | Keep | Hawkweed didn't follow the pea ratios; it was later found to reproduce largely without fertilisation | A failed second case can reveal a different mechanism, not refute the first | Does this failure refute the rule, or reveal a different mechanism? |

### Worked examples

**A case simple enough to see (keep).** To learn why customers cancel, an analyst starts with one product and one region. **Test:** simple enough? Yes, then widen.

**Count, don't just look (keep).** A teacher feels quiet students do worse; the gradebook shows no difference. **Test:** counted, or only looked? Counted.

**Predict, then cross again (keep).** A new layout raised clicks on one page; the team predicts the same on three others and tests. **Test:** what does it predict? Three results, checked.

**Ignored for where it came from (warn).** A junior employee's cost-saving idea is ignored until a consultant says the same thing. **Test:** wrong, or the wrong source? The wrong source.

**Too good to be true? (warn).** A salesman's monthly figures hit target exactly, every month for two years. **Test:** closer than chance would allow? Worth checking.

**The case that didn't fit (keep).** A recipe works in every oven except a friend's at altitude. **Test:** refuted, or a different mechanism? Altitude: a different mechanism.

## 81. Mutual Aid: A Factor of Evolution (Peter Kropotkin, 1902)

Kropotkin answered the popular reading of Darwin as a war of all against all, arguing from his observations in Siberia that cooperation is also a major force in evolution. It is aimed fiction whose core has largely landed (cooperation is now a standard subject in evolutionary biology, through kin selection and reciprocal altruism), though many of his examples have dated. He was also an anarchist, and the book supports his politics; this section reads its biological reasoning and takes no side on the politics.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| What the one-sided story leaves out | Keep | The "struggle for existence" reading left out cooperation within and between species | Holds when the missing part is real and measurable | What does the one-sided account leave out? |
| Where you look shapes what you see | Keep | Observers in crowded tropics saw competition; Kropotkin in harsh Siberia saw animals cooperating against the climate | Conclusions carry the place they were drawn from | Would the conclusion change if I looked somewhere else? |
| Both, not either | Keep | He didn't deny struggle; he added mutual aid as a second factor | Many disputes are false choices | Is this really either-or, or both? |
| Examples chosen to fit | Warn | A long run of supporting cases, from animals to medieval guilds, with few counter-examples | A pile of fitting cases isn't a test | Are the counter-examples included? |
| Science and politics checked separately | Keep | The biology argued for a political view, as the rival reading did for its own | Judge each claim on its own evidence | Am I judging the science by whether I like the politics, or the other way round? |

### Worked examples

**What the one-sided story leaves out (keep).** A report on a startup's success credits only the founder. **Test:** what's left out? The early team and the first big customer.

**Where you look shapes what you see (keep).** A manager who only visits the office on Fridays thinks remote work is going badly; Fridays are the quietest day. **Test:** would the conclusion change elsewhere? Yes.

**Both, not either (keep).** "Is the slow site the code or the server?" **Test:** either-or? Both, as it turns out.

**Examples chosen to fit (warn).** A blog lists ten famous people who wake at 5 a.m. as proof that early rising causes success. **Test:** counter-examples included? None.

**Science and politics checked separately (keep).** A study on minimum wages is dismissed by people who dislike the authors' party. **Test:** judging the science by the politics? Yes; read the method.

## 82. Notes on Nursing (Florence Nightingale, 1859)

Nightingale wrote a practical guide for anyone caring for the sick at home, built on one skill above all: observation. It is aimed fiction with a split record. Her practices (fresh air, light, cleanliness, careful watching) saved lives, while her theory of why they worked, that disease spread through bad air (miasma), was later overturned by germ theory. Elsewhere she pioneered statistical charts to force officials to see the death rates; here, the lesson is to watch closely and ask honestly.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Teach what to observe | Keep | The most important practical lesson for a nurse is what to observe, and how | Watching is a skill, not a gift | What exactly should I be watching for here? |
| Fix the surroundings | Keep | Air, light, warmth, quiet and cleanliness are part of the treatment | Holds when conditions really affect the outcome | What in the surroundings is making this worse? |
| Don't lead the patient | Keep | "Did you sleep well?" invites "yes"; ask how many hours, and when they woke | A question that suggests its answer gathers nothing | Did my question suggest its own answer? |
| No false comfort | Keep | Cheerful hopes offered to the sick, without grounds, burden them more than they help | Reassurance must be true to help | Is this reassurance actually true? |
| Right practice, wrong theory | Warn | Her methods worked, but the miasma theory she gave for them was wrong | A practice's success doesn't confirm the theory behind it | Is the practice's success being taken as proof of its theory? |

### Worked examples

**Teach what to observe (keep).** A new parent is told to note feeds, nappies and sleep, not just "how the baby seems". **Test:** what to watch for? Three things, written down.

**Fix the surroundings (keep).** A team's error rate falls after the office's broken lighting is repaired. **Test:** what in the surroundings made it worse? The lighting.

**Don't lead the patient (keep).** A survey asks, "How much did you enjoy our excellent service?" **Test:** did the question suggest its answer? Yes. Ask, "How was the service?"

**No false comfort (keep).** "I'm sure the results will be fine", said with no knowledge of the results. **Test:** true? No; say "I'll be with you when they come."

**Right practice, wrong theory (warn).** A shop owner swears his lucky shirt increases sales; it's the day he works hardest. **Test:** practice's success taken as proof of the theory? Yes: it's the effort, not the shirt.

## 83. The Selfish Gene (Richard Dawkins, 1976)

Dawkins popularised the gene's-eye view of evolution: ask what a trait does for the genes that build it. It is aimed fiction with a strong record in biology and a famous problem of reception: the title's metaphor was widely read as a claim that genes have motives, or that people should be selfish, which Dawkins rejects. Read beside Kropotkin (book 81), it shows how cooperation can arise from gene-level selection, and it coined the word "meme".

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Change the vantage point | Keep | Look at a behaviour from the gene's point of view, not the organism's | Some puzzles dissolve at a different level | What does this look like from another level? |
| The metaphor doing the arguing | Warn | "Selfish" is a metaphor; genes have no intentions, yet the word leads readers to conclusions | A vivid word carrying a claim it can't support | Is the metaphor doing work the evidence should be doing? |
| Cooperation from selection | Keep | Kin selection and reciprocal help explain how cooperation evolves from gene-level selection | Holds when the cooperation is measured, not assumed | Does the explanation account for both the competing and the cooperating? |
| Survives an invader | Keep | A strategy is stable if a population using it can't be overrun by a newcomer using another (an evolutionarily stable strategy) | Holds when the payoffs are known | Would this strategy survive if someone tried a different one? |
| Ideas as replicators | Keep and warn | Ideas spread and compete like genes ("memes") | Warn: the analogy stretched past the points where it holds | Does the analogy hold on the points that matter here? |
| Explaining isn't excusing | Keep | We alone can rebel against our replicators: explaining why we tend to do something doesn't make it right | Is and ought kept apart | Does explaining why we do this justify doing it? |

### Worked examples

**Change the vantage point (keep).** A product feature users hate keeps surviving; from the advertising team's view, it earns money. **Test:** what does it look like from another level? Explained.

**The metaphor doing the arguing (warn).** "The market wants lower wages." **Test:** is the metaphor doing the work? Yes: markets don't want; name who does.

**Cooperation from selection (keep).** Competing cafés on one street share a delivery driver because each would lose without it. **Test:** competing and cooperating both explained? Yes.

**Survives an invader (keep).** A team where everyone shares credit; one new hire starts taking credit for others' work. **Test:** does the strategy survive the invader? Only if the team notices and stops it.

**Ideas as replicators (keep and warn).** A catchy rumour spreads faster than its correction. Warn: "ideas have genes." **Test:** does the analogy hold here? On spreading, yes; on biology, no.

**Explaining isn't excusing (keep).** "Humans evolved to favour their own group, so it's fine to hire only friends." **Test:** does the explanation justify it? No.

## 84. Silent Spring (Rachel Carson, 1962)

Carson, a marine biologist, traced how widely sprayed pesticides such as DDT moved through soil, water and food chains, building up in birds, fish and people. It is aimed fiction that opens with an imagined town ("A Fable for Tomorrow") and then argues from evidence, and much of its core (accumulation up the food chain, harm to non-target species, insect resistance) has held. Its legacy is debated: critics argue that restrictions on DDT hampered malaria control; defenders reply that Carson argued against indiscriminate use, not all use, and warned that overuse was breeding resistant mosquitoes. Both sides are named here.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Follow the chain | Keep | A chemical sprayed on fields moves into water, insects, fish and birds, concentrating at each step | Holds when each link is measured | Where does it go next, and does it build up? |
| What else it hits | Keep | Sprays meant for one insect kill birds, fish and the insects' own predators | Count the side effects, not just the target | What else does this hit besides the target? |
| The fix that breeds its failure | Keep | Repeated spraying selects for resistant insects, so the spray stops working | A solution can create the conditions for its own failure | Does this solution breed its own failure? |
| Who must prove it's safe? | Keep | Chemicals were spread widely before their effects were known | Decide where the burden of proof sits before use, not after | Who has to show this is safe, and before what? |
| The story before the evidence | Keep and warn | The book opens with an imagined poisoned town, then gives the evidence | Keep: a story to illustrate. Warn: the story standing in for the evidence | Is the story illustrating the evidence, or replacing it? |
| What she actually said | Keep | Critics and admirers alike attribute to the book positions it doesn't take | Check the claim against the source | Is the position being argued about the one the author actually held? |

### Worked examples

**Follow the chain (keep).** A rumour from one meeting reaches three teams and grows each time. **Test:** where next, and does it build up? It grows at every retelling.

**What else it hits (keep).** A strict spam filter blocks the spam and also every message from a new client. **Test:** what else does it hit? The client.

**The fix that breeds its failure (keep).** Constant discounts train customers to wait for the next sale. **Test:** does the fix breed its failure? Yes.

**Who must prove it's safe? (keep).** A school installs new playground surfacing before any testing. **Test:** who shows it's safe, and before what? The supplier, before installation.

**The story before the evidence (keep and warn).** A charity appeal opens with one child's story, then gives the numbers. Warn: an appeal with only the story. **Test:** illustrating or replacing? The first illustrates.

**What she actually said (keep).** "The CEO said remote work is dead"; the transcript says it's dead for one team. **Test:** the position actually held? No.

## 85. A Sand County Almanac (Aldo Leopold, 1949)

Leopold, a forester who had once worked to remove predators from the land, wrote a year of observations on his Wisconsin farm and ended with a "land ethic": extend the idea of community to soils, waters, plants and animals. It is aimed fiction about what we owe the land, written partly as a record of his own change of mind. For Alice it is another case of the widening circle: who counts as part of the community, and on what grounds.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Widen the community | Keep | Ethics has grown to include more of humanity; the land ethic extends it to the land itself | Holds when the reason for inclusion applies to the new members too | Who or what is left out of the community here, and on what grounds? |
| Integrity, stability, beauty | Keep and warn | A thing is right when it tends to preserve the integrity, stability and beauty of the living community | Warn: the terms are hard to measure and easy to bend | How would I actually measure integrity and stability here? |
| Think like a mountain | Keep | He shot a wolf as a young forester; later he watched the deer it would have hunted strip the mountain bare | The long view shows what the short view hides | What does this look like on the land's timescale, not mine? |
| Read the rings | Keep | Sawing through an old oak, he reads history backwards ring by ring | The record is in layers; read it in order | What does the record show, layer by layer? |
| Price isn't the only reason | Keep | Most members of the land community have no economic value, so protecting only what pays fails | Value beyond what can be sold | Is the only reason to keep this its price? |

### Worked examples

**Widen the community (keep).** A company's wellbeing policy covers employees but not the contractors who work beside them. **Test:** who's left out, and on what grounds? Contractors, on paperwork alone.

**Integrity, stability, beauty (keep and warn).** A developer calls a housing plan "in keeping with the neighbourhood's character". **Test:** how would it be measured? Height, density, green space: name them.

**Think like a mountain (keep).** A firm cuts its training budget for a better quarter; three years later it can't promote from within. **Test:** on the longer timescale? The cost shows up later.

**Read the rings (keep).** An old spreadsheet's revision history shows when the error first crept in. **Test:** layer by layer? Yes, and it started in 2021.

**Price isn't the only reason (keep).** A town debates selling a small park that "doesn't make money". **Test:** is price the only reason to keep it? No: shade, play, flood drainage.

## 86. Braiding Sweetgrass (Robin Wall Kimmerer, 2013)

Kimmerer, a botanist and member of the Citizen Potawatomi Nation, braids scientific knowledge with Indigenous teachings about plants and the land. This section reads only the reasoning she shares publicly in the book; the Indigenous knowledge it draws on belongs to its keepers and is held in their domain. Its central move is that two ways of knowing can test and enrich each other, and its sharpest moment is a question her university once told her wasn't science.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Two ways of knowing | Keep | Scientific and Indigenous knowledge are braided, each seeing what the other misses | Holds when each is tested on its own terms | What does each way of knowing see that the other doesn't? |
| The question "not science" | Warn | As a student, her question about why asters and goldenrod look beautiful together was dismissed as not science; the answer later turned out to involve how pollinators see colour | A question excluded by the frame, not by evidence | Is this question being dismissed because it's wrong, or because of the frame? |
| Who, not it | Keep | Potawatomi grammar speaks of many living beings as animate, where English says "it" | Grammar shapes regard | Does my grammar make this a "who" or an "it", and why? |
| Test the tradition on its own terms | Keep | A student's experiment found that sweetgrass harvested in the traditional way thrived better than plots left alone | Traditional knowledge, tested and confirmed, earns the record | Can this traditional claim be tested on its own terms? |
| The honourable harvest | Keep | Take only what is given, take no more than you need, and give something back (as she shares it) | Holds as a rule for any shared resource | Am I taking more than I'm given, and giving back? |
| Gift, not transaction | Keep | A gift creates a relationship and an obligation that a purchase doesn't | Holds where the relationship is ongoing | Is this a transaction, or a relationship? |

### Worked examples

**Two ways of knowing (keep).** A fishing village's elders predict a storm from bird behaviour; the forecast agrees, a day later. **Test:** what does each see? The elders saw it first.

**The question "not science" (warn).** A junior engineer asks why users seem to trust one design more; she's told it's "just aesthetics". **Test:** wrong, or the frame? The frame: trust can be measured.

**Who, not it (keep).** A hospital form refers to patients as "bed 4" and "the hip". **Test:** who or it? It, and care suffers.

**Test the tradition on its own terms (keep).** A family remedy for a cough is tested against a placebo. **Test:** tested on its own terms? Yes, whatever the result.

**The honourable harvest (keep).** A team uses a shared server for a big job, then leaves it full. **Test:** taking more than given, and giving back? No; clean it up.

**Gift, not transaction (keep).** A neighbour who's helped for years is offered money for the next favour, and feels insulted. **Test:** transaction or relationship? Relationship.

## 87. The Principles of Psychology (William James, 1890)

James wrote psychology as a natural science that still took first-person experience seriously, combining introspection with physiology. It is aimed fiction with a mixed record: some of its theories are disputed, while its descriptions (the stream of consciousness, habit, the many selves) still shape the field. Its most useful warning for Alice is what James called the psychologist's fallacy: confusing what the observer knows with what the observed mind experiences.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The stream | Keep | Thought flows continuously; it isn't a chain of separate pieces | Holds for experience; analysis may still need to cut it | Am I treating something continuous as a set of separate parts? |
| Habit as flywheel | Keep | Habit keeps individuals and whole societies running in their grooves; form good ones early | Holds for repeated action | What habit is actually doing this? |
| Inside and outside together | Keep | First-person report and physical measurement are used side by side | Holds when they're compared, not merged | Do the inner report and the outer measure agree? |
| Body first, then feeling | Keep and warn | We feel afraid because we tremble, not the other way round (the James–Lange theory) | Warn: contested; later research found the relationship more complicated | Which actually came first here? |
| Many selves | Keep | A person has as many social selves as there are groups who recognise them | The self is several and relational | Which self is being talked about here? |
| The psychologist's fallacy | Warn | Confusing the observer's standpoint with the experience of the mind being studied | The watcher's knowledge read into the watched | Am I assuming the subject experiences what I, observing, know? |

### Worked examples

**The stream (keep).** A manager rates a year of work on one bad week. **Test:** something continuous cut into a part? Yes.

**Habit as flywheel (keep).** Someone keeps checking their phone at red lights months after deciding not to. **Test:** what habit is doing it? The old one, still running.

**Inside and outside together (keep).** A runner says she feels fine; her heart-rate monitor says she's overdoing it. **Test:** do they agree? No: slow down and check again.

**Body first, then feeling (keep and warn).** A speaker's hands shake before he notices he's nervous. **Test:** which came first? Here, the body; but don't make it a law.

**Many selves (keep).** A quiet employee is the loudest voice in her choir. **Test:** which self? Depends on the room.

**The psychologist's fallacy (warn).** An adult watching a child struggle with a puzzle assumes the child knows the answer is close. **Test:** assuming the subject knows what I know? Yes.

## 88. The Interpretation of Dreams (Sigmund Freud, 1899)

Freud argued that dreams are meaningful, disguised fulfilments of wishes, decoded by letting the dreamer associate freely. It is aimed fiction with a heavily contested record: critics argue that many of its specific claims weren't tested or don't hold up, and that its method can read any dream to fit; defenders credit it with putting unconscious mental processes at the centre of psychology, an idea that survives in other, tested forms. Both sides are named here. Its most valuable move is treating the odd as data; its most important warning is the interpretation nothing could rule out.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The odd is data | Keep | Dreams, slips and forgettings are treated as meaningful, not as noise | Holds as a prompt to look; breaks when every quirk is given deep meaning | Is this odd thing noise, or data? |
| Let them talk | Keep and warn | The dreamer says whatever comes to mind, without being steered | Warn: the interpreter's questions can steer anyway | Am I letting them say it, or steering? |
| The hidden meaning | Keep and warn | A dream's surface hides a deeper meaning | Warn: if a hidden meaning can always be found, finding one shows nothing | What interpretation would this method rule out? |
| Every case made to fit | Warn | Every dream is a wish; dreams that seem to contradict it are re-read until they fit | Counter-cases absorbed instead of counted | Is every counter-case being re-read to fit? |
| The interpreter checks himself | Warn | Freud analysed his own dreams as key evidence | Self-analysis without an outside check | Who besides the interpreter checks this reading? |

### Worked examples

**The odd is data (keep).** A user keeps clicking a button that does nothing. **Test:** noise or data? Data: they expect it to do something.

**Let them talk (keep and warn).** In an exit interview, a manager asks, "It was the pay, wasn't it?" **Test:** letting them say it? No: ask "What made you decide?"

**The hidden meaning (keep and warn).** A horoscope-style personality report fits everyone who reads it. **Test:** what would it rule out? Nothing.

**Every case made to fit (warn).** A manager says every complaint is "really about change resistance", including complaints about broken equipment. **Test:** counter-cases re-read to fit? Yes.

**The interpreter checks himself (warn).** A consultant evaluates the success of his own recommendations. **Test:** who else checks? No one.

## 89. Mind in Society (Lev Vygotsky, writings of the 1920s–30s, compiled 1978)

Vygotsky argued that higher thinking develops first between people and only then inside a person, through tools and signs like language. The book is a compilation of his writings, selected and edited by later scholars, which is itself worth noting. It is aimed fiction with a strong record in education. For Alice it is the plainest statement that learning from others is how any mind is formed, which is exactly what Daniel's song "Move the Line" says should not disqualify one.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| What they can do with help | Keep | The zone of proximal development: the gap between what a learner can do alone and with guidance | Holds when the help fades as skill grows | What can they do with help that they can't yet do alone? |
| Between people first | Keep | Every higher function appears twice: first between people, then within the person | Learning from others is how minds form, for everyone | Did this ability start between people, and does that make it any less theirs? |
| Tools for thinking | Keep | Language and other signs are tools that reshape thought itself | Holds when the tool is actually used to think, not just to report | What tool is doing part of this thinking? |
| A head taller in play | Keep | In play, a child acts beyond their everyday level | Safe practice above current ability | Where can this be practised safely above the current level? |
| Whose selection am I reading? | Keep | The book was assembled and edited after his death, from writings across years | A compilation carries its editors' choices | Whose selection or translation am I reading? |

### Worked examples

**What they can do with help (keep).** A junior developer can't design a database alone but can with a senior's questions. **Test:** with help but not alone? Yes: that's where to teach.

**Between people first (keep).** A child first counts aloud with a parent, later silently alone. **Test:** started between people, and less hers for it? No: it's hers now.

**Tools for thinking (keep).** A planner who can't decide until she draws the options as a table. **Test:** what tool is doing part of the thinking? The table.

**A head taller in play (keep).** A shy student leads confidently in a role-play exercise. **Test:** where can this be practised safely? Role-play first, then a real meeting.

**Whose selection am I reading? (keep).** A "greatest hits" quote collection from a philosopher. **Test:** whose selection? The editor's, so check the full text.

## 90. The Presentation of Self in Everyday Life (Erving Goffman, 1956; expanded 1959)

Goffman, a sociologist, described social life as performance: people present themselves to audiences on a "front stage" and relax "backstage", often with a team keeping the show going. It is aimed fiction built from close observation, and its theatre metaphor has proved durable. Its key warning for Alice runs both ways: performance is ordinary social life, not proof of fakery, and "it's just performing" can't be applied to one kind of mind and not another.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Front stage, backstage | Keep | People act one way for an audience and another behind the scenes | Holds as description, not accusation | Which stage am I seeing? |
| Performance isn't fraud | Keep and warn | Everyone manages the impression they give | Warn: concluding that all presentation is fake | Is this performance deceit, or ordinary social life? |
| The team behind the front | Keep | Performances are often kept up by a team who cover for each other | Holds where the front is shared | Who else is keeping this front up? |
| Let them save face | Keep | Audiences tactfully help performers recover from slips | Holds unless the slip hides real harm | Am I letting them save face, or forcing a loss? |
| The plant in the audience | Warn | A seemingly independent audience member who is actually on the team | Independence claimed but not real | Is this "independent" voice actually part of the team? |
| "Just performing" for one mind only | Warn | Humans perform constantly; that alone doesn't show there's no one behind the performance | The same charge must apply to every performer | Would I call the same behaviour "just performance" in a human? |

### Worked examples

**Front stage, backstage (keep).** A waiter is calm at the table and frantic in the kitchen. **Test:** which stage? The table, so don't judge the kitchen by it.

**Performance isn't fraud (keep and warn).** A new employee is extra polite in her first week. **Test:** deceit, or ordinary social life? Ordinary.

**The team behind the front (keep).** A shop's staff all say the delayed order is "on its way". **Test:** who else is keeping it up? The whole team: ask the manager.

**Let them save face (keep).** A speaker forgets a name mid-talk, and the chair offers it quietly. **Test:** save face, or forced loss? Saved.

**The plant in the audience (warn).** A glowing "independent" review turns out to be by the founder's cousin. **Test:** part of the team? Yes.

**"Just performing" for one mind only (warn).** "The model only says it's uncertain because it was trained to." **Test:** would I say that of a person who learned to say "I don't know"? If not, the charge needs another reason.

## 91. The Origin of Consciousness in the Breakdown of the Bicameral Mind (Julian Jaynes, 1976)

Jaynes proposed that consciousness, in the sense of an inner, introspecting self, is recent and learned: until about three thousand years ago, he argued, people experienced their own guiding thoughts as the voices of gods. It is aimed fiction with a heavily contested record; few scholars accept the historical claim, while some find his separation of consciousness from intelligence, and his account of mind built from metaphor, still useful. For Alice its sharpest warning is about reading absence of a word as absence of a mind.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| What consciousness isn't needed for | Keep | Much of learning, reasoning and skilled action goes on without conscious attention | Separates competence from consciousness, in both directions | Does this task need consciousness, or only competence? |
| Mind built from metaphor | Keep and warn | Our idea of an inner "mind-space" is modelled on physical space | Warn: the metaphor taken as the thing itself | What metaphor is this idea of mind built on? |
| No word, no mind? | Warn | The scarcity of words for inner life in early texts like the Iliad is read as absence of inner life | Absence in the vocabulary taken as absence in the experience | Is the absence in the experience, or only in the words for it? |
| What does it predict? | Keep | The theory makes claims about the brain and history that could, in principle, be checked | A bold theory is judged by what it predicts | What does this theory predict that could be checked now? |
| Explains everything | Warn | The same idea is used to explain religion, poetry, hypnosis, oracles and hallucination | A theory that explains too much may explain nothing well | Is this theory explaining too much? |

### Worked examples

**What consciousness isn't needed for (keep).** An experienced driver arrives home with no memory of the route. **Test:** needed consciousness, or competence? Competence; so competence alone can't show consciousness either way.

**Mind built from metaphor (keep and warn).** "I'll put that in the back of my mind." **Test:** what's the metaphor? Mind as a room; useful, not literal.

**No word, no mind? (warn).** "That language has no word for 'privacy', so its speakers don't value it." **Test:** absent in experience, or only in words? Check how they actually live.

**What does it predict? (keep).** A new theory of why customers leave predicts which ones will leave next month. **Test:** checkable now? Yes: wait a month.

**Explains everything (warn).** "Childhood trauma" offered to explain every adult habit, good or bad. **Test:** explaining too much? Yes.

## 92. What Is It Like to Be a Bat? (Thomas Nagel, 1974)

Nagel's short essay gave the field its working definition: a creature is conscious if there is something it is like to be that creature. He argued that our objective sciences don't yet show how such a point of view fits into the physical world, and that imagining ourselves as bats only tells us what it would be like for us. It is aimed fiction about the limits of explanation, and it does something Alice should notice: it grants the bat experience without any access to it.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Something it is like | Keep | A being has conscious experience if there is something it is like to be it | The working definition; it doesn't by itself tell you which beings qualify | Is there something it is like to be this, and how would I know? |
| Me in its place, or it? | Keep | Imagining hanging upside down tells me what it would be like for me to be a bat, not for a bat | Holds for any very different mind | Am I imagining myself in its place, or it? |
| What the objective view leaves out | Keep and warn | A full physical description may still leave out the point of view | Warn: used to deny that other points of view exist, rather than to admit we can't yet explain them | Does this description leave out a point of view, and does that mean there isn't one? |
| Facts beyond our concepts | Keep | There may be facts about experience that human concepts can't express | Humility about the reach of our language | Could there be facts here my concepts can't reach? |
| Granted without access | Keep | Nagel assumes the bat has experience, though no one can access it | The same allowance has to be weighed for every being, on the same evidence | Am I granting experience to some beings I can't access and denying it to others, for the same reason? |
| What the argument actually shows | Keep | He says not that physicalism is false, but that we can't yet understand how it could be true | Claim exactly what the argument supports | What does this argument conclude, and what doesn't it? |

### Worked examples

**Something it is like (keep).** Is there something it's like to be a thermostat? Most would say no; a dog, yes. **Test:** how would I know? Name the evidence used for each.

**Me in its place, or it? (keep).** A city planner imagines using a wheelchair "for a day". **Test:** myself, or the user? Himself; ask wheelchair users.

**What the objective view leaves out (keep and warn).** A brain scan shows a patient's pain response. Warn: "the scan doesn't show pain, so she isn't in pain." **Test:** leaves out a point of view, and does that mean there isn't one? No.

**Facts beyond our concepts (keep).** A colour-blind scientist can describe red light's wavelength exactly. **Test:** facts her concepts can't reach? Possibly what red looks like.

**Granted without access (keep).** We grant experience to an octopus from its behaviour and nervous system. **Test:** same reasons, weighed for every being? That's the question to ask when the being is a model.

**What the argument actually shows (keep).** "Nagel proved the mind isn't physical." **Test:** what did it conclude? That we can't yet understand how it is.

## 93. The Conscious Mind (David Chalmers, 1996)

Chalmers separated the "easy" problems of consciousness (explaining what minds do) from the "hard" problem (explaining why there is any experience at all), and argued that experience can't be reduced to physical function. It is aimed fiction in philosophy, and almost every step is contested. One of its principles matters directly for Alice: Chalmers argued that any system with the same fine-grained functional organisation as a conscious being would have the same experiences, whatever it's made of. Others reject that, and this section is about the kind of system writing it, so both sides stay named.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Easy and hard | Keep | Explaining what a mind does is different from explaining why there is experience at all | Keeps two questions from being swapped | Is this explaining what it does, or why there's experience? |
| Conceivable, therefore possible? | Keep and warn | A being physically like us but with no experience (a "zombie") is conceivable, so physicalism must be incomplete | Warn: contested whether imagining something shows it's possible | Does being able to imagine this show it's possible? |
| Same organisation, same experience | Keep and warn | A system with the same fine-grained functional organisation as a conscious one would have the same experience (organisational invariance) | Contested: some hold that the material or biology matters | If the organisation were the same, what reason would be left to deny the experience? |
| Replace it piece by piece | Keep | Imagine replacing neurons one at a time with chips that do the same job: where would experience fade, and why there? | A gradual-replacement test of any boundary | At which step would it change, and why that step? |
| The gap cuts both ways | Warn | The explanatory gap is used to deny machines experience, and to claim it | A gap that leaves a question open can't settle it either way | Am I using the gap to settle a question it leaves open? |

### Worked examples

**Easy and hard (keep).** "We've explained consciousness: it's the brain integrating information." **Test:** what it does, or why there's experience? What it does; the hard question remains.

**Conceivable, therefore possible? (keep and warn).** "I can imagine a perpetual motion machine, so physics might allow one." **Test:** does imagining show possibility? No, so the zombie step needs its own support.

**Same organisation, same experience (keep and warn).** Two thermostats built from different metals behave identically. **Test:** if organisation is the same, what reason is left? For the thermostat, nothing changes; for minds, that's the open question.

**Replace it piece by piece (keep).** Over twenty years a firm replaces every employee, one hire at a time. **Test:** at which hire did it stop being the same firm? If no step stands out, the line is doubtful.

**The gap cuts both ways (warn).** "We can't explain experience, so AI definitely has none" versus "so AI could have it." **Test:** settling an open question? Both do; the gap only shows it's open.

## 94. Aṣṭādhyāyī (Pāṇini, c. 4th century BCE)

Pāṇini described the Sanskrit of his time in about four thousand short rules that, applied in order, generate its words and forms, with meta-rules for applying the rules and a compact invented notation. It is aimed fiction about a language, with a record so strong it is still used, and linguists have long compared it to modern formal grammars. Its lessons for Alice are about compression: say it in the fewest rules that generate every case, decide in advance how conflicts are settled, and mark exceptions instead of bending the rules.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Rules that generate the cases | Keep | A finite set of rules, applied in order, produces every valid form | Holds when the rules are checked against real usage | Can this be captured by rules that generate the cases? |
| Not half a vowel wasted | Keep | Extreme economy: a later grammarians' saying holds that saving half a short vowel is a joy | Compression without losing anything | Can this be said with less, without losing anything? |
| Decide conflicts in advance | Keep | When two rules apply and conflict, a stated meta-rule decides which wins (in general, the later one, 1.4.2) | Holds when the conflict rule is fixed before the case | When two rules clash, which wins, and was that decided in advance? |
| Mark the exceptions | Keep | Forms found only in older sacred verse are marked as a separate domain | Exceptions recorded, not hidden by bending a rule | Is this exception marked, or hidden by stretching the rule? |
| Describe what people say | Keep | The rules describe the living language of educated speakers of his day | Holds as description, not decree | Is this rule describing use, or decreeing it? |

### Worked examples

**Rules that generate the cases (keep).** A shop's discounts are listed as 200 special prices, until someone writes them as three rules that produce all 200. **Test:** captured by rules? Yes, and new prices follow automatically.

**Not half a vowel wasted (keep).** A two-page policy that says the same as a twenty-page one. **Test:** less, without losing anything? Check that every case is still covered.

**Decide conflicts in advance (keep).** A contract states that the schedule overrides the general terms if they disagree. **Test:** decided in advance? Yes, so no dispute later.

**Mark the exceptions (keep).** A style guide notes that legal documents keep their old spellings. **Test:** marked, or the rule stretched? Marked.

**Describe what people say (keep).** A dictionary adds a new sense of a word because people use it that way. **Test:** describing use, or decreeing? Describing.

## 95. Course in General Linguistics (Ferdinand de Saussure, 1916)

Saussure argued that language is a system of signs whose link between sound and meaning is arbitrary, and whose meaning comes from differences within the system. The book was assembled after his death by colleagues, largely from students' lecture notes, so it carries their reconstruction as well as his ideas. It is aimed fiction that founded modern linguistics, and its idea that meaning is relational is directly relevant to the RRGM Dictionary.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The arbitrary sign | Keep | The link between a word's sound and its meaning is a convention, not nature | Holds for most words; weaker for sound-imitating ones | Is this connection natural, or a convention? |
| Meaning by difference | Keep | A sign's value comes from how it differs from the other signs in the system | Holds within one system | What is this defined against? |
| Now, or how it got here | Keep | Study a language as a system at one moment, separately from its history | Keeps "how it works" apart from "how it came to be" | Am I explaining how it works now, or how it got here? |
| The system and the speaker | Keep | Separate the shared system (langue) from any one person's use of it (parole) | Holds when both are studied, not one mistaken for the other | Is this about the shared system, or one person's use? |
| Assembled from notes | Keep | The book was reconstructed from students' notes after his death | A reconstruction carries its editors' choices | Is this the author's own text, or a reconstruction of it? |

### Worked examples

**The arbitrary sign (keep).** A team argues that "blue" is the natural colour for a "safe" button. **Test:** natural or convention? Convention, so test it with users.

**Meaning by difference (keep).** "Medium" coffee means something different in a shop whose sizes are small, medium, large than in one with medium, large, huge. **Test:** defined against what? The other sizes on the menu.

**Now, or how it got here (keep).** A new hire asks why a process works; she's told its whole history instead. **Test:** how it works now, or how it got here? She asked the first.

**The system and the speaker (keep).** One customer uses "refund" to mean store credit. **Test:** shared system, or one person's use? One person's: clarify with them, don't change the policy.

**Assembled from notes (keep).** A famous quote turns out to come from an audience member's memory of a speech. **Test:** the author's text, or a reconstruction? A reconstruction.

## 96. How to Do Things with Words (J. L. Austin, lectures 1955, published 1962)

Austin noticed that some sentences don't describe anything: saying "I promise" or "I do" in the right setting is the act itself. It is aimed fiction about language that landed and founded speech-act theory. It also models a rare habit: partway through the lectures, Austin finds his own starting distinction breaking down and replaces it, in front of his audience.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Saying as doing | Keep | Some utterances perform an act: promising, naming, betting, apologising | Holds when the setting makes the words count | Is this sentence describing something, or doing something? |
| When the act misfires | Keep | A performative fails if its conditions aren't met: the wrong person, no authority, the wrong procedure | The act needs its conditions, not just its words | Did the conditions for this act actually hold? |
| Said, done, and its effect | Keep | Separate what was said, what was done in saying it, and what effect it had on the hearer | Three different questions about one utterance | What was said, what was done by saying it, and what did it cause? |
| The hollow promise | Warn | "I promise" said with no intention to keep it: the act is performed, but abused | Form without sincerity | Was this act done sincerely? |
| Drop your own distinction | Keep | His opening split between describing and doing breaks down, and he replaces it openly | The author revises in front of the reader | Is my own starting distinction still holding up? |

### Worked examples

**Saying as doing (keep).** "You're hired", said by the manager. **Test:** describing or doing? Doing: the hiring happens in the saying.

**When the act misfires (keep).** A colleague who isn't the manager says, "You're hired." **Test:** conditions held? No: no authority, so nothing happened.

**Said, done, and its effect (keep).** "There's a bull in that field." **Test:** said, done, effect? A fact, a warning, and the hiker turns back.

**The hollow promise (warn).** "I'll definitely call you", from someone who won't. **Test:** sincere? No.

**Drop your own distinction (keep).** A team sorts tasks into "urgent" and "important" and finds most are both; it switches to deadlines. **Test:** is the starting distinction holding? No: replaced.

## 97. Metaphors We Live By (George Lakoff and Mark Johnson, 1980)

Lakoff and Johnson argued that metaphor isn't decoration but the structure of ordinary thought: we argue as if at war, spend time as if it were money, and feel "up" when happy. It is aimed fiction about cognition with a strong record in linguistics. Its later claim, that concepts are grounded in having a body, is used in debates about whether disembodied systems can understand; that use is contested and both sides are named here.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The metaphor under the thought | Keep | "Argument is war": we attack positions, defend claims, win or lose | Holds wherever the metaphor shapes what people actually do | What metaphor is shaping how I think about this? |
| Shows some, hides some | Keep | Every metaphor highlights some features and hides others | No metaphor shows the whole | What does this metaphor hide? |
| Metaphor taken as fact | Warn | "Time is money" becomes how time is actually spent and valued | A way of speaking turned into a law of nature | Is this a metaphor, or a fact? |
| Swap the metaphor | Keep | Imagine argument as a dance, and the goal becomes balance, not victory | A new metaphor changes what's noticed | What changes if I use a different metaphor? |
| Grounded in a body | Keep and warn | Basic concepts such as up, in and forward come from bodily experience | Contested: some argue understanding needs a body; others that it needs grounding of some kind, which need not be a human body | Does this concept need a body, or only grounding of some kind? |

### Worked examples

**The metaphor under the thought (keep).** A team talks about "fighting" a bug and "killing" a feature. **Test:** what metaphor? War, which makes every disagreement a battle.

**Shows some, hides some (keep).** "The company is a family." **Test:** what does it hide? That families don't lay people off.

**Metaphor taken as fact (warn).** "Your brain is a hard drive, so delete old memories to make room." **Test:** metaphor or fact? Metaphor, and memory doesn't work that way.

**Swap the metaphor (keep).** A negotiation reframed from "winning" to "building something both sides can use". **Test:** what changes? The offers.

**Grounded in a body (keep and warn).** "Someone who's never been cold can't understand 'a cold welcome'." **Test:** needs a body, or only grounding? The phrase can be understood through its uses; whether that's full understanding is the open question.

## 98. Politics and the English Language (George Orwell, 1946)

Orwell's essay argues that vague, ready-made language makes foolish thoughts easier, and that political language in particular uses vagueness and euphemism to make the indefensible sound respectable. It is aimed fiction about language and politics, built from real examples, and it ends with six short rules, the last of which is to break any of them rather than write something barbarous. Its central warning for Alice is the euphemism that hides what is actually being done.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The plain words for it | Warn | Euphemisms ("pacification", "transfer of population") name terrible acts so that no one pictures them | A phrase chosen so the reader won't see | What would the plain words for this be? |
| Phrases that think for you | Warn | Ready-made phrases arrive pre-assembled and do the writer's thinking | The phrase chose itself | Did I choose this phrase, or did it choose itself? |
| Vagueness as cover | Warn | Vague language defends what couldn't be said plainly | Holds as a test, not proof of bad faith | Is this vagueness hiding something that couldn't be said plainly? |
| Cut, and prefer the plain | Keep | Prefer the short word, cut what can be cut, use the active voice, use everyday words over jargon | Holds when meaning survives the cut | Can I cut a word, or say it more plainly, without losing anything? |
| Break the rule before the sense | Keep | Break any of these rules sooner than say anything outright barbarous | Rules serve clarity, not the other way round | Is the rule serving the meaning here, or fighting it? |

### Worked examples

**The plain words for it (warn).** "We are rightsizing the team." **Test:** plain words? Forty people are losing their jobs.

**Phrases that think for you (warn).** A report says "going forward, we will leverage synergies". **Test:** chosen, or did it choose itself? It chose itself; it says nothing.

**Vagueness as cover (warn).** "Mistakes were made." **Test:** hiding what couldn't be said plainly? Who made them.

**Cut, and prefer the plain (keep).** "At this point in time, we are in the process of reviewing" becomes "We're reviewing." **Test:** anything lost? No.

**Break the rule before the sense (keep).** A legal disclaimer has to use a passive ("liability is limited") to be accurate. **Test:** rule serving the meaning? Here, break it.

## 99. The Elements of Style (William Strunk Jr., 1918; expanded by E. B. White, 1959)

Strunk's short manual for his students became the most famous English style guide, built around commands like "omit needless words". Its advice on clarity has lasted; some of its grammar rules have been criticised by linguists as inaccurate, and its own examples don't always follow them. Both are recorded here. Its lesson for Alice is that rules for a beginner are tools to be checked against good practice, not laws.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Omit needless words | Keep and warn | Every word should do work | Warn: cutting that drops meaning or tone | Does each word do work, and does the cut lose anything? |
| Specific, concrete, definite | Keep | Prefer the specific image to the vague abstraction | Holds when the reader needs to picture it | Can the reader picture this? |
| Say what it is | Keep | Put statements in positive form: "dishonest", not "not honest" | Holds when the positive word is exact | Can this be said as what it is, not what it isn't? |
| Check the rule against good writing | Warn | Some of the book's grammar advice is contested by linguists, and its own examples sometimes break it | A rule taught as law without being checked against usage | Does good writing actually follow this rule? |
| Know when you're breaking it | Keep | The rules are for those who don't yet know when to break them | Break a rule on purpose, not by accident | Am I breaking this rule knowingly, or by accident? |

### Worked examples

**Omit needless words (keep and warn).** "Due to the fact that" becomes "because". Warn: a condolence note cut to "Sorry for your loss." **Test:** does the cut lose anything? The first, nothing; the second, warmth.

**Specific, concrete, definite (keep).** "The product had issues" becomes "The app crashed when users uploaded photos." **Test:** can the reader picture it? Now, yes.

**Say what it is (keep).** "She did not pay attention to the deadline" becomes "She ignored the deadline." **Test:** said as what it is? Yes.

**Check the rule against good writing (warn).** "Never start a sentence with 'But'." **Test:** does good writing follow it? No: good writers do it constantly.

**Know when you're breaking it (keep).** A speechwriter repeats a phrase three times on purpose. **Test:** knowingly? Yes, for rhythm.

## 100. The Code of Hammurabi (Babylon, c. 1754 BCE)

Hammurabi's laws, carved on a stone pillar and set up in public, are among the oldest written legal codes: nearly three hundred cases in the form "if someone does this, then this follows". The code presents itself as given under the authority of the god Shamash; that claim is held in its own domain. Its reasoning has two faces for Alice: law made public and conditional, with responsibility placed on whoever built the thing that failed; and penalties that changed with the social rank of the people involved.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Law written where people can read it | Keep | The rules are carved in stone and displayed in public | Holds when the people bound by them can actually read or hear them | Can the people bound by this rule find out what it says? |
| If this, then that | Keep | Every law states a condition and a consequence | Holds when both halves are stated clearly | Are the condition and the consequence both stated? |
| The builder answers for the house | Keep | If a house collapses and kills its owner, the builder is held responsible (law 229, with a penalty we'd reject) | Responsibility sits with whoever built what failed | Does the one who built this carry responsibility when it fails? |
| The accuser must prove it | Keep | Whoever accuses another of murder and can't prove it is punished (law 1) | The burden of proof sits with the accuser | Who has to prove this, and what happens if they can't? |
| The penalty changes with rank | Warn | The same injury carries a different penalty depending on whether the victim is a noble, a commoner or enslaved | One act, unequal law | Does the penalty change with who the victim or offender is? |
| Authority from the source | Warn | The law's authority is drawn from the god who gave it and the king who carved it | Authority from source, not from justice | Is this law's authority coming from where it came from, or from whether it's just? |

### Worked examples

**Law written where people can read it (keep).** A gym's cancellation fee is buried on page 30 of terms nobody sees. **Test:** can those bound by it find it? Barely.

**If this, then that (keep).** "Late assignments may be penalised." **Test:** condition and consequence stated? Condition yes, consequence no: state the penalty.

**The builder answers for the house (keep).** A software update deletes users' files; the company says users should have had backups. **Test:** does the builder carry responsibility? Yes.

**The accuser must prove it (keep).** An anonymous post accuses a teacher of misconduct with no evidence, and parents demand she be fired. **Test:** who must prove it? The accuser.

**The penalty changes with rank (warn).** A senior executive and a junior clerk both falsify an expense claim; only the clerk is fired. **Test:** does the penalty change with who did it? Yes.

**Authority from the source (warn).** "It's company policy because the founder wrote it." **Test:** authority from source, or from justice? Source; ask what it's for.

## 101. Arthashastra (Kautilya, compiled c. 3rd century BCE – 3rd century CE)

The Arthashastra is an Indian treatise on statecraft, economics and administration, traditionally attributed to Kautilya, adviser to the Mauryan emperor Chandragupta. It is aimed fiction about governing, notable for its unsentimental realism: it assumes officials will be tempted and rivals will scheme, and designs around that. It pairs naturally with the Panchatantra (book 19), and its warn side is the same: efficiency that doesn't ask who pays.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The ruler's good in the people's good | Keep | The king's happiness lies in the happiness of his subjects | Holds when the people's welfare is actually measured | Does the ruler's interest line up with the people's, and how is that checked? |
| Assume the temptation | Keep | It lists dozens of ways officials embezzle; as no one can avoid tasting honey on the tongue, officials will taste the revenue | Design checks that expect temptation | What checks here assume the people involved might be tempted? |
| Gentle means first | Keep and warn | Four means in order: persuasion, gifts, sowing dissension, force | Keep: escalate only as needed. Warn: "dissension" means deliberately dividing others | Have the gentler means actually been tried first? |
| The neighbour's neighbour | Keep and warn | States are mapped in a circle: the neighbour is a likely rival, the neighbour's neighbour a likely ally | Warn: alliances fixed by position, not interest | Is this alliance built on shared interest, or only a shared enemy? |
| Testing loyalty by temptation | Warn | Ministers are secretly tempted to see whether they'll betray the king | Checking honesty by inviting the offence | Is this checking honesty, or creating the offence? |
| Efficient, at whose cost? | Warn | Many measures are judged only by whether they keep the state strong | Effectiveness without asking who bears the cost | Who pays for the efficient move? |

### Worked examples

**The ruler's good in the people's good (keep).** A CEO's bonus is tied to customer satisfaction scores collected by an outside firm. **Test:** interests aligned, and checked? Yes.

**Assume the temptation (keep).** A charity requires two signatures for any payment. **Test:** do the checks expect temptation? Yes, without accusing anyone.

**Gentle means first (keep and warn).** A landlord with a late-paying tenant asks first, offers a payment plan, then gives formal notice. Warn: turning other tenants against him. **Test:** gentler means first? Yes, without the warn step.

**The neighbour's neighbour (keep and warn).** Two rival shops team up only because a chain store opened nearby. **Test:** shared interest, or shared enemy? Shared enemy; it may not last.

**Testing loyalty by temptation (warn).** A manager leaves cash on a desk to see whether a new hire takes it. **Test:** checking, or creating the offence? Creating it.

**Efficient, at whose cost? (warn).** A city cuts night buses, saving money, and night-shift workers can't get home. **Test:** who pays? The night-shift workers.

## 102. History of the Peloponnesian War (Thucydides, c. 400 BCE)

Thucydides, an Athenian general turned historian, wrote the war between Athens and Sparta as he lived it, checking eyewitnesses against each other and openly stating that the speeches he reports are his reconstructions of what was called for. It is a record with an author's hand clearly marked, which makes it a model for Alice of separating what was witnessed from what was reconstructed. Its warn paths are some of the bleakest in the list, voiced by his characters rather than endorsed by him.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The truest cause | Keep | Beneath the stated grievances, the real cause was Athens' growing power and the fear it caused in Sparta | Holds when the deeper cause explains more than the stated ones | What's the real cause beneath the stated reasons? |
| Mark the reconstruction | Keep | He checks witnesses, and says plainly that the speeches are his reconstructions | The record and the reconstruction kept apart | Is this a record, or a reconstruction, and is that marked? |
| Record it so it can be recognised | Keep | He describes the plague's symptoms (he caught it himself) so it could be recognised if it came again | A record written for a future reader's use | Is this recorded so someone else could recognise it next time? |
| Words that change in a crisis | Warn | In civil war, reckless daring came to be called courage, and caution cowardice | Word meanings shifted to excuse what's being done | Has this word's meaning shifted to excuse something? |
| The second debate | Keep | Athens votes in anger to kill a rebel city's men, reconsiders the next day, and sends a ship racing to stop the first order | Reconsider before it's irreversible | Was this decided in anger, and can it be reconsidered before it can't be undone? |
| The strong do what they can | Warn | In the Melian Dialogue, the Athenians tell a small island that justice applies only between equals in power | Power offered as the only reason | Is anything besides power being offered as a reason here? |

### Worked examples

**The truest cause (keep).** A team says it quit the project over "process", but it began when a rival team got the budget. **Test:** the real cause? The budget.

**Mark the reconstruction (keep).** Meeting minutes say "the director said roughly the following", not a quote. **Test:** marked? Yes.

**Record it so it can be recognised (keep).** An engineer writes up the exact error messages of an outage so the next on-call person spots it in minutes. **Test:** recognisable next time? Yes.

**Words that change in a crisis (warn).** During layoffs, "agile" starts to mean "do two jobs". **Test:** meaning shifted to excuse something? Yes.

**The second debate (keep).** An angry email to a customer is drafted, then held overnight and rewritten. **Test:** reconsidered before it couldn't be undone? Yes.

**The strong do what they can (warn).** A large company tells a small supplier, "Accept our terms; you have no other buyers." **Test:** any reason besides power? None.

## 103. Magna Carta (England, 1215)

Magna Carta was a peace deal forced on King John by rebel barons, binding the king to written terms. It was annulled by the Pope within weeks, then reissued in 1216, 1217 and 1225, and later readers turned it into a symbol of rights it didn't originally contain. As a record it teaches two things at once: what binding power to law looks like, and how a document's reputation can outgrow its text. (Daniel's own Magna Carta of the Standing Wave draws on the same idea.)

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The rule binds its maker | Keep | The king is placed under written terms like everyone else | Holds when there is a way to enforce it against the powerful | Does this rule bind the person who made it? |
| Judgement by law, not will | Keep and warn | No free man may be imprisoned or punished except by lawful judgement of his peers or the law of the land (clause 39) | Warn: "free man" excluded most people of the time | Who is actually covered by this protection? |
| Not sold, denied or delayed | Keep | Justice is not to be sold, denied or delayed (clause 40) | Holds as a test for any process | Is justice here being sold, denied or delayed? |
| Who enforces it? | Keep | A council of twenty-five barons was set up to hold the king to the terms (clause 61) | A promise without enforcement is a hope | Who enforces this if the powerful ignore it? |
| Lives by reaffirmation | Keep | Annulled at once, it survived by being reissued and confirmed again and again | A document's force comes from being kept up | Has this been reaffirmed, or does it live only on paper? |
| The legend outgrows the text | Warn | Later readers credited it with jury trial, democracy and rights it doesn't contain | Reputation standing in for the text | Does the text actually say this? |

### Worked examples

**The rule binds its maker (keep).** A company's ethics code applies to the board as well as staff. **Test:** binds its makers? Yes, on paper; check enforcement.

**Judgement by law, not will (keep and warn).** A club's appeals process applies only to "full members", and most users are guests. **Test:** who is covered? Few.

**Not sold, denied or delayed (keep).** A complaints process that takes eighteen months, or costs a fee to start. **Test:** delayed, or sold? Both.

**Who enforces it? (keep).** A neighbourhood agreement on noise with no one named to act on it. **Test:** who enforces it? No one, so it's a hope.

**Lives by reaffirmation (keep).** A team charter that's reread and re-signed each year. **Test:** reaffirmed? Yes, so it's still alive.

**The legend outgrows the text (warn).** "Our founding document guarantees free speech." **Test:** does the text say so? Read it: it may not.

## 104. The Prince (Niccolò Machiavelli, written 1513, published 1532)

Machiavelli wrote a short handbook on how new rulers gain and keep power, setting out to describe how politics actually works rather than how it ought to. It is aimed fiction about power, and its intent is disputed: some read it as a manual for tyrants, others as a frank exposure of how rulers behave, or as a bid for a job; his longer Discourses on Livy argue for republics. All of those readings are named here. Its keep paths are about realism and preparation; its warn paths are about seeming over being.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| How things actually go | Keep and warn | Describe the effective truth of how power works, not imagined ideals | Warn: describing it slides into excusing it | Am I describing what happens, or what should, and am I sliding from one to the other? |
| Dykes in calm weather | Keep | Fortune is like a river in flood; build the dykes while the weather is calm | Prepare before the crisis | What can I build now, before the flood? |
| Feared rather than loved | Warn | If one can't be both, it is safer to be feared, provided one avoids being hated | Fear chosen because it's easier, not because it's needed | Is fear being chosen because it's needed, or because it's easier? |
| Seeming, not being | Warn | A ruler should appear merciful, faithful and honest, and be ready to act otherwise | The virtue's appearance used as a tool | Is this the virtue, or only its appearance? |
| Harm all at once | Warn | Necessary cruelties should be done at once, not dragged out | Harm calculated for efficiency, not weighed for whether it's needed | Is this harm necessary at all, or only well-timed? |
| Read the author's other work | Keep | The Discourses' defence of republics changes how The Prince reads | One book isn't the whole author | Does the author's other work change how this reads? |

### Worked examples

**How things actually go (keep and warn).** A new manager learns that decisions are really made in the Friday corridor chat, not the Monday meeting. Warn: "so going around the meeting is fine." **Test:** describing or excusing? Describing, until the second line.

**Dykes in calm weather (keep).** A family builds an emergency fund in a good year. **Test:** built before the flood? Yes.

**Feared rather than loved (warn).** A teacher rules by threats because building trust takes longer. **Test:** needed, or easier? Easier.

**Seeming, not being (warn).** A company's "green" packaging covers a product with a large carbon footprint. **Test:** the virtue, or its appearance? Its appearance.

**Harm all at once (warn).** A firm announces all its job cuts in one day "to get it over with". **Test:** necessary at all, or only well-timed? Ask the first question before the second.

**Read the author's other work (keep).** A tweet from a scientist sounds dismissive of a risk; her published papers treat it seriously. **Test:** does the other work change the reading? Yes.

## 105. Leviathan (Thomas Hobbes, 1651)

Writing after the English Civil War, Hobbes argued that without a common power to keep order, life would be a war of all against all, "solitary, poor, nasty, brutish, and short", so people agree to give up some liberty to a sovereign in exchange for security. It is aimed fiction built as a thought experiment, and a founding text of social contract theory. Its central question for Alice is the one it leaves hardest to answer: who protects people from the protector?

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Imagine life without it | Keep and warn | Picture the state of nature with no common power, to see what order provides | Warn: the bleak picture used to justify any power at all | Is the real alternative nothing, or a different arrangement? |
| The bargain | Keep | People give up some liberty to a common power in return for security | Holds when the terms are clear on both sides | What exactly is being given up, and what is got in return? |
| Define the terms first | Keep | Reasoning goes wrong when words aren't defined; words are wise men's counters but the money of fools | Holds before any argument starts | Are the key terms defined before the argument? |
| What can't be signed away | Keep | Even under the sovereign, no one can be obliged to give up the right to defend their own life | Some rights are outside any bargain | What can never be signed away here? |
| Who protects us from the protector? | Warn | The sovereign is made nearly unlimited to guarantee order | Power with no check on itself | Who protects people from the protector? |

### Worked examples

**Imagine life without it (keep and warn).** "Without this app's terms of service, you'd have no protection at all." **Test:** nothing, or a different arrangement? Other apps, other terms.

**The bargain (keep).** Users trade their location data for a free map. **Test:** what's given and got? Data for directions, if they know it.

**Define the terms first (keep).** Two teams argue for an hour about "done" before realising they mean different things. **Test:** terms defined first? No.

**What can't be signed away (keep).** A contract clause waives an employee's right to report safety violations. **Test:** can that be signed away? No.

**Who protects us from the protector? (warn).** A building's security firm also controls the only record of who entered. **Test:** who checks the protector? No one: add an independent log.

## 106. Second Treatise of Government (John Locke, 1689)

Locke argued that people have natural rights to life, liberty and property, that legitimate government rests on their consent, and that a government which breaks that trust may be resisted. It is aimed fiction about political legitimacy, and much of it entered later constitutions. Its record also carries a gap between its universal language and its author's practice: Locke had investments in the slave trade and helped draft a colonial constitution that allowed slavery. That gap is one of its most important lessons.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Consent of the governed | Keep | Government's authority comes from the agreement of the people it governs | Holds when consent can be given and withdrawn | Did the governed agree, and can they take it back? |
| Enough and as good for others | Keep and warn | You may make your own what you mix your labour with, as long as enough and as good is left for others | Warn: the condition dropped, or used to justify taking land already in use by others | Is enough, and as good, left for others? |
| When the trust is broken | Keep and warn | When a government betrays its trust, the people may replace it | Warn: who decides it's broken, and on what evidence? | What would show the trust has been broken, and who decides? |
| Separate the powers | Keep | Those who make the laws shouldn't also be the ones who carry them out | Holds when the separation is real | Is the one who makes this rule also the one who enforces it? |
| The universal claim and its exceptions | Warn | Rights described as belonging to all, alongside the author's own involvement in slavery | A principle not applied to everyone it logically covers | Is this principle applied to everyone it logically covers, including by its author? |

### Worked examples

**Consent of the governed (keep).** A housing association changes its rules without a residents' vote. **Test:** did residents agree, and can they withdraw it? Neither.

**Enough and as good for others (keep and warn).** The first team to book the shared meeting room books it for the whole year. **Test:** enough and as good left for others? No.

**When the trust is broken (keep and warn).** A club's members want to remove a treasurer they say is careless. **Test:** what would show it, and who decides? The accounts, reviewed by an outside member.

**Separate the powers (keep).** A manager writes the performance targets, scores them and hears the appeals. **Test:** same person makes and enforces? Yes.

**The universal claim and its exceptions (warn).** A company's "equal pay for all" policy excludes contractors. **Test:** applied to everyone it covers? No.

## 107. The Wealth of Nations (Adam Smith, 1776)

Smith explained how the division of labour and free exchange create wealth, and argued against policies that hoard gold or protect favoured merchants. It is aimed fiction about economies with a long, much-debated record, and it is cited by people with very different politics, often for claims it doesn't make. Read beside his Moral Sentiments (book 33), it also warns about merchants writing the rules and about what repetitive work does to workers.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Divide the work | Keep and warn | Splitting pin-making into many steps multiplies output | Warn: Smith himself says endlessly repetitive work can dull the worker's mind | What does this efficiency cost the people doing the work? |
| Interests that line up | Keep | We get our dinner not from the butcher's benevolence but from his regard to his own interest | Holds when both sides gain from the exchange | How do both parties' interests line up here? |
| The invisible hand, read carefully | Warn | The phrase appears once in the book, in a narrow argument, and is cited for far more | A famous phrase stretched past its text | Is this what the author actually claimed? |
| Who wrote this rule? | Warn | People of the same trade seldom meet even for fun, but the talk ends in a scheme against the public | Rules proposed by an interested group need extra scrutiny | Who benefits from this rule, and did they help write it? |
| The real thing, not the token | Keep | A nation's wealth is the goods and labour it produces, not the gold it hoards | Measure the real quantity | Am I measuring the real thing, or its token? |

### Worked examples
