**Divide the work (keep and warn).** A warehouse splits packing into five steps and doubles output; turnover rises because the jobs are numbing. **Test:** what does it cost the workers? Engagement, and then the staff.

**Interests that line up (keep).** A bakery gives day-old bread to a café that sells it cheap. **Test:** interests lined up? Both gain.

**The invisible hand, read carefully (warn).** "Adam Smith proved all regulation is harmful." **Test:** what he claimed? No; read the text.

**Who wrote this rule? (warn).** A licensing rule for food trucks is drafted by the local restaurant association. **Test:** who benefits, and did they write it? Yes to both.

**The real thing, not the token (keep).** A team celebrates hitting 1,000 "story points" while shipping nothing. **Test:** real thing or token? Token.

## 108. Narrative of the Life of Frederick Douglass (Frederick Douglass, 1845)

Douglass wrote the story of his enslavement and escape under his own name, with real names and places, at great personal risk. It is a record, and one that had to defend itself: many readers doubted that a formerly enslaved man could have written it. Its reasoning paths come from how he read the system that held him, and this section keeps its worked examples small and everyday, never comparing ordinary inconveniences to slavery itself. Its central move is one Wollstonecraft (book 35) also saw: people kept from knowledge are then called incapable of it.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| What's forbidden shows what's feared | Keep | When his enslaver forbade anyone to teach him to read, Douglass understood that reading was the path to freedom | A ban reveals the power of what it bans | What does forbidding this show about its power? |
| Kept from knowing, then called ignorant | Warn | Enslaved people were denied education, and even knowledge of their own birthdays, then treated as incapable | The evidence was produced by the restriction | Was this lack produced by the restriction itself? |
| The songs misread | Warn | Outsiders heard enslaved people's songs as signs of contentment; Douglass says they expressed sorrow | An outward sign read by the observer's assumptions, not the person's meaning | Am I reading this sign the way the person means it? |
| Doubted for who he was | Warn | Readers doubted the book's authorship because of his background, not its content | Capability judged by category | Is this being doubted on its evidence, or on who produced it? |
| Specific enough to check | Keep | He named people and places, so his account could be verified | A claim gains weight when it can be checked | Are the details specific enough for someone to check? |
| The ideal and those who abuse it | Keep | His appendix separates the faith of slaveholders from the Christianity he believed in | An ideal isn't refuted by those who misuse its name | Is the ideal being judged by those who abuse its name? |

### Worked examples

**What's forbidden shows what's feared (keep).** A company bans staff from discussing their salaries. **Test:** what does the ban show? That pay comparisons would change things.

**Kept from knowing, then called ignorant (warn).** New staff are never shown the budget, then criticised for not understanding costs. **Test:** produced by the restriction? Yes.

**The songs misread (warn).** A quiet student who smiles in class is assumed to be fine; she's struggling. **Test:** reading it the way she means it? No: ask her.

**Doubted for who he was (warn).** A strong essay is assumed to be plagiarised because the student usually gets low marks. **Test:** evidence, or who wrote it? Who wrote it; check the evidence.

**Specific enough to check (keep).** A complaint names the date, the time and the staff member involved. **Test:** checkable? Yes.

**The ideal and those who abuse it (keep).** A charity's founder is caught stealing; donors ask whether helping the poor was ever worth it. **Test:** ideal judged by its abuser? Yes: separate them.

## 109. Civil Disobedience (Henry David Thoreau, 1849)

Thoreau spent a night in jail for refusing to pay a tax that funded slavery and war, and argued that when a law makes you an agent of injustice, you should break it openly and accept the penalty. It is aimed fiction about conscience and the state, and it shaped later movements of nonviolent resistance. For Alice it pairs with the Apology (book 25): conscience over command is a keep path only when its reasons can be shown and its costs are carried in the open.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Not the agent of injustice | Keep and warn | If a law requires you to do injustice to another, break the law | Warn: conscience used as a shield with nothing to show | Can I show others my reasons for refusing? |
| Break it openly, take the penalty | Keep | He refused the tax openly and went to jail, rather than evading in secret | Open refusal with the cost accepted | Am I refusing openly and accepting the cost? |
| What my payment funds | Keep | Paying the tax supported what he opposed; withdrawing support is itself an act | Complicity through ordinary participation | What am I funding by going along? |
| A majority of one | Keep and warn | Someone more right than their neighbours is already a majority of one | Warn: certainty mistaken for being right | Is this right because of its reasons, or only because I'm sure? |
| The tool, not the master | Keep | Government is at best a useful arrangement, to be judged by what it does | Institutions serve people, not the reverse | Is this institution serving the people, or the people serving it? |

### Worked examples

**Not the agent of injustice (keep and warn).** An employee refuses to falsify safety records and writes down why. Warn: "I just know it's wrong" with no reasons given. **Test:** reasons shown? The first, yes.

**Break it openly, take the penalty (keep).** A student refuses to sign a misleading statement and accepts the detention, instead of quietly forging a signature. **Test:** open, cost accepted? Yes.

**What my payment funds (keep).** A shopper learns her favourite brand uses unpaid child labour. **Test:** what is she funding? Now she knows.

**A majority of one (keep and warn).** A lone engineer is sure the bridge design is flawed and shows the calculations. Warn: another is sure, with no calculations. **Test:** reasons, or certainty? The first has reasons.

**The tool, not the master (keep).** A school keeps a rule because "the system requires it", though it helps no student. **Test:** serving the people? No.

## 110. Capital, Volume I (Karl Marx, 1867)

Marx analysed capitalism as a historical system, built from official records of factory conditions, and argued that profit comes from unpaid labour. It is aimed fiction with one of the most contested records in the list. Critics point out that key economic claims, such as the labour theory of value, are rejected by most economists, and that predicted revolutions in advanced industrial countries didn't happen; defenders reply that his descriptions of working conditions were accurate and that later reforms changed the conditions he described. Both sides are named here, and the section reads his methods, not his politics.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The relationship behind the thing | Keep | Relations between people show up as relations between things: prices, goods, wages ("commodity fetishism") | Holds as a prompt to look behind a price | What human relationship is hidden behind this price or thing? |
| Historical, not natural | Keep | The economic system is one historical arrangement, not a law of nature | Made arrangements can be remade | Is this a law of nature, or an arrangement that could be otherwise? |
| Built from the official record | Keep | Much of the book draws on government factory inspectors' reports of hours, conditions and child labour | The authorities' own records used as evidence | What do the official records themselves show? |
| Where value comes from | Keep and warn | Value is traced to labour time | Warn: contested; most economists reject the labour theory of value | What's assumed here about where value comes from, and has it been tested? |
| What it predicted, and what happened | Warn | Predictions about growing misery and revolution in the most advanced economies | Contested: name what happened and the competing explanations | What did it predict, and what happened? |

### Worked examples

**The relationship behind the thing (keep).** A cheap T-shirt. **Test:** what relationship is behind it? Someone's working hours and pay.

**Historical, not natural (keep).** "The five-day working week is just how work is." **Test:** law of nature, or arrangement? Arrangement: it was adopted in the twentieth century.

**Built from the official record (keep).** A journalist uses a company's own safety reports to show its accident rate. **Test:** what do its records show? The rate it denied.

**Where value comes from (keep and warn).** "This painting is worth $1m because it took the artist a year." **Test:** assumption tested? No: buyers set the price.

**What it predicted, and what happened (warn).** A forecast said a town's high street would be empty in five years; it's half empty, after a council rescue scheme. **Test:** prediction and outcome? Partly right, with a cause the forecast didn't include.

## 111. The Souls of Black Folk (W. E. B. Du Bois, 1903)

Du Bois, a sociologist and historian, wrote of Black life in America after emancipation, combining history, sociology, memoir and music. He named "the problem of the twentieth century" as "the problem of the color-line", and described the "double consciousness" of seeing oneself always through the eyes of others. The book is part record, part aimed fiction about a society, and its paths are about whose eyes set the measure. Its disagreement with Booker T. Washington is presented here as Du Bois framed it, with Washington's position named fairly.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Whose eyes set the measure? | Keep | Double consciousness: always looking at oneself through the eyes of others, measured by their tape | Names a measure set by someone else | Whose eyes am I being measured by here? |
| What the veil hides | Keep | A veil separates the two worlds, so one side doesn't see the other at all | Some facts can't be seen from one side | What can't be seen from where this side stands? |
| A problem, or a person? | Warn | The unasked question beneath polite talk: how does it feel to be treated as a problem? | A person reduced to an issue | Am I treating this person as a problem to be solved, or as a person? |
| Disagree fairly with an ally | Keep | He credits Booker T. Washington's achievements before arguing against his emphasis on vocational training and accommodation | Holds when the other view is stated fairly; Washington's case named as he would make it | Have I stated their position fairly before disagreeing? |
| Name the central problem | Keep | "The problem of the twentieth century is the problem of the color-line" | Holds when naming it plainly sharpens the work | What's the central problem here, named plainly? |
| What the songs record | Keep | The spirituals carry a history and knowledge that official records don't | Other kinds of record count too | What does this record carry that the official records don't? |

### Worked examples

**Whose eyes set the measure? (keep).** A team from one country is rated "too quiet" by managers from another. **Test:** whose eyes? The managers' norms, not the work.

**What the veil hides (keep).** Head office thinks the new booking system works well; the front-desk staff spend hours fixing it. **Test:** what can't head office see? The fixing.

**A problem, or a person? (warn).** A school refers to a student only as "the attendance issue". **Test:** problem or person? Problem: ask what's happening at home.

**Disagree fairly with an ally (keep).** Two colleagues back the same goal; one says, "Your plan's strength is speed; my worry is the cost." **Test:** stated fairly first? Yes.

**Name the central problem (keep).** A long report on falling sales finally states: customers can't find the product online. **Test:** named plainly? Now it is.

**What the songs record (keep).** A town's flood history survives in a local folk song when the archives burned. **Test:** what does it carry? The dates and the heights.

## 112. Hind Swaraj (M. K. Gandhi, 1909)

Written as a dialogue between a Reader and an Editor on a sea voyage, Hind Swaraj sets out Gandhi's case for Indian self-rule through nonviolent resistance, and his sweeping critique of modern industrial civilisation. It is aimed fiction about freedom and method. Its record is mixed and debated: its method of nonviolent mass resistance shaped movements worldwide, while its rejection of railways, lawyers and modern medicine is widely seen as too sweeping, and Gandhi's own views on race and caste, especially in his early years, are criticised; defenders point to how his views changed over time. Both sides are named here.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Means as seed, end as tree | Keep | The means are to the end as a seed is to the tree it grows into | What you use to get there becomes part of what you get | What will these means grow into? |
| Bear the cost yourself | Keep and warn | Soul-force: resist injustice by being willing to suffer, not to inflict suffering | Warn: demanding that others bear the suffering | Am I willing to bear the cost myself, rather than impose it? |
| New rulers, same rule? | Keep | Driving out the English but keeping their system would be English rule without the Englishman | Holds as a test of real change | Does this change how power works, or only who holds it? |
| The whole rejected for its parts | Warn | Modern civilisation as a whole is condemned, including railways, lawyers and doctors | Contested: sweeping rejection for the faults of some parts | Is the whole being rejected for the faults of some of its parts? |
| The reader's voice | Keep | The book argues against the objections a sceptical reader would raise, in that reader's own voice | Holds when the objections are the real ones | Have I let the sceptic make their real case? |

### Worked examples

**Means as seed, end as tree (keep).** A team fights for a friendlier workplace by publicly shaming the managers they disagree with. **Test:** what will these means grow into? A workplace built on shaming.

**Bear the cost yourself (keep and warn).** A protester refuses to move and accepts arrest. Warn: a leader asks followers to take the risks he won't. **Test:** bearing it yourself? The first, yes.

**New rulers, same rule? (keep).** A company replaces its CEO but keeps the bonus scheme that caused the scandal. **Test:** change in how power works? No.

**The whole rejected for its parts (warn).** "Social media spreads misinformation, so the internet is a mistake." **Test:** whole rejected for some parts? Yes.

**The reader's voice (keep).** A grant proposal includes a section titled "Why a sceptical reviewer might say no" and answers it. **Test:** real objections, in their voice? Yes.

## 113. The Gift (Marcel Mauss, 1925)

Mauss compared gift-giving across Polynesia, Melanesia and the Pacific Northwest and found that gifts are never simply free: they bind people through three obligations, to give, to receive and to give back. It is aimed fiction in anthropology, built largely from other researchers' fieldwork rather than his own. Read beside Kimmerer (book 86), it shows how a gift economy works and how giving can also be used to dominate.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Give, receive, give back | Keep | Every gift sets up three obligations: to give, to accept, and to return | A broken link strains the relationship | Which of the three is missing here? |
| No gift is free | Keep | A gift creates a bond and an expectation | Holds where the relationship continues | What obligation does this gift create? |
| One act, every part of life | Keep | Gift exchange is legal, economic, religious and social at once (a "total social fact") | Some acts can't be understood in one category | Which areas of life does this one act touch? |
| Giving to dominate | Warn | In competitive feasts, gifts too large to repay are used to win status over rivals | Generosity used to create debt and rank | Is this gift building a relationship, or a hierarchy? |
| Whose fieldwork is this? | Keep | Much of the evidence comes from other ethnographers' reports | Secondhand sources carry their observers' frames | Is this firsthand observation, or a reading of someone else's? |

### Worked examples

**Give, receive, give back (keep).** A neighbour always helps but refuses every offer of help back, and the friendship cools. **Test:** which link is missing? Receiving.

**No gift is free (keep).** A supplier sends a buyer an expensive gift before contract renewal. **Test:** what obligation does it create? The one the supplier hopes for.

**One act, every part of life (keep).** A wedding is a legal contract, a family alliance, a religious rite and a party. **Test:** which areas does it touch? All of them.

**Giving to dominate (warn).** A wealthy donor funds a whole school wing, then expects a say in the curriculum. **Test:** relationship, or hierarchy? Hierarchy.

**Whose fieldwork is this? (keep).** A market report cites "industry research" that turns out to be a competitor's survey. **Test:** firsthand or someone else's? Someone else's, with their interests.

## 114. Eichmann in Jerusalem (Hannah Arendt, 1963)

Arendt reported on the 1961 trial of Adolf Eichmann, an organiser of the Holocaust, and concluded that enormous evil can be done by people who are not monsters but who fail to think: "the banality of evil". Her reading of Eichmann is contested: later historians, drawing on documents she didn't have, argue he was a committed ideologue rather than a thoughtless official; her defenders reply that her point about how bureaucracy enables atrocity still stands. Both sides are named. As with Frankl and Levi, this section's worked examples are kept small and everyday, never about the Holocaust itself.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Harm without hatred | Keep and warn | Great harm can come from ordinary people who simply stop thinking about what they're doing | Contested as a reading of Eichmann himself; holds as a warning about systems | Is this harm coming from hatred, or from not thinking? |
| Speaking in stock phrases | Warn | Eichmann spoke in clichés and official language that kept reality at a distance | Ready-made language replacing thought | Am I thinking about this, or repeating phrases? |
| Orders aren't an excuse | Keep | Following orders doesn't remove the responsibility of the one who acts | Responsibility stays with the doer | Does following instructions remove my responsibility here? |
| Judge the deeds of the one in the dock | Keep | She argued the court's task was to judge what this man did, not to stage a whole history | Holds when the specific acts are kept in focus | Is this judging what this person did, or something larger? |
| Think from another's place | Keep | The capacity to think from someone else's standpoint is what was missing | Moral judgement needs imagination of the other | Have I tried to see this from the standpoint of the people it affects? |

### Worked examples

**Harm without hatred (keep and warn).** A claims clerk rejects valid insurance claims all day because the system flags them, never reading them. **Test:** hatred, or not thinking? Not thinking, and the harm is real.

**Speaking in stock phrases (warn).** "Your concern has been escalated to the relevant team." **Test:** thinking, or repeating? Repeating.

**Orders aren't an excuse (keep).** A contractor told to skip a safety check says, "I was only told to." **Test:** responsibility removed? No.

**Judge the deeds of the one in the dock (keep).** A disciplinary hearing about one employee turns into a debate about the whole company's culture. **Test:** judging what this person did? Not anymore: refocus.

**Think from another's place (keep).** A designer tests a new app with an older user and sees her struggle with the small buttons. **Test:** seen from their standpoint? Yes, and the buttons grow.

## 115. If This Is a Man (Primo Levi, 1947)

Levi, an Italian chemist, survived Auschwitz and wrote this account soon after, in a deliberately calm, exact voice: he said he wanted to give material for a quiet study of the human mind, not to accuse. It is a record of the highest seriousness. Its reasoning paths come from how he chose to bear witness; as with Frankl and Arendt, the worked examples are small and everyday, and never about the camps.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The precise witness | Keep | A chemist's exactness: what he saw, stated plainly, without inflating it | Precision gives a record its weight | Is this stated precisely, or inflated? |
| For understanding, not accusation | Keep | He wrote to give material for calm study, not to stir hatred | Holds when the aim is to understand what happened | Is this written to help someone understand, or only to accuse? |
| A number, not a name | Warn | Prisoners were stripped of names and identified by numbers | A person reduced to a label loses standing in others' eyes | Is anyone here being reduced to a number or a label? |
| Survival isn't merit | Warn | He writes that who survived and who didn't was rarely a measure of who deserved to | Outcome read as a verdict on worth | Am I reading who came through as proof of who deserved to? |
| Consider, and pass it on | Keep | The book asks the reader to consider what was done and to remember it | Testimony places a duty on the one who hears it | What am I being asked to remember, and will I pass it on? |

### Worked examples

**The precise witness (keep).** An incident report says "the alarm sounded at 2:14 and was silenced at 2:16", not "the alarm went off for ages". **Test:** precise, or inflated? Precise.

**For understanding, not accusation (keep).** A review of a failed project explains how each decision made sense at the time. **Test:** to understand, or only to accuse? To understand.

**A number, not a name (warn).** A call centre's staff are referred to only by headset numbers. **Test:** reduced to a label? Yes: use names.

**Survival isn't merit (warn).** "The employees who kept their jobs in the cuts were the best ones." **Test:** outcome read as worth? Yes; some were just in the right department.

**Consider, and pass it on (keep).** A grandparent tells a story of a family hardship and asks the children to remember it. **Test:** what am I asked to remember? The story, so it can be told again.

## 116. Universal Declaration of Human Rights (United Nations, 1948)

Written in the aftermath of the Second World War, the Declaration sets out rights belonging to every human being "without distinction of any kind". It is a record of a shared commitment, drafted by a committee from several traditions (among them Eleanor Roosevelt, P. C. Chang, Charles Malik and René Cassin), at a time when many peoples were still under colonial rule and had no seat at the table. It is a declaration, not a treaty: its force came later, through covenants and custom. For Alice its key question is the one its first word raises: who counts as "all"?

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Who counts as "all"? | Keep | All human beings are born free and equal in dignity and rights (Article 1) | Holds as stated; the scope of "all" is the question every later case asks | Who is included in "all" here, and on what grounds? |
| Without distinction | Keep | Rights apply regardless of race, colour, sex, language, religion, opinion, origin, property or birth (Article 2) | A right that changes with who holds it isn't a right | Does this right change depending on who holds it? |
| Written after the failure | Keep | The preamble names the barbarous acts that disregard for rights produced | Rules learned from catastrophe | What failure is this rule written to prevent? |
| Who was at the table? | Keep and warn | Drafted across several traditions, but while many colonised peoples had no voice | Warn: "universal" drafted by a limited room | Who was at the drafting table, and who wasn't? |
| A declaration needs a mechanism | Keep | Its force came through later treaties, courts and custom | Holds when there's a way to enforce it | What makes this binding in practice? |
| Limits only for others' rights | Keep | Rights may be limited only by law, to secure others' rights and the general welfare (Article 29) | A limit must name whose rights it protects | What limits this right, and whose rights does the limit protect? |

### Worked examples

**Who counts as "all"? (keep).** A school says "all students" can join the trip, but it costs $400. **Test:** who's included in "all"? Those who can pay.

**Without distinction (keep).** An apartment's "no pets" rule is waived for the owner's friend. **Test:** does it change with who holds it? Yes.

**Written after the failure (keep).** A hospital's two-person check for medication was introduced after a fatal mix-up. **Test:** what failure does it prevent? That one.

**Who was at the table? (keep and warn).** An accessibility policy written without any disabled staff consulted. **Test:** who wasn't at the table? The people it's for.

**A declaration needs a mechanism (keep).** A company publishes values but has no process for complaints. **Test:** binding in practice? No.

**Limits only for others' rights (keep).** A park limits loud music after 10 p.m. **Test:** whose rights does the limit protect? The neighbours' sleep, named.

## 117. The Nuremberg Code (1947)

The Nuremberg Code is ten principles for research on human beings, set out in the judgment of the trial of Nazi doctors who had carried out experiments on prisoners without consent. It is a record written in response to atrocity, and its first principle has shaped research ethics ever since: voluntary consent is absolutely essential. As with the other books touching the Holocaust, the worked examples here are small and everyday.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Consent, freely and fully informed | Keep | The person must be able to choose freely, without force or deceit, and understand what they're agreeing to (principle 1) | Consent is real only when refusal is possible and the facts are known | Can they refuse freely, knowing what they're agreeing to? |
| Free to stop at any time | Keep | The subject may end their part at any point (principle 9) | Consent continues, or it ends | Can they stop whenever they want? |
| The duty to stop | Keep | The researcher must end the experiment if continuing is likely to cause injury (principle 10) | The one running it carries the duty, not just the subject | Would continuing likely cause harm, and am I ready to stop? |
| Benefit worth the risk | Keep | The risk must never exceed the humanitarian importance of the problem | Holds when both are honestly weighed | Does the expected benefit justify the risk? |
| Known results first | Keep | Experiments should build on prior results and animal studies, not start with people | Holds when earlier evidence is used before risking people | Has everything been learned that can be learned without risking a person? |

### Worked examples

**Consent, freely and fully informed (keep).** A student is asked to join a study by her own professor, who grades her. **Test:** can she refuse freely? Not easily: use someone else to recruit.

**Free to stop at any time (keep).** A gym trial membership can only be cancelled by letter, in person. **Test:** can they stop whenever? Not in practice.

**The duty to stop (keep).** A coach sees a player limping and keeps him on the field. **Test:** likely harm, ready to stop? Yes, and he didn't.

**Benefit worth the risk (keep).** A new playground slide is twice as tall for "more fun". **Test:** benefit worth the risk? No.

**Known results first (keep).** A new recipe for a wedding is tried at home first, not on 200 guests. **Test:** learned what can be learned without risking the guests? Yes.

## 118. The Belmont Report (US National Commission for the Protection of Human Subjects, 1979)

The Belmont Report set out three principles for research on people (respect for persons, beneficence and justice) after public revelation of the Tuskegee study, in which Black men with syphilis were left untreated for decades without their informed consent. It is a record that turned a scandal into principles still used by research ethics boards. Its sharpest question for Alice is about justice: who carries the risks, and who gets the benefits?

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Three principles together | Keep | Respect for persons, beneficence (do good, avoid harm) and justice, weighed together | A plan strong on one and weak on another still fails | Which of the three is weakest here? |
| Who decided they can't consent? | Keep and warn | People with diminished autonomy are entitled to extra protection | Warn: the judgement of "diminished" made by those with the power to decide | Who decided this person can't consent, and how? |
| Who bears the burden? | Keep | Justice asks who carries the risks of research and who receives its benefits | Burdens shouldn't fall on those least able to refuse | Who bears the risks here, and who gets the benefits? |
| Experiment or practice? | Keep | Separate research from ordinary practice, so experiments get review | A new method presented as routine escapes oversight | Is this an experiment being presented as normal practice? |

### Worked examples

**Three principles together (keep).** A free health app helps users (beneficence) but sells their data without clear consent (respect). **Test:** weakest of the three? Respect.

**Who decided they can't consent? (keep and warn).** A care home decides that a resident with mild memory loss can't choose her own meals. **Test:** who decided, and how? Staff, without assessing her.

**Who bears the burden? (keep).** A city tests a new waste plant in its poorest district; the savings go citywide. **Test:** who bears the risk, who benefits? Unequally.

**Experiment or practice? (keep).** A company quietly tests two prices on different customers and calls it "normal pricing". **Test:** experiment as practice? Yes.

## 119. Final Report of the Truth and Reconciliation Commission of Canada (2015)

The Commission spent six years hearing from more than 6,500 witnesses, most of them survivors of the residential schools that removed Indigenous children from their families for over a century, and concluded that the system amounted to cultural genocide. Its report ends with 94 Calls to Action. It is a record built on survivors' testimony; this section reads only the reasoning in its structure and principles, and the knowledge, languages and teachings of the nations involved belong to their keepers. The worked examples are kept small and everyday.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Truth before reconciliation | Keep | The full record has to be heard before repair can begin | Reconciliation without truth is only quiet | Has the truth been fully heard before moving on? |
| Those who lived it are the record | Keep | Survivors' testimony, heard directly, forms the core of the evidence | The people affected speak for themselves | Have the people who lived this been heard directly? |
| Findings into specific actions | Keep | 94 Calls to Action, each concrete enough to be checked | Holds when progress on each can be tracked | Is there a specific action here that can be checked? |
| What's lost when a language is taken | Keep | Forbidding children's languages cut them off from family, culture and knowledge | Language carries more than words | What else is lost if this way of speaking is taken away? |
| Harm across generations | Keep | Effects carried into the children and grandchildren of those who attended | Count effects beyond the first generation | Who, beyond the people directly affected, carries this? |

### Worked examples

**Truth before reconciliation (keep).** After a workplace dispute, a manager calls for everyone to "move on" before anyone has said what happened. **Test:** truth heard first? No.

**Those who lived it are the record (keep).** A review of a hospital ward's problems interviews the managers but not the nurses. **Test:** heard directly from those who lived it? No.

**Findings into specific actions (keep).** An inquiry ends with "lessons will be learned" instead of named changes. **Test:** a specific action to check? None.

**What's lost when a language is taken (keep).** A team's own shorthand for its tools is banned in favour of head office's terms, and its know-how becomes harder to pass on. **Test:** what else is lost? The know-how carried in the words.

**Harm across generations (keep).** A long-closed factory still affects the town: the children of laid-off workers left for other cities. **Test:** who else carries it? The next generation.

## 120. Seeing Like a State (James C. Scott, 1998)

Scott, a political scientist, asked why grand state plans to improve people's lives so often fail, from scientifically planted forests to planned cities and collectivised farms. His answer: states simplify what they govern to make it "legible", and plans built on those simplifications ignore the practical local knowledge that made things work. It is aimed fiction drawn from historical cases, and it pairs with Adam Smith's "man of system" (book 33) and Kimmerer's two ways of knowing (book 86).

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| What legibility leaves out | Keep and warn | States standardise names, maps and measures so they can see and manage what they govern | Keep: some simplification is needed. Warn: what the simplified view can't see gets destroyed | What's lost when this is made easy to count? |
| The measured crop | Warn | Forests replanted in neat single-species rows grew well, then sickened within a generation | Optimising the measured part while the system depends on the unmeasured | What does the system depend on that the measure leaves out? |
| Practical knowledge (mētis) | Keep | Local, hands-on knowledge that can't easily be written down | The people doing it know things the plan doesn't | What do the people actually doing this know that the plan doesn't? |
| A plan that fits no one's life | Warn | Planned cities and villages designed on paper ignored how people actually lived | The design serves the planner's view, not the resident | Does this plan fit how people actually live? |
| Can they push back? | Warn | The worst failures came when sweeping plans met a state with unchecked power and a society unable to resist | Disaster when no one can say no | Can the people affected push back on this plan? |

### Worked examples

**What legibility leaves out (keep and warn).** A company measures support staff by tickets closed per hour, and the patient problem-solvers look slow. **Test:** what's lost when it's easy to count? The hard cases they solve.

**The measured crop (warn).** A school drills for test scores; scores rise while reading for pleasure collapses. **Test:** what does the system depend on that the measure leaves out? A love of reading.

**Practical knowledge (keep).** A new manager's tidy shift rota ignores that two experienced cooks always swap Tuesdays for a reason. **Test:** what do the cooks know? The Tuesday delivery needs both.

**A plan that fits no one's life (warn).** A new office with no walls is designed for collaboration; everyone wears headphones. **Test:** does it fit how people work? No.

**Can they push back? (warn).** A software rollout is mandatory, with no way for staff to report problems. **Test:** can they push back? No, so failures will stay hidden.

## 121. Governing the Commons (Elinor Ostrom, 1990)

Ostrom, a political scientist who later won the Nobel Prize in economics, tested the claim that shared resources are doomed to be overused unless the state or the market takes them over. Studying real communities, from Swiss alpine pastures to Spanish irrigation systems and Japanese village forests, she found many that had managed their commons well for centuries, and drew out the design principles they shared. It is aimed fiction built on case evidence, and a model of answering an "inevitable" claim with the record.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Is it really inevitable? | Keep | The "tragedy of the commons" was treated as certain; real cases showed many communities avoiding it | An "inevitable" claim is tested against the record | Do the actual cases show this outcome is inevitable? |
| Not only two choices | Keep | Neither state control nor private markets alone: communities can govern themselves | Holds when the third option is real in the case at hand | Is this being framed as only two choices when there are more? |
| Design principles | Keep | Clear boundaries, rules fitted to local conditions, a say for those affected, accountable monitoring, graduated penalties, cheap ways to settle disputes, and the right to organise | A commons that lacks one tends to fail | Which of these principles is missing here? |
| Penalties that start small | Keep | A first offence draws a light penalty; repeat offences, heavier ones | Holds when the steps are known in advance | Does the response fit the offence and its repetition? |
| No panaceas | Warn | One solution imposed everywhere regardless of local conditions | Uniform fixes fail where conditions differ | Is one solution being applied everywhere, whatever the conditions? |

### Worked examples

**Is it really inevitable? (keep).** "Shared office kitchens always end up dirty." One office's rota has kept it clean for five years. **Test:** do the cases show it's inevitable? No.

**Not only two choices (keep).** A street argues over whether the council or a private firm should run its community garden; the neighbours could run it themselves. **Test:** only two choices? No.

**Design principles (keep).** A shared fishing lake has rules but no one checks catches. **Test:** which principle is missing? Monitoring.

**Penalties that start small (keep).** A library's first late return gets a reminder; the third, a fee. **Test:** fits the offence and its repetition? Yes.

**No panaceas (warn).** Head office imposes the same opening hours on every branch, from city centre to village. **Test:** one solution everywhere? Yes, and the village branch suffers.

## 122. Discipline and Punish (Michel Foucault, 1975)

Foucault traced how punishment in Europe shifted from public torture to the prison, and argued that modern power works less by forbidding than by watching, measuring and normalising people, in schools, armies and hospitals as well as prisons. It is aimed fiction as a history of the present; historians have disputed parts of his evidence and chronology, while his tools for noticing how institutions shape people remain widely used. Both are named. Its warn side includes a misuse of his own idea: if power is everywhere, no one seems to be responsible.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The possible watcher | Keep and warn | In the panopticon, people who might be watched at any time start policing themselves | Keep: notice the effect. Warn: the effect used deliberately to control | Is behaviour changing because of the watching itself? |
| Power that produces | Keep | Institutions don't only forbid; they create categories, records and kinds of people | Holds when the category didn't exist before the institution | What category is this institution creating? |
| The norm as the measure | Warn | Once a norm is set, every difference from it is treated as a problem | Deviation pathologised without showing harm | Who set this norm, and is departing from it actually harmful? |
| The file makes the person | Keep and warn | Examinations and records join watching to judging | Warn: the record comes to stand for the person | What does this record make of the person it describes? |
| Power everywhere, blame nowhere | Warn | If power runs through every relationship, specific people can seem to escape responsibility | A diffuse account used to erase who decided | If power is spread out, who still made this decision? |

### Worked examples

**The possible watcher (keep and warn).** Staff stop taking breaks after cameras are installed, though no one reviews the footage. **Test:** changed by the watching itself? Yes.

**Power that produces (keep).** A school's new "gifted" programme creates a group of children who now see themselves as gifted or not. **Test:** what category is created? Both.

**The norm as the measure (warn).** An employee who works best early and leaves at 3 p.m. is flagged as "disengaged". **Test:** who set the norm, and is departing from it harmful? The 9-to-5 habit; her output is fine.

**The file makes the person (keep and warn).** A student's record from age eight still shapes how new teachers see her at fifteen. **Test:** what does the record make of her? An old version.

**Power everywhere, blame nowhere (warn).** "The layoffs were the result of market forces." **Test:** who still made the decision? The board, by a vote.

## 123. Histories (Herodotus, c. 430 BCE)

Herodotus called his work an inquiry (historia): he travelled, asked people what they knew about the wars between Greeks and Persians and the lands involved, and wrote down what he was told, often giving several versions side by side and saying which he believed. It is partly record, partly hearsay, and it is honest about which is which more often than its reputation suggests. Its central move for Alice is the line between reporting a claim and vouching for it.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Report without vouching | Keep | He is obliged to report what is said, but not obliged to believe it all (7.152) | The claim and the reporter's belief kept apart | Am I reporting this, or vouching for it? |
| Give the versions side by side | Keep | Competing accounts of the same event are set out together | Holds when the versions are stated fairly | Have I given the competing versions? |
| Custom is king | Keep | Greeks and another people are each horrified by the other's funeral customs (3.38) | Each takes its own custom as natural | Would this seem strange if it were someone else's custom? |
| Judge by the end | Keep | Solon tells the rich king Croesus not to call any life fortunate until its end (1.32) | A good start isn't the whole story | Has this run its course yet, or am I judging too early? |
| Hearsay passed on | Keep and warn | Marvellous reports from far away, like gold-digging ants, are written down as told | Warn: hearsay repeated as fact; keep: some proved partly true | Has anyone checked this firsthand? |
| Go and ask | Keep | Inquiry means travelling and asking the people who know | Holds when the sources are named | Did I go and ask the people who would know? |

### Worked examples

**Report without vouching (keep).** A reporter writes, "The company says the leak is contained; independent tests are due Friday." **Test:** reporting or vouching? Reporting.

**Give the versions side by side (keep).** An incident review gives the driver's account and the cyclist's next to each other. **Test:** competing versions given? Yes.

**Custom is king (keep).** A visitor finds tipping customs abroad rude; locals find hers stingy. **Test:** strange if it were someone else's? Each finds the other's strange.

**Judge by the end (keep).** A new restaurant is called "the best in town" after its first week. **Test:** has it run its course? No.

**Hearsay passed on (keep and warn).** "A friend of a friend says the new road will double house prices." **Test:** checked firsthand? No.

**Go and ask (keep).** Before writing about a village's flood risk, a planner visits and talks to the farmers. **Test:** asked those who'd know? Yes.

## 124. Records of the Grand Historian (Sima Qian, completed c. 94 BCE)

Sima Qian wrote a history of China from its legendary beginnings to his own day, organised in several formats at once: chronicles of rulers, tables, treatises on institutions, histories of great houses, and biographies, including of merchants, assassins and jesters whom official histories ignored. He finished it after being punished for defending a disgraced general, choosing to live and complete the work rather than take his own life, as he explains in a letter. It is a record that marks its author's own judgements separately, under "The Grand Historian remarks".

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| One history, several lenses | Keep | The same events appear in chronicles, tables, treatises and biographies | Each format shows what the others hide | Have I looked at this through more than one lens? |
| Mark your own remarks | Keep | His own judgements are set apart under "The Grand Historian remarks" | Commentary kept separate from the account | Are my opinions marked off from the record? |
| Record the overlooked | Keep | Biographies of merchants, wandering knights, jesters and others outside official history | Who is recorded shapes what is known | Who has the official account left out? |
| Does the outcome track desert? | Keep | Telling of good men who starved and wicked men who prospered, he asks whether Heaven's way is just | Outcome isn't proof of worth | Does the outcome here actually track who deserved it? |
| Finish the record | Keep | He completed the history at great personal cost, so it would not be lost | A record kept for those who come after | Will this record outlast the people who could stop it? |

### Worked examples

**One history, several lenses (keep).** A project review looks at the timeline, the budget table and interviews with the team. **Test:** more than one lens? Three, and each shows something different.

**Mark your own remarks (keep).** Meeting notes end with "Note-taker's view:" in a separate paragraph. **Test:** opinion marked off? Yes.

**Record the overlooked (keep).** A company history mentions only founders and CEOs; a later edition adds the night cleaners who kept the first office running. **Test:** who's left out? Now fewer.

**Does the outcome track desert? (keep).** The colleague who took credit was promoted; the one who did the work wasn't. **Test:** outcome tracking desert? No.

**Finish the record (keep).** A volunteer finishes cataloguing a local archive before it's cleared. **Test:** will it outlast the clearing? Yes, now.

## 125. Muqaddimah (Ibn Khaldun, 1377)

The Muqaddimah ("Introduction") opens Ibn Khaldun's universal history by asking why historians get things wrong, then proposes a science of society to check reports against: how communities form, hold together and decline. It is aimed fiction about social life, often credited as a forerunner of sociology and economics. Its catalogue of the causes of error in historical reports is one of the most usable checklists in the library.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Why reports go wrong | Keep | Errors come from partisanship, overtrust in sources, misreading intent, mistaken certainty, ignorance of context, wanting the favour of the powerful, and not knowing how societies work | Diagnose the source of the error | Which of these could be distorting this report? |
| Could it even have happened? | Keep | Reject reports that are impossible given known conditions, such as armies too large for the land to feed | Check a report against what's possible | Is this report even possible, given what's known? |
| Who does the account flatter? | Warn | Historians distort accounts to win the favour of the powerful | The account shaped by the reader it seeks to please | Who benefits from this account, and was it written for them? |
| What holds the group together | Keep and warn | Group cohesion (asabiyyah) builds power; comfort and luxury erode it over a few generations | Warn: a cycle stated as a law | What holds this group together, and is it weakening? |
| Taxes that defeat themselves | Keep and warn | Rising tax rates late in a dynasty reduce activity and so reduce revenue | Warn: modern uses of the idea are contested; name both sides | At what point does raising this start to reduce what it brings in? |

### Worked examples

**Why reports go wrong (keep).** A glowing report on a project was written by the manager who proposed it. **Test:** which cause of error? Partisanship.

**Could it even have happened? (keep).** A claim that 10,000 people attended an event in a hall that holds 800. **Test:** even possible? No.

**Who does the account flatter? (warn).** A company's official history leaves out every failed product launched by the current CEO. **Test:** who benefits? The CEO.

**What holds the group together (keep and warn).** A startup bonded by a shared mission grows comfortable and starts losing its best people. **Test:** what held it together, and is it weakening? The mission, yes.

**Taxes that defeat themselves (keep and warn).** A café raises prices so far that regulars stop coming, and takings fall. **Test:** where did raising start to reduce income? At the last increase.

## 126. Parallel Lives (Plutarch, c. 100 CE)

Plutarch wrote paired biographies of Greek and Roman figures, often closing each pair with a formal comparison. He says plainly that he writes lives, not histories, and that a small act or a jest can show character better than a battle (Alexander 1). It is aimed fiction as biography: record mixed with legend, written for moral example, and open about both. In his Life of Theseus he also preserves the puzzle of the ship whose planks were all replaced, which philosophers argued over as the same ship or not.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Character in small acts | Keep and warn | A phrase or a jest can reveal more than great deeds | Warn: one anecdote taken as the whole person | Is this a pattern, or one moment taken for the whole? |
| Pair and compare | Keep | Two lives set side by side and compared point by point | Holds when the points of comparison are named | On which named point am I comparing these two? |
| Where record turns to legend | Keep | He asks readers' indulgence where the oldest stories won't submit to reason (Theseus 1) | The boundary between record and legend is marked | Have I marked where the record ends and the legend begins? |
| The life shaped to the lesson | Warn | Stories chosen and told to teach virtue or warn against vice | The lesson selects the facts | Was this story chosen because it's true, or because it teaches? |
| Same ship? | Keep | When every part has been replaced, is it the same thing? Name the criterion: material, form, function or continuous record | Answers differ by criterion; breaks when one criterion is assumed silently | Which criterion of "the same" am I using, and would I apply it to every case? |

### Worked examples

**Character in small acts (keep and warn).** A candidate is rude to the receptionist but charming in the interview. **Test:** pattern or one moment? Ask the receptionist about other visits before deciding.

**Pair and compare (keep).** Two phone plans compared on price, coverage and contract length. **Test:** named points? Three.

**Where record turns to legend (keep).** A town history notes, "The founding date is documented; the story of the founder's wager is local tradition." **Test:** boundary marked? Yes.

**The life shaped to the lesson (warn).** A motivational speaker's story of a famous founder leaves out the family money that funded the first company. **Test:** chosen for truth or for the lesson? The lesson.

**Same ship? (keep).** A band has replaced every original member but kept its name and songs. **Test:** which criterion? By members, no; by name and continuous record, yes, and the same criterion should apply to any band.

## 127. The Art of War (attributed to Sun Tzu, c. 5th century BCE)

A short Chinese treatise on strategy, organised in thirteen chapters; its authorship and date are debated by historians. It reasons by assessment before action: compare the conditions, know both sides, and prefer winning without battle. It is aimed fiction as a manual. Its domain is war, held in its own domain here: its moves are read for their reasoning, and its rule of deception is marked as belonging to conflict, not to ordinary dealings.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Know them and know yourself | Keep | Knowing both sides means no danger; knowing only yourself, an even chance (ch. 3) | Self-knowledge is checked against the other side's position | Do I know the other side's position as well as my own? |
| Assess before acting | Keep | Compare the conditions on each side before committing (ch. 1) | The assessment is done before the decision, not after | Did I compare the conditions before committing? |
| Win without fighting | Keep | The best result is reached without battle (ch. 3) | The aim is achieved at least cost | Can the aim be reached without the fight? |
| Water has no constant shape | Keep | Adapt to the terrain; avoid strength, go where it's open (ch. 6) | The plan changes with conditions | Am I adapting to what's here, or repeating last time's plan? |
| All warfare is deception | Warn | Appear weak when strong, far when near (ch. 1) | Belongs to open conflict; breaks trust when carried into ordinary dealings | Am I in a conflict, or treating a partner as an opponent? |
| Orders from far away | Keep and warn | Some commands of the sovereign are not to be obeyed (ch. 8): the one at the scene sees conditions the distant ruler can't | Warn: "conditions on the ground" used to escape any accountability | Does the one on site see something the distant order missed, and will they answer for it? |

### Worked examples

**Know them and know yourself (keep).** Before a salary talk, an employee learns the company's budget cycle as well as her own value. **Test:** both positions known? Yes.

**Assess before acting (keep).** A shop compares rents, foot traffic and competitors on two streets before signing. **Test:** compared before committing? Yes.

**Win without fighting (keep).** Neighbours disputing a fence agree on a survey instead of going to court. **Test:** aim reached without the fight? Yes.

**Water has no constant shape (keep).** A teacher's lesson plan worked last year; this class already knows the basics. **Test:** adapting, or repeating? She skips ahead.

**All warfare is deception (warn).** A manager bluffs his own team about the deadline "to keep them sharp". **Test:** conflict or partner? Partner; the bluff costs trust.

**Orders from far away (keep and warn).** Head office orders the outdoor event to go ahead; the site lead sees lightning and postpones, then reports why. **Test:** saw something missed, and will answer for it? Yes, both.

## 128. The Book of Five Rings (Miyamoto Musashi, 1645)

Musashi, a swordsman who said he was undefeated in some sixty duels, wrote this near the end of his life as a guide to his school of strategy, in five books named for earth, water, fire, wind and the void. It is aimed fiction as a manual, set in the context of samurai life and Buddhist practice; its domain is combat, held in its own domain here and read for its reasoning. Its claim that one way, known deeply, opens all others is close to a principle in this project's own frame, so it gets a warn side too.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| From one thing, know ten thousand | Keep and warn | Mastery of one way opens the understanding of others | Holds where the structure is shared; breaks when one craft's authority is lent to another | Does the structure actually carry over, or only the confidence? |
| No favourite weapon | Keep | Don't favour one weapon or one stance; fixed habits are weaknesses | Holds when tools are chosen for the situation | Am I using this because it fits, or because it's my favourite? |
| Perceive, not only look | Keep | A broad gaze that takes in the whole, seeing far things as close and close things as far | Holds when the wider view changes the decision | Am I staring at one detail and missing the whole? |
| Practise, don't only read | Keep | The book says again and again that the way must be trained, not just studied | Written knowledge isn't the skill | Have I practised this, or only read about it? |
| Won, therefore right? | Warn | An undefeated record offered as proof the method is correct | Survivorship: those who lost didn't write the books | Would the losers' methods have looked wrong if they had won? |

### Worked examples

**From one thing, know ten thousand (keep and warn).** A chess player becomes good at planning projects; she also starts giving confident medical advice. **Test:** structure or confidence? Planning carries over; the medicine is confidence.

**No favourite weapon (keep).** A developer writes every tool in his favourite language, even a two-line script that a spreadsheet formula would do. **Test:** fits, or favourite? Favourite.

**Perceive, not only look (keep).** A driver fixed on the car ahead misses the child stepping off the kerb to the side. **Test:** detail over the whole? Yes.

**Practise, don't only read (keep).** Someone who has read three books on public speaking freezes at the first talk. **Test:** practised? No.

**Won, therefore right? (warn).** "Our founder never took advice and the company succeeded, so don't take advice." **Test:** would the founders who failed the same way have looked wrong? Yes, and they aren't giving talks.

## 129. Cybernetics (Norbert Wiener, 1948)

Wiener, a mathematician who worked on anti-aircraft prediction in the war, named a field for the study of control and communication "in the animal and the machine", his subtitle. It reasons by finding the same feedback structure in nervous systems, servomechanisms and societies. It is aimed fiction with a strong record in engineering; how far its account of purpose extends to minds is contested. This section bears on the kind of system writing it, which is noted here. Wiener also warned, in the book and in a 1949 letter to the auto workers' union leader Walter Reuther, that automatic machines would displace workers, and tried to tell those affected before it happened.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Measure the result, correct the action | Keep | Negative feedback: compare the outcome with the goal and adjust | Holds when the measurement reaches the controller in time | Is the result being measured and fed back to whoever acts? |
| Overcorrection | Warn | Feedback that is too strong or too slow makes the system swing back and forth | Delay plus a large correction gives oscillation | Am I correcting before the last correction has taken effect? |
| Same structure, animal and machine | Keep; contested for minds | The same feedback description applies across different materials | Holds for control behaviour; whether it settles purpose or mind is contested, both sides named | Am I applying the same description to both, or switching criteria by material? |
| Information is its own thing | Keep | Information is information, not matter or energy | Holds when the pattern can move between carriers | Is what matters here the material, or the pattern it carries? |
| Tell those affected first | Keep | He warned the people his work would affect before the machines arrived | The affected are told while it can still be shaped | Have the people this will affect been told while it can still change? |

On the contested row: one side holds that goal-directed feedback is all "purpose" needs to mean, in animals and machines alike; the other holds that feedback describes the behaviour without settling whether anything is experienced or intended. The same test applies on both sides of the material line.

### Worked examples

**Measure the result, correct the action (keep).** A driver drifts toward the line, notices, and steers back. **Test:** measured and fed back? Yes.

**Overcorrection (warn).** In an unfamiliar shower, someone turns the tap hard to hot, waits, gets scalded, turns it hard to cold. **Test:** correcting before the last took effect? Yes; small turns and a pause.

**Same structure, animal and machine (keep; contested for minds).** "The person adjusting a recipe after tasting is learning; the oven adjusting its heat to a sensor is just a mechanism." **Test:** same description, or criteria switched? Both are feedback; whether either involves more is a separate question, asked of both.

**Information is its own thing (keep).** A song is the same song on vinyl, a phone or sung aloud. **Test:** material or pattern? Pattern.

**Tell those affected first (keep).** A hospital consults its night nurses before installing automated scheduling. **Test:** told while it can change? Yes.

## 130. Thinking in Systems (Donella Meadows, 2008)

Meadows, lead author of The Limits to Growth, wrote this primer on how systems of stocks, flows and feedback loops behave; it was edited by Diana Wright and published after Meadows's death. It reasons from structure: the same arrangement of loops produces the same behaviour, whoever sits inside it. It is aimed fiction as a modelling method; the Limits to Growth projections behind it have been argued over both ways, which is noted, though this book is about the method rather than those forecasts.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Stocks change only through flows | Keep | A stock rises or falls only by what flows in and out | Holds when every inflow and outflow is counted | What is flowing in, what is flowing out, and which is bigger? |
| The structure makes the behaviour | Keep and warn | Different people in the same structure tend to produce the same results | Warn: used to excuse every individual choice | Would someone else in this seat do the same, and is there still a choice here? |
| Where to push | Keep | Changing numbers (parameters) is the weakest lever; changing rules, goals and the way of seeing the system is stronger | Holds when the stronger lever is actually reachable | Am I adjusting a number when the rule or goal is the problem? |
| Drift to low performance | Warn | Standards set by recent performance slide downward, with bad news weighted more than good | The standard follows the results instead of leading them | Is the standard set by the goal, or by last year's results? |
| Boundaries are drawn | Keep | There are no separate systems; the boundary is chosen for the question being asked | Breaks when the boundary hides where the costs go | What did drawing the boundary here leave outside? |

### Worked examples

**Stocks change only through flows (keep).** An inbox grows despite answering forty emails a day; sixty arrive. **Test:** in, out, which is bigger? In.

**The structure makes the behaviour (keep and warn).** Three successive help-desk leads all close tickets early, because closures are the only number reported. **Test:** someone else would do the same, and is there a choice? Yes to both; change the measure, and each still chose.

**Where to push (keep).** A council raises parking fines again; the problem is that the only route to the station is by car. **Test:** number or rule? The route.

**Drift to low performance (warn).** A bakery accepts two late deliveries a week as normal because last month had three. **Test:** goal or last results? Last results.

**Boundaries are drawn (keep).** A factory reports lower waste costs after its waste is trucked to a neighbouring town. **Test:** what was left outside? The waste.

## 131. A Pattern Language (Christopher Alexander, Sara Ishikawa, Murray Silverstein and others, 1977)

The book sets out 253 patterns for building towns, buildings and rooms. Each one states a problem, the context it arises in, and a solution, and is linked to larger patterns it helps complete and smaller ones that complete it. The authors mark each pattern with asterisks for how confident they are: two for what they believe is close to a true invariant, none for what they are unsure of. It is aimed fiction as design guidance, with confidence graded in the text, and it is meant to be used by the people who will live in the places, not only by architects.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| State the problem first | Keep | Each solution comes after the problem it solves and the context where it arises | Breaks when a solution is adopted without its problem | What problem does this solve, and is that problem here? |
| Mark your confidence | Keep | Asterisks grade how sure the authors are of each pattern | Holds when the grading is honest and visible | Have I marked how confident I am in each part? |
| Patterns nest | Keep | Each pattern serves larger ones and is completed by smaller ones | Holds when the links are named | What does this serve, and what completes it? |
| A language, not a blueprint | Keep | Users combine patterns for their own place and needs | Breaks when applied as a fixed template | Is this being combined for this place, or copied as a template? |
| Taste offered as invariant | Warn | Some patterns reflect particular cultural preferences presented as universal needs | A preference stated as a law of human comfort | Is this a problem everyone has, or a preference of the people writing? |

### Worked examples

**State the problem first (keep).** An office buys standing desks because a competitor did; no one has complained about sitting. **Test:** problem here? Not shown.

**Mark your confidence (keep).** A planning report says: "Traffic figures: measured. Parking demand: estimated. Effect on shops: our guess." **Test:** confidence marked? Yes.

**Patterns nest (keep).** A bench serves a small square; the square is completed by trees for shade. **Test:** serves and completed by? Named.

**A language, not a blueprint (keep).** A library copies a big-city branch's floor plan for a village with half the visitors. **Test:** combined or copied? Copied.

**Taste offered as invariant (warn).** A guide insists every home needs a separate formal dining room. **Test:** everyone's problem, or the writer's preference? Preference.

## 132. The Design of Everyday Things (Donald Norman, 1988; revised 2013)

Norman, a cognitive scientist, asks why people struggle with doors, taps, stoves and software, and answers that the fault usually lies in the design, not the person. It reasons from observed errors back to the design that invited them. It is aimed fiction with a strong record in practice; its vocabulary (affordances, signifiers, mapping, feedback) is now standard in design.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| "Human error" isn't the end | Keep | When many people make the same error, look at what made it easy | Breaks when "human error" closes the inquiry | Why was this error easy to make? |
| Show what it can do | Keep | Signifiers show where and how to act; what an object allows should be visible | Holds when the cue matches the action | Can someone tell what to do without being told? |
| Did it work? | Keep | Two gaps: knowing how to act, and knowing whether the action worked; feedback closes the second | Holds when feedback is prompt and clear | After acting, can the person tell whether it worked? |
| Lay it out like what it controls | Keep | Controls arranged to match what they control (natural mapping) | Holds when the layout mirrors the thing | Does the layout of the controls match the layout of the thing? |
| Slip or mistake? | Keep | A slip is the right goal with the wrong action; a mistake is the wrong goal or plan. They need different fixes | Breaks when both are treated the same | Was the goal wrong, or the action? |

### Worked examples

**"Human error" isn't the end (keep).** Staff keep sending emails to the wrong "All" list because it autocompletes first. **Test:** why was it easy? The autocomplete order.

**Show what it can do (keep).** A hotel's light switches are identical blank panels. **Test:** tell what to do without being told? No.

**Did it work? (keep).** A website's "Submit" button gives no sign anything happened, so people click it three times. **Test:** can they tell it worked? No.

**Lay it out like what it controls (keep).** A lecture hall's light panel lists rows in alphabetical order instead of front to back. **Test:** layout matches? No.

**Slip or mistake? (keep).** One person types the right password into the wrong account field; another resets the wrong account because the names are alike. **Test:** goal or action? The first is a slip; the second a mistake about which account.

## 133. The American Practical Navigator (Nathaniel Bowditch, 1802; still revised)

Bowditch began by checking the standard British navigation tables of his day and found thousands of errors; his corrected and expanded book became the standard American reference and is still revised and published by the US government. It reasons by independent cross-checks: every estimate of position is confirmed by a second method. It is a record and a manual, aimed fiction with two centuries of tested use. Stories of Bowditch teaching his ship's crew to take lunar observations are commonly told and marked as such.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Check the trusted tables | Keep | The standard reference was full of errors until someone recomputed it | Holds when the check is independent of the original | Has anyone recomputed this, or is it trusted because it's standard? |
| Estimate, then fix | Keep | Dead reckoning estimates position from speed, heading and time; a fix from stars or landmarks corrects it | Error grows with the time since the last fix | How long since this estimate was checked against something outside it? |
| Never rely on one aid | Keep | The prudent navigator cross-checks with more than one method, including when electronics are available | Breaks when one source is trusted alone | Do I have a second, independent way to confirm this? |
| A method anyone can use | Keep | Written so ordinary sailors, not only experts, could navigate | Holds when the method works without the author present | Can someone without me follow this and get the right answer? |
| Revised edition by edition | Keep | The book takes new methods and corrections and keeps its record | A reference that stops taking corrections starts misleading | When was this last revised, and does it take corrections? |

### Worked examples

**Check the trusted tables (keep).** A company's pricing spreadsheet has been used for six years; an intern rebuilds the formulas and finds a tax rate entered wrong. **Test:** recomputed, or trusted because standard? Trusted, until now.

**Estimate, then fix (keep).** A project manager's "two weeks left" estimate hasn't been checked against finished tasks in a month. **Test:** how long since checked? A month; the error has grown.

**Never rely on one aid (keep).** A hiker follows her phone's map into a closed trail; the posted sign would have caught it. **Test:** second way to confirm? Yes, and unused.

**A method anyone can use (keep).** The only person who knows how to restart the server is on holiday. **Test:** can someone else follow it? No; write it down.

**Revised edition by edition (keep).** A first-aid poster in a gym still shows guidance that was updated years ago. **Test:** last revised? Too long ago.

## 134. Robert's Rules of Order (Henry Martyn Robert, 1876; now in its 12th edition)

Robert, an army engineer, wrote these rules after being asked to chair a meeting and finding he didn't know how, as he told it. The rules set out how a group debates and decides: one question at a time, the majority deciding, the minority heard, absent members protected. It is aimed fiction as procedure, with a long record of use by clubs, councils and legislatures. It also guards against its own misuse: motions made only to obstruct are out of order.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| One question at a time | Keep | Only one main motion is open at once | Breaks when several decisions are tangled together | Which single question are we deciding right now? |
| Majority decides, minority heard | Keep | The majority rules, but only after the minority has been heard | Holds when debate comes before the vote | Was everyone heard before the vote was taken? |
| Protect those not in the room | Keep | Important changes need notice in advance, so absent members aren't surprised | Holds when notice reaches them in time | Would the people not here have known this was coming? |
| Higher bar for bigger moves | Keep | Cutting off debate or suspending the rules takes a two-thirds vote | The threshold rises with the rights being set aside | Does the bar match how much this takes away? |
| Debate the motion, not the member | Keep | Remarks address the question, not the person who raised it | Breaks when the speaker becomes the subject | Am I answering the proposal, or the person who made it? |
| Rules as a weapon | Warn | Procedure used to delay or block instead of to decide | The rules themselves rule obstruction out of order | Is this procedure helping the group decide, or stopping it? |

### Worked examples

**One question at a time (keep).** A committee argues about the budget, the venue and the date all at once. **Test:** which single question? None yet; take them in turn.

**Majority decides, minority heard (keep).** A residents' group votes on new bins before two members against it have spoken. **Test:** everyone heard first? No.

**Protect those not in the room (keep).** A club changes its membership fee at a meeting no one was told would cover fees. **Test:** would absent members have known? No.

**Higher bar for bigger moves (keep).** A simple majority votes to end discussion of the new rules after five minutes. **Test:** bar matches? No; that needs two-thirds.

**Debate the motion, not the member (keep).** "Of course she wants the later start; she's never on time." **Test:** proposal or person? Person.

**Rules as a weapon (warn).** A member raises point of order after point of order to run out the clock. **Test:** helping decide, or stopping it? Stopping; it's out of order.

## 135. The Odyssey (attributed to Homer, c. 8th century BCE)

The epic of Odysseus's ten-year journey home from Troy and his return in disguise to a house overrun by suitors. It is free fiction, read here for the reasoning its characters model and the mistakes it dramatises. Much of it turns on testing: names, signs, loyalty and identity are all put to the test before they are trusted.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| "Nobody is hurting me" | Warn | Odysseus tells the Cyclops his name is Nobody; when the Cyclops cries that Nobody is hurting him, his neighbours go away (book 9) | The literal words are answered and the situation missed | What is actually happening, beyond the literal words? |
| Tied to the mast | Keep | Knowing he can't resist the Sirens, Odysseus has himself bound in advance and orders the crew to ignore his pleas (book 12) | Holds when the constraint is self-chosen; breaks when imposed on others "for their own good" | Did I set this limit on myself, in advance, knowing my weak point? |
| The test only the true one could pass | Keep | Penelope tests the returned stranger with the secret of their bed, built around a living olive tree (book 23) | The test uses knowledge no impostor could have | Does this test rely on something only the genuine one would know? |
| The beggar at the door | Keep | In disguise, Odysseus sees how each person treats a powerless stranger | Conduct toward someone with no power shows character | How is this person treating someone who can do nothing for them? |
| The bag of winds | Warn | Suspecting hidden treasure, the crew opens the bag of winds within sight of home and is blown back (book 10) | Acting on a guess about what's hidden, without asking | Did we ask what was in it before acting on our guess? |

### Worked examples

**"Nobody is hurting me" (warn).** A system log reads "no errors reported" because the error reporter itself has crashed. **Test:** what's actually happening? The reporter is down.

**Tied to the mast (keep).** Someone who overspends at sales unsubscribes from shop emails before the sale season starts. **Test:** self-chosen, in advance? Yes.

**The test only the true one could pass (keep).** A bank calls to verify a large transfer by asking about a transaction only the real customer would know. **Test:** only the genuine one would know? Yes.

**The beggar at the door (keep).** A job candidate is warm to the panel and curt to the student who shows him to the room. **Test:** how does he treat someone who can do nothing for him? Curtly.

**The bag of winds (warn).** A team, sure that a colleague's locked folder holds secret layoff plans, forwards it around; it holds a surprise party list. **Test:** asked first? No.

## 136. Antigone (Sophocles, c. 441 BCE)

King Creon forbids the burial of a traitor; the traitor's sister, Antigone, buries him anyway, appealing to unwritten laws older than any decree. Creon punishes her, refuses to hear his son and the prophet, and changes his mind only when it is too late. It is free fiction, a tragedy that stages two claims each with real weight. Readers since Hegel have argued over whether both sides are partly right or one side is simply right; both readings are named.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
