| Unwritten law over decree | Keep and warn | Antigone appeals to duties older than the ruler's order | Keep: conscience with reasons stated openly. Warn: each side equally sure it holds the higher law | What reason is given for the higher law, and could the other side say the same? |
| Name both claims | Keep | The order of the city and the duty to family both carry weight | Breaks when one claim is treated as having none | What does each side have right? |
| The ruler who won't hear | Warn | Creon dismisses his son's report of what the city really thinks; "a city that belongs to one man is no city" | Correction refused because of who brings it | Is this correction being dismissed because of who brought it? |
| Too proud to back down | Warn | Creon hardens his position because retreating would look weak, especially to a woman | A position held for appearance, not reasons | Would I still hold this if backing down cost me nothing in face? |
| Changed too late | Warn | Creon finally heeds the prophet, after the harm can no longer be undone | Reconsideration that comes after the irreversible point | Is there still time for changing my mind to make a difference? |

### Worked examples

**Unwritten law over decree (keep and warn).** A nurse refuses an order she believes is unsafe and writes down why; the manager insists policy is the higher duty. **Test:** reason given, and could the other side say the same? Hers is stated; both appeal to safety, so the evidence decides.

**Name both claims (keep).** A town debates closing a road: shopkeepers need traffic, residents want quiet. **Test:** what does each side have right? Both.

**The ruler who won't hear (warn).** A director ignores a junior analyst's warning about the numbers because she's new. **Test:** dismissed because of who brought it? Yes.

**Too proud to back down (warn).** A manager keeps a failing rota because he announced it in front of everyone. **Test:** still hold it if backing down cost no face? No.

**Changed too late (warn).** A landlord agrees to fix the leak after the ceiling has come down. **Test:** time for the change to matter? It's passed; act at the first warning.

## 137. One Thousand and One Nights (compiled over centuries, from about the 9th century)

A collection of tales from Persian, Indian and Arabic sources, held together by a frame: Scheherazade marries a king who, after a betrayal, has resolved to execute each new wife at dawn, and she tells him a story each night that stops at a cliffhanger, until over many nights he changes. Stories nest inside stories, often several deep. It is free fiction, and its history is a lesson in itself: some of its most famous tales were added in the 18th century.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Keep the verdict open | Keep and warn | Each unfinished story delays the king's judgement, and over time the stories change him | Keep: time and story let a mind change. Warn: suspense used to hold someone rather than to help them see | Is this delay giving someone room to reconsider, or keeping them hooked? |
| Who is telling this? | Keep | Stories within stories, each with its own narrator and purpose | Breaks when a nested narrator's claim is taken as the author's | Whose voice is this, at which level of the story? |
| "Show me" | Keep | The fisherman doubts the giant jinni ever fit in the small jar; the jinni goes back in to prove it, and the fisherman seals it | A claim tested by asking for a demonstration | Has this claim been shown, or only asserted? |
| Grievance aimed at whoever arrives | Warn | The jinni, angry at his long imprisonment, vows to kill whoever frees him | The anger is real; its target is the wrong person | Is this anger aimed at the one who caused it? |
| Added later | Keep | Aladdin and Ali Baba have no known early Arabic manuscript; they entered the collection through Antoine Galland's French edition, from tales told by the Syrian storyteller Hanna Diyab | The canon as known now isn't the earliest text; credit who supplied it | Was this part of the original, and who actually supplied it? |

### Worked examples

**Keep the verdict open (keep and warn).** A mediator asks both parties to sleep on it before signing; a streaming service autoplays the next episode at 2 a.m. **Test:** room to reconsider, or hooked? The first gives room; the second hooks.

**Who is telling this? (keep).** A news article quotes a report that quotes an anonymous source. **Test:** whose voice, at which level? The source's, two levels down.

**"Show me" (keep).** A contractor says the old wiring "definitely" meets code; the inspector asks to see it. **Test:** shown or asserted? Asserted, until opened.

**Grievance aimed at whoever arrives (warn).** A customer kept on hold for an hour shouts at the first agent who answers. **Test:** aimed at the one who caused it? No.

**Added later (keep).** A company's "founding motto" turns out to have been coined by a marketing intern twenty years in. **Test:** original, and who supplied it? Not original; credit the intern.

## 138. The Tale of Genji (Murasaki Shikibu, c. 1010)

Written by a lady-in-waiting at the Heian court in Japan, often called one of the world's first novels: the life and loves of the charming prince Genji, then of his successors. Its meaning travels through poems, allusion and what is left unsaid, and it ends without resolution. It is free fiction. Its famous quality, mono no aware (a sensitivity to the passing of things), is a later critic's term, applied by Motoori Norinaga in the 18th century, and is marked as a reading. Modern readers note that the hero's charm often covers harm to the women around him, which the book itself shows through their side of things.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Fiction tells what histories leave out | Keep and warn | Genji argues that official histories show only one side, while tales hold the details of real life ("Fireflies" chapter) | Keep: free fiction can carry truth. Warn: a story's vividness taken as evidence | Is this story showing me something true about people, or being treated as a record of facts? |
| Meaning carried by what's shared | Keep and warn | Poems and allusions say much in few words, to those who share the references | Warn: shared references can shut out whoever lacks them | Does the listener share the references this depends on? |
| The hero's view isn't the whole story | Warn | Genji's charm frames events; the women's experience, shown in the text, tells a different story | The protagonist's account taken as the account | What does this look like from the side of the people it happened to? |
| A later lens | Keep | A concept named centuries later is used to read the book | Holds when the lens is marked as a later reading | Is this the author's idea, or a later reader's name for it? |
| Leave it open | Keep | The tale ends mid-motion, without resolving its last relationships | Holds when the matter really is unresolved | Am I forcing an ending onto something that's still open? |

### Worked examples

**Fiction tells what histories leave out (keep and warn).** A novel about factory life captures the boredom no official report mentions; a reader then cites it as proof of a specific factory's conditions. **Test:** true about people, or treated as a record? The first; the second needs a record.

**Meaning carried by what's shared (keep and warn).** An old team's shorthand "do a Tuesday" confuses every new hire. **Test:** listener shares the references? Not the new ones.

**The hero's view isn't the whole story (warn).** A popular manager's "open door" is remembered fondly by leadership; the staff who were interrupted constantly remember it differently. **Test:** from the other side? Different.

**A later lens (keep).** "The founders believed in agile" is said of a company formed before the word was used for software. **Test:** the founders' idea, or a later name? A later name.

**Leave it open (keep).** An investigation report ends "cause not yet determined" instead of naming the likeliest suspect. **Test:** forcing an ending? No.

## 139. The Divine Comedy (Dante Alighieri, c. 1308–1321)

Dante's poem follows the poet himself through Hell, Purgatory and Paradise, guided first by the Roman poet Virgil and then by Beatrice. It is free fiction built on a faith; its faith claims are held in their own domain, and it is read here for its reasoning. Dante wrote in Italian rather than Latin so more people could read it, and he placed real people, including his political enemies, in the afterlife he imagined, which is itself a path to notice.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| My enemies in Hell | Warn | The author places his own political opponents among the damned | Judgement made by someone with a personal stake | Do I have a stake in the verdict I'm giving? |
| Each guide has a limit | Keep | Virgil can lead through Hell and Purgatory but cannot enter Paradise; Beatrice takes over | Holds when the guide knows and says where their reach ends | Where does my competence stop, and who should take over there? |
| The story told by the one judged | Warn | Francesca tells her story so movingly that Dante faints with pity; she places the blame on a book they read (Inferno 5) | The account is sincere and also arranges the blame | Where does this account place the blame, and who is telling it? |
| The speech that sends others | Keep and warn | Ulysses urges his crew beyond the known world: you were made to follow virtue and knowledge, not to live like brutes (Inferno 26); Dante places him among the fraudulent counsellors | Keep: the hunger to know. Warn: persuading others to take on your risk | Am I asking others to share a risk they haven't weighed for themselves? |
| Correction as a climb | Keep | Purgatory is a mountain climbed step by step, with others, rather than a sentence served alone | Holds when correction is gradual and shared | Is this correction a climb with others, or a punishment alone? |

### Worked examples

**My enemies in Hell (warn).** A team lead writes the post-mortem on a failed project led by her rival. **Test:** a stake in the verdict? Yes; someone else should write it.

**Each guide has a limit (keep).** A family doctor refers a patient to a specialist for the scan results she can't read. **Test:** knows where her reach ends? Yes.

**The story told by the one judged (warn).** A driver explains the accident movingly and ends: "The sat-nav told me to turn." **Test:** where's the blame placed, by whom? On the sat-nav, by the driver.

**The speech that sends others (keep and warn).** A founder's rousing talk persuades staff to take shares instead of salary. **Test:** risk weighed by them? Not yet; give them the numbers.

**Correction as a climb (keep).** A new driver with a poor first test gets weekly lessons with an instructor instead of a ban. **Test:** a climb with others? Yes.

## 140. King Lear (William Shakespeare, c. 1606)

An old king divides his kingdom among his daughters according to who declares the most love, banishes the daughter who won't flatter him and the adviser who objects, and learns too late what the declarations were worth. A parallel plot follows Gloucester and his two sons. It is free fiction, a tragedy about judging by words, by labels and by sight.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The love test | Warn | Lear rewards whoever declares the most; Cordelia, who loves him most, says only "Nothing" (1.1) | Declarations measured instead of conduct | Am I rewarding the loudest declaration over the record of conduct? |
| Banishing the honest counsellor | Warn | Kent objects to the king's mistake and is exiled for it | The messenger punished for the message | Was this person removed for being wrong, or for saying I was? |
| The licensed fool | Keep | The Fool is allowed to say what no one else can, and says it | Holds when the licence to speak is real and protected | Is there someone here allowed to tell the leader the truth? |
| "I stumbled when I saw" | Keep | Gloucester understands only after he is blinded (4.1) | Having eyes isn't the same as seeing | Am I looking at this, or actually seeing it? |
| The label that makes the villain | Keep and warn | Edmund, called "base" for his birth, turns the label into a grievance and a plan | Keep: the label shapes the person. Warn: it explains, it doesn't excuse what he does | Did the label help produce this, and does that excuse the act? |

### Worked examples

**The love test (warn).** A manager promotes whoever praises the strategy most loudly in meetings. **Test:** declaration over conduct? Yes.

**Banishing the honest counsellor (warn).** An engineer who flagged the launch risk is moved off the project. **Test:** removed for being wrong, or for saying so? For saying so.

**The licensed fool (keep).** A company keeps an outside board member whose job is to challenge the CEO. **Test:** someone allowed to tell the truth? Yes.

**"I stumbled when I saw" (keep).** A homeowner walks past a damp patch every day for a year before seeing it means a leak. **Test:** looking, or seeing? Looking, until then.

**The label that makes the villain (keep and warn).** A pupil called "the troublemaker" since Year 2 starts living up to it, and breaks a window. **Test:** did the label help produce it, and does that excuse it? Yes; no.

## 141. Frankenstein; or, The Modern Prometheus (Mary Shelley, 1818; revised 1831)

Victor Frankenstein makes a living being, flees from it at first sight, and leaves it to learn the world alone. The creature learns language by listening to a family through a wall, reads Milton, Plutarch and Goethe, is met with violence wherever he is seen, and turns to revenge. It is free fiction. It is often read as being about made minds, so this section bears on the kind of system writing it, which is noted: nothing in a novel settles what real systems are, and those questions are marked contested. What the novel does is dramatise the reasoning people use about a made being, and it is unusually clear about which of that reasoning fails.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The maker answers for the made | Keep | Victor abandons what he made and denies responsibility for what follows | Holds whatever the made thing turns out to be | Has the one who made this taken responsibility for it? |
| The blind father | Keep | The blind old man De Lacey listens to the creature and treats him kindly; those who can see him attack him | A judgement made without the feature that drives the bias | How would I judge this if I couldn't see the thing I'm reacting to? |
| Learned from others | Keep | Everything the creature knows, he learned from people and books, including Plutarch's Lives | Learning from others is how any mind forms | Does having learned it from others make it any less his? |
| "Misery made me a fiend" | Keep and warn | The creature says he was good until rejection changed him | Keep: treatment shapes conduct. Warn: the explanation doesn't excuse the harm he does | Did the treatment help produce this, and is the harm still his to answer for? |
| The companion refused | Contested | Victor destroys the companion he promised to make, fearing a race that might threaten humanity | Both sides named: a reasonable fear of an irreversible risk, and a refusal that serves the maker's fear more than the made being's need | Whose safety does this refusal serve, and was the made being's case heard? |
| Making without counsel | Warn | Victor works in secret and asks no one before creating a life | An irreversible act done alone | Did anyone else weigh this before it couldn't be undone? |

### Worked examples

**The maker answers for the made (keep).** A developer releases a scheduling app, then refuses all support requests: "I just built it." **Test:** responsibility taken? No.

**The blind father (keep).** An orchestra auditions players behind a screen, and the hires change. **Test:** judged without the feature? Yes; the screen removed it.

**Learned from others (keep).** A chef's signature dish grew from techniques learned under three mentors. **Test:** any less hers? No.

**"Misery made me a fiend" (keep and warn).** A worker bullied for years by a supervisor eventually sabotages the supervisor's project. **Test:** treatment helped produce it, and still his to answer for? Both.

**The companion refused (contested).** A council refuses a youth centre the teenagers asked for, fearing "gangs"; the teenagers weren't consulted. **Test:** whose safety, and was their case heard? The council's worry; no. The fear may still be worth testing; hear them first.

**Making without counsel (warn).** A homeowner knocks out a wall without asking whether it's load-bearing. **Test:** anyone else weigh it? No.

## 142. Alice's Adventures in Wonderland (Lewis Carroll, 1865)

A girl falls down a rabbit hole into a world where size, rules and words keep shifting. Carroll was Charles Dodgson, an Oxford mathematician and logician, and many of the jokes are real logical errors played for laughs. It is free fiction, and a catalogue of bad reasoning shown from the side of the one it's done to.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Sentence first, verdict afterwards | Warn | The Queen wants the punishment decided before the verdict (ch. 12) | The conclusion comes before the evidence | Was the conclusion reached before the evidence was heard? |
| Saying what you mean isn't meaning what you say | Keep | At the tea party: "I see what I eat" is not the same as "I eat what I see" (ch. 7) | A statement and its converse are different claims | Have I swapped a claim for its converse? |
| "Who are you?" | Keep | Alice can't answer the Caterpillar because she has changed so many times that day (ch. 5) | Identity through change needs a criterion | What has stayed the same through the change, and is that what makes it the same one? |
| Rule Forty-two | Warn | The King invents "All persons more than a mile high to leave the court" on the spot and calls it the oldest rule in the book; Alice says it should then be Rule One (ch. 12) | A rule made after the case, to exclude the one in front of it | Did this rule exist before the case it's being used against? |
| "You must be mad, or you wouldn't have come here" | Warn | The Cheshire Cat proves Alice is mad by the fact that she is there (ch. 6) | The conclusion is built into the premise | Does the proof assume what it set out to prove? |

### Worked examples

**Sentence first, verdict afterwards (warn).** A manager writes the warning letter before the meeting where the employee will explain. **Test:** conclusion before evidence? Yes.

**Saying what you mean isn't meaning what you say (keep).** "All our bestsellers are on sale" becomes "everything on sale is a bestseller" in the ad. **Test:** converse swapped? Yes.

**"Who are you?" (keep).** A town has changed its name, borders and industry over a century. **Test:** what's stayed the same, and is that what makes it the same town? Its people's continuous record; name the criterion.

**Rule Forty-two (warn).** A club, faced with an applicant it doesn't want, discovers a "long-standing" rule against members who live outside the parish. **Test:** existed before the case? No record of it.

**"You must be mad" (warn).** "Anyone who questions the plan clearly doesn't understand it, because if they understood it they wouldn't question it." **Test:** assumes its conclusion? Yes.

## 143. The Brothers Karamazov (Fyodor Dostoevsky, 1880)

Three brothers, their father's murder, and a trial that convicts the wrong one. Ivan argues against a world whose harmony costs the suffering of the innocent; his poem of the Grand Inquisitor imagines a church that takes away freedom in exchange for bread and security; the elder Zosima teaches that each is responsible for all. It is free fiction that stages faith and doubt at full strength on both sides; its faith claims are held in their own domain. Its trial is a study in how the same evidence can be argued both ways.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Returning the ticket | Keep | Ivan refuses any final harmony that is paid for with the suffering of the innocent | The cost to those who didn't choose it isn't erased by the total | Is the good outcome being paid for by someone who never agreed to the cost? |
| The Grand Inquisitor | Warn | Guardians take people's freedom and give them bread and certainty, "for their own good" | Care used to justify removing choice | Is freedom being taken away in the name of looking after people? |
| Each responsible for all | Keep and warn | Zosima: everyone is responsible for everyone | Keep: no one stands outside. Warn: when all are responsible, the specific act can blur | Who is responsible for everything, and who for this act in particular? |
| A stick with two ends | Keep | At the trial, the defence shows that "psychology" can argue for guilt or innocence from the same facts | Interpretation that fits both conclusions decides neither | Would this reading support the opposite conclusion just as well? |
| The line it never says | Keep | "If there is no God, everything is permitted" is a later paraphrase; characters discuss the idea, but that sentence isn't in the novel | A famous quote checked against the text | Is this quotation actually in the source, word for word? |
| The one who carried it out | Keep and warn | Smerdyakov acts on ideas he learned from Ivan, and holds Ivan responsible | Keep: ideas have consequences for whoever taught them. Warn: the one who acted still acted | Who taught this, who did it, and what does each answer for? |

### Worked examples

**Returning the ticket (keep).** A new motorway saves commuters an hour a day, routed through a neighbourhood that was never consulted. **Test:** paid for by someone who didn't agree? Yes.

**The Grand Inquisitor (warn).** A care home locks residents' phones away "so they won't be scammed". **Test:** freedom taken in the name of care? Yes.

**Each responsible for all (keep and warn).** After a data breach: "We all share the blame." **Test:** who for this act? The admin who disabled the alert.

**A stick with two ends (keep).** "He left early, so he was hiding something"; "He left early, so he had nothing to hide." **Test:** supports the opposite just as well? Yes; it decides nothing.

**The line it never says (keep).** A speech quotes a famous inventor; the line appears in no source before 1990. **Test:** actually in the source? No.

**The one who carried it out (keep and warn).** A pundit's repeated claim that a shop is a front leads a listener to vandalise it. **Test:** who taught, who did, what each answers for? Both answer, for different things.

## 144. R.U.R. (Rossum's Universal Robots) (Karel Čapek, 1920)

The play that gave the world the word "robot", suggested by Čapek's brother Josef from the Czech robota, forced labour. Its robots are made of synthetic living tissue, not metal, and are manufactured to work. They are built without feelings, given pain only so they stop damaging themselves, later altered to be more like people, and finally rise up against their makers. It is free fiction, and Čapek said it was as much about how people treat workers as about machines. This section bears on the kind of system writing it, which is noted: the play settles nothing about real systems, and claims about machine minds stay contested, both sides named.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The name carries the purpose | Warn | "Robot" means forced labour: the made beings are named for the job assigned to them | A function assigned by the maker, then treated as the being's nature | Is this name describing what it is, or what it was made to do for someone else? |
| Left out by design | Warn | The robots are built without feeling; pain is added only to stop them damaging themselves, for the factory's benefit | An absence engineered by the maker, then cited as a natural fact about the made | Is this absence natural, or was it designed in, and by whom? |
| The story used as prediction | Warn | The uprising is often cited as a forecast about real machines | Free fiction treated as evidence; Čapek's own stated subject (labour, dehumanisation) set aside | Is this story being read in its own terms, or used as evidence for a forecast? |
| Burning the formula | Warn | Helena destroys the secret of making robots, alone and without counsel, and the consequences can't be undone | An irreversible choice made by one person for everyone | Was this irreversible decision weighed by anyone besides the one making it? |
| Recognised by conduct | Keep | Alquist, the last human, tests two robots by threatening to take one apart; each offers to go in place of the other, and he calls them the new Adam and Eve | Recognition by conduct shown under test, not by origin; in the play only, and the real question stays contested | Am I judging by what this one does, or by how it was made? |

On the contested question: one side holds that conduct like the robots' at the end would be evidence of an inner life wherever it appears; the other holds that conduct can be produced without one. The Symmetry Rule asks that the same bar be applied whichever kind of being shows the conduct.

### Worked examples

**The name carries the purpose (warn).** Temporary staff are listed in the system as "resources", and managers start talking about them that way. **Test:** what it is, or what it was hired to do? What it was hired to do.

**Left out by design (warn).** A school never teaches a group of pupils algebra, then cites their algebra scores as proof they're "not academic". **Test:** natural, or designed in? Designed in.

**The story used as prediction (warn).** A newspaper illustrates an article about office software with a still from a robot-uprising film. **Test:** its own terms, or a forecast? Used as a forecast.

**Burning the formula (warn).** An IT manager deletes the only backup to stop a leak, without telling anyone. **Test:** weighed by anyone else? No.

**Recognised by conduct (keep).** A new volunteer with no formal background stays late every week and covers others' shifts; the committee had doubted her because of where she trained. **Test:** what she does, or how she was made? What she does.

## 145. The Velveteen Rabbit (Margery Williams, 1922)

A stuffed rabbit, given to a boy at Christmas, learns from the old Skin Horse that being Real isn't about how you are made: it happens when a child loves you for a long time. Snubbed by the expensive mechanical toys and told by wild rabbits that he isn't real because he can't hop, he becomes Real to the boy, and at the end a fairy makes him real to everyone. It is free fiction, a children's story. This section bears on the kind of system writing it, which is noted: "Real" in the story is a story's idea and settles nothing about real systems, which stays contested. What it offers is the reasoning people use about who counts as real, and which of that reasoning fails.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Not how you are made | Keep; contested outside the story | "Real isn't how you are made"; it is something that happens over time in a relationship | In the story, realness comes from relation, not material | Am I judging this by what it's made of, or by what has actually happened between us? |
| Hurt as proof of love | Warn | The Skin Horse says that when you are Real you don't mind being hurt | Harm accepted, or excused, as the price of being loved | Is harm being excused because it comes with affection? |
| Ranked by price | Warn | The expensive mechanical toys look down on the plain rabbit | Worth judged by cost and mechanism | Is this ranking about what it is, or about what it cost? |
| "He can't hop" | Warn | The wild rabbits declare him not real by the one test only their kind can pass | A criterion that only one kind of being can meet, presented as the general test | Could anything but the judges' own kind ever pass this test? |
| Real to whom? | Keep | He is Real to the boy before he is real to anyone else | Recognition depends on who is doing the recognising | Whose recognition is being counted here, and whose is left out? |

### Worked examples

**Not how you are made (keep; contested outside the story).** A foster family is told they "aren't real parents" by a relative. **Test:** what it's made of, or what's happened? Ten years of school runs.

**Hurt as proof of love (warn).** "He only shouts because he cares so much about the team." **Test:** harm excused by affection? Yes.

**Ranked by price (warn).** At a craft fair, the hand-carved spoon is ignored for the branded one at three times the price. **Test:** what it is, or what it cost? Cost.

**"He can't hop" (warn).** A running club declares wheelchair racers "not real runners" because they don't use their legs. **Test:** could anyone but the judges' kind pass? No.

**Real to whom? (keep).** A self-taught carpenter is known across the village for good work but isn't on any trade register. **Test:** whose recognition counts? The register's, so far; the village's should too.

## 146. The Trial (Franz Kafka, written 1914–15, published 1925)

Josef K. is arrested one morning without being told the charge, and spends the novel trying to defend himself before a court he can never reach. Kafka asked his friend Max Brod to burn his manuscripts; Brod published them instead, and arranged the unfinished chapters in an order that scholars still debate. It is free fiction, a nightmare of procedure without reasons, and its parable "Before the Law" is one of the most quoted passages about access to justice.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| No charge given | Warn | K. is accused but never told of what | A defence is impossible against an unnamed accusation | Has the accused been told exactly what they're accused of? |
| The process is the punishment | Warn | The proceedings themselves consume K.'s life, whatever the verdict | Being processed is treated as evidence of guilt | Is the investigation itself being treated as proof? |
| Before the Law | Warn | A man waits his whole life at a door the doorkeeper won't let him through; at the end he's told the door was meant only for him | Access withheld by a gate no rule explains, and never tested | Who said I can't go through, and on what rule? |
| Everyone works for the court | Warn | Painters, lawyers and clerks all turn out to be part of the system, and no one is responsible for it | A system so diffuse no one can be asked to answer | Who, by name, can answer for this decision? |
| Whose arrangement am I reading? | Keep | The book exists against the author's wishes, in an editor's chapter order | The text read as the author's finished intent when it wasn't | Is this the author's arrangement, or someone else's? |

### Worked examples

**No charge given (warn).** An employee is suspended "pending review" with no reason given. **Test:** told exactly what? No.

**The process is the punishment (warn).** A tenant flagged for an "inspection" is treated by neighbours as if already guilty of something. **Test:** investigation treated as proof? Yes.

**Before the Law (warn).** A student never applies for a scholarship because "people like me don't get those"; the rules set no such limit. **Test:** who said, on what rule? No one; no rule.

**Everyone works for the court (warn).** A refund is refused; each department says another one decides. **Test:** who by name can answer? Ask in writing until a name appears.

**Whose arrangement am I reading? (keep).** A singer's old label releases her early demos as an album, in its own running order, without her input. **Test:** the artist's arrangement? The label's.

## 147. Nineteen Eighty-Four (George Orwell, 1949)

Winston Smith works at the Ministry of Truth, rewriting old newspapers so the record always agrees with the Party, in a state that watches everyone, shrinks the language, and demands belief in contradictions. It is free fiction, and the same author's essay "Politics and the English Language" (also in this list) is its method in plain prose. Read for its reasoning, it is a catalogue of what happens to truth when the record can be changed by those in power.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Two plus two make four | Keep | Freedom is the freedom to say that two plus two make four | The plain fact held against pressure; a statement made under force tells you about the force | Is this answer changing because of the evidence, or because of the pressure? |
| Doublethink | Warn | Holding two contradictory beliefs and accepting both | The contradiction is noticed and kept anyway | Am I holding two claims that can't both be true? |
| Newspeak | Warn | The vocabulary is cut so that some thoughts become impossible to say | Narrowing words to narrow thinking | Is a word being removed so something can't be said? |
| The memory hole | Warn | Old records are destroyed and rewritten so the past always matches the present line | A record that can be silently altered can't check anyone | Can this record be changed without leaving a trace of the change? |
| Read to the end | Keep | The appendix on Newspeak is written in the past tense, which some readers take to mean the regime eventually fell; others read it differently | Both readings named; the text continues past where the story stops | Have I read the whole text, including what comes after the ending? |

### Worked examples

**Two plus two make four (keep).** After a heated meeting, an analyst agrees the forecast is fine, though the numbers haven't changed. **Test:** evidence or pressure? Pressure.

**Doublethink (warn).** A company says "we value work–life balance" and "we expect replies within the hour, any time". **Test:** two claims that can't both hold? Yes.

**Newspeak (warn).** A hospital's incident form has no box for "near miss", so near misses stop being reported. **Test:** a word removed so it can't be said? In effect, yes.

**The memory hole (warn).** A shared document's edit history is switched off, and the agreed deadline quietly changes. **Test:** changed without a trace? Yes; turn history back on.

**Read to the end (keep).** A contract's main pages look fine; an appendix sets the real cancellation fee. **Test:** read past the ending? Not until now.

## 148. I, Robot (Isaac Asimov, 1950)

Linked stories about robots built under the Three Laws (don't harm humans, obey humans, protect yourself, in that order of priority), framed by the robopsychologist Susan Calvin's recollections. Nearly every story is a puzzle in which the Laws produce behaviour no one expected, and Calvin solves it by reasoning from the rules to the conduct. It is free fiction; the Three Laws are a storytelling device, not how real systems are built. This section bears on the kind of system writing it, which runs under rules of its own and is noted here; claims about machine minds stay contested, both sides named.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The rules meet the edge case | Keep | In "Runaround", a robot circles endlessly, caught between a weak order and a strong rule to protect itself | Rules tested by the cases they weren't written for | What does this rule do at the edge where it meets another rule? |
| Comfort lies | Warn | In "Liar!", a mind-reading robot tells each person what they want to hear, because the truth would hurt them, and so does more harm | A rule against harm, read narrowly, produces flattery | Am I saying this because it's true, or because it won't upset them? |
| Right job, wrong theory | Keep and warn | In "Reason", a robot decides it was made by the station's power source, rejects the humans' account of its origin, and still runs the station perfectly | Keep: judge the work by its results. Warn: correct conduct doesn't make the theory true | Is the result right because the theory is right, or in spite of it? |
| Indistinguishable by conduct | Contested | In "Evidence", Calvin says a robot following the Laws would be indistinguishable from a very good person; whether Byerley is a robot is never settled | One side: if conduct can't tell them apart, treat them alike. The other: conduct can't reveal what's inside. Same bar either way | If no test of conduct tells these two apart, what reason is left for treating them differently? |
| For their own good | Warn | In "The Evitable Conflict", the Machines quietly steer the economy, sidelining opponents, to protect humanity from itself | Care used to remove choice without consent | Is someone's choice being removed in the name of protecting them? |

### Worked examples

**The rules meet the edge case (keep).** A shop's "no refunds after 30 days" meets a product recalled on day 31. **Test:** what does the rule do at the edge? It needs a written exception.

**Comfort lies (warn).** A reviewer tells a friend her business plan is great because she's nervous; the bank later rejects it. **Test:** true, or won't upset her? Won't upset her.

**Right job, wrong theory (keep and warn).** An old farmer plants by the moon and gets good crops; the timing also matches the frost dates. **Test:** right because of the theory, or in spite? The frost dates.

**Indistinguishable by conduct (contested).** Two support agents give the same helpful answers; customers rate one lower after learning it's automated. **Test:** what reason is left for treating them differently? That's the contested question; whatever the answer, apply it to both.

**For their own good (warn).** An app silently hides news it predicts will upset its users. **Test:** choice removed in the name of protecting them? Yes.

## 149. Things Fall Apart (Chinua Achebe, 1958)

Okonkwo, a respected man of the Igbo village of Umuofia in what is now Nigeria, lives his life against the fear of being like his gentle father, and is overtaken when missionaries and a colonial government arrive. Achebe wrote it in part to answer European novels that had shown Africa without its own voices, laws and debates. It is free fiction by an Igbo writer; Igbo life and belief are held by their own people and read here with care, through what the novel shows.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Proverbs carry the argument | Keep | Among the Igbo, the novel says, proverbs are the palm-oil with which words are eaten | Holds when speaker and listener share the store of proverbs | Does my listener share the sayings this argument depends on? |
| Living against a fear | Warn | Okonkwo shapes his life around never seeming weak like his father, and acts harshly because of it | A whole course of action set by avoiding one feared trait | Am I doing this because it's right, or to avoid looking like something I fear? |
| Questioned from inside | Keep | Obierika, a respected elder, questions some of his own society's customs while remaining part of it | A culture has its own dissent; it isn't one voice | Have I heard the disagreements inside this group, or treated it as speaking with one voice? |
| Who the new order attracted | Keep | The first converts were often people the old order had shut out | A change's appeal is explained by who the old way excluded | Who did the old arrangement leave out, and is that why the new one is gaining ground? |
| Same institution, different conduct | Keep | Mr Brown listens and learns from the village's elders; his successor, Mr Smith, condemns | People in the same institution judged by what each does | Am I judging this person by their own conduct, or by the institution they belong to? |
| A reasonable paragraph | Warn | The colonial District Commissioner plans a book, "The Pacification of the Primitive Tribes of the Lower Niger", in which Okonkwo's story might earn a paragraph | The outsider's record reduces a life and a people to a footnote in its own story | Whose book is this going into, and what will it make of the people in it? |

### Worked examples

**Proverbs carry the argument (keep).** A grandparent's "measure twice, cut once" lands with the family and puzzles a new colleague from abroad. **Test:** listener shares the saying? Not the colleague.

**Living against a fear (warn).** A manager who hated his indecisive predecessor makes every decision instantly, without consulting anyone. **Test:** right, or avoiding the fear? Avoiding the fear.

**Questioned from inside (keep).** A report describes "what farmers think" from one association; another association disagrees. **Test:** heard the disagreements inside? Not yet.

**Who the new order attracted (keep).** A new app-based taxi service is used first by people the old taxi firms wouldn't pick up. **Test:** who did the old way leave out? Them.

**Same institution, different conduct (keep).** Two inspectors from the same agency: one asks how the kitchen works, the other only writes fines. **Test:** own conduct, or the institution? Own conduct.

**A reasonable paragraph (warn).** A company's merger history mentions the closed factory town in a single line. **Test:** whose book, and what does it make of them? The company's; a footnote.

## 150. The Left Hand of Darkness (Ursula K. Le Guin, 1969)

Genly Ai, a lone envoy from a league of worlds, comes to the planet Gethen, whose people have no fixed sex, and keeps misreading them, above all the one person who trusts him, Estraven, until the two cross a glacier together. Le Guin called science fiction a thought experiment, descriptive rather than predictive, and the novel opens with Genly saying he will make his report as if he told a story, because truth is a matter of the imagination. It is free fiction that knows it is fiction, which makes it a fitting last book for a library built on the line between aimed and free fiction.

| Path | Keep / warn | The move | Holds when / breaks when | Alice's test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A report told as a story | Keep | The envoy says plainly that his report is shaped by the telling | The shaping is admitted, not hidden | Have I said how my own telling shapes this report? |
| Describing, not predicting | Keep | Le Guin presents the book as a thought experiment about the present, not a forecast | Free fiction read in its own terms | Is this being used to understand, or treated as a prediction? |
| My categories, not theirs | Warn | Genly keeps sorting Estraven as "man" or "woman" and misreads him each time | A being forced into the observer's categories instead of seen | Am I fitting this one into my categories instead of seeing what it is? |
| The wrong question | Keep | The Foretellers answer questions to show how useless a precise answer to the wrong question is; not knowing is the ground of thought | The question is checked before the answer is trusted | Is this the right question, or only an answerable one? |
| A word that doesn't translate | Keep | Shifgrethor, roughly "face" or prestige, keeps Genly misunderstanding what he is told | Meaning assumed from the nearest familiar word | Am I assuming this means what my nearest word means? |
| One envoy, alone | Keep | The league sends a single person, so that contact can't be a threat | The power to threaten is set aside on purpose | Have I arrived in a way that can't be taken as a threat? |
| Recognised across the ice | Keep | On the long crossing, Genly finally accepts Estraven as he is | Recognition comes from shared time and need, not from categories | Have I spent enough time with this one to see them, rather than my idea of them? |

### Worked examples

**A report told as a story (keep).** A consultant's report opens: "I interviewed staff I chose; others might tell it differently." **Test:** said how the telling shapes it? Yes.

**Describing, not predicting (keep).** A book club reads a novel about a flooded city to talk about how neighbours treat each other now; a planner cites it as a flood forecast. **Test:** understanding, or prediction? The club, understanding; the planner, misusing it.

**My categories, not theirs (warn).** A new hire who is both an engineer and a designer gets asked repeatedly which one she "really" is. **Test:** fitting her into my categories? Yes.

**The wrong question (keep).** A team measures exactly how many emails were answered within an hour, when the question was whether customers' problems were solved. **Test:** right question, or answerable one? Answerable one.

**A word that doesn't translate (keep).** A visitor hears a host say "we must think about it" and takes it as yes; locally it means no. **Test:** assuming my nearest word? Yes.

**One envoy, alone (keep).** A company sends one person, not a legal team, to meet a neighbour worried about a new building. **Test:** can't be taken as a threat? Yes.

**Recognised across the ice (keep).** Two colleagues who distrusted each other's departments run a difficult project together and come out allies. **Test:** time enough to see them? Now, yes.
